## N: New Edinburgh–no-eyes

### New Edinburgh
 
 <i>n.</i> (Dunedin) [cf. Edinburgh of the South]

>  <b>IRVINE & ALPERS</b> Colonel Wakefield.. had in 1844commissioned Frederick Tuckett, asurveyor and engineer.. to examine theeastern coast of the Middle Island with aview to selecting a site for the "NewEdinburgh" settlement. <i>(1902,Progress of NZ in the 20th Century,167</i>)

### new egg
 
 <i>n.</i> a new boy [1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 25 NEW EGGS]

### New Enzedder
 
 <i>n.</i> a non-British immigrant

>  [Hdg] Truculent "New Enzedders." <i>(Jan 2, 1951,NZ Truth,6</i>)

>  The [Prime Minister's] visit [to a Danish immigrant girl in Adelaide] originated a new phrase, `New Enzedder', which Mr Holland said would become the equivalent of `New Australian'. Thegirl said to Mr Holland: `You can't call us new New Zealanders; it's too clumsy. Why not NewEnzedders?' `That is excellent.' said Mr Holland. `we will use it'. <i>(Jun 18, 1952,Evening Post</i>)

### new fart
 
 <i>n.</i> a new boy [1951 15 M 9 St Bede's, Chch 25 NEW FART]

### new fish
 
 <i>n.</i> a new boy [1953 16-17M A39 Thames DHS 25 NEW FISH -->1951 14-16 M 59 Wellington H.S. 25 NEW FISH]

### new fry
 
 <i>n.</i> a new boy [1951 16-18 M 26 Marlborough C. 25 NEW FRY]

### new hand
 
 <i>n.</i> new chum; not an old hand

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> A new hand, pulling one of the oars, begins to look frightened .... a volley of oaths from the headsman accompanies a threat to `break every bone in his skin if he funks now'. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/327</i>)

>  Of course, Mr. T. is a new hand here and knows nothing of the particular case. I am sure no old hand could have so written. <i>(1871,McLean Papers,37/161</i>)

### New Holland
 
 <i>n.</i> an early name for Australia

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Mr. Marsden entered with much apparent interest, upon the idea of a New Zealand settlement in Port Jackson; that is to say, within the Colony. ... [We think] that it should not be within one hundred miles of Sydney; that the natives should not be subject to visits from the curious intruders, ... [May 5 54] The natives have had much conversation, relative to the projected settlement in New Holland; ... They wish to be distinct from the whites or blacks, to retain their own teachers... <i>(Apr 9, 1827,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,51,1961 ed.</i>)

>  New Holland n. (now only historical) name given to Australia by first Dutch explorers. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),621</i>)

### new iniquity
 
 <i>n.</i> the newcomers (espec. miners) to Dunedin in the 1860s. (contrast OLD IDENTITY) [(obs. Otago) often pl. constr. in sg. usually with the.]

>  In our celebrations of the arrival of theOld Identities, we overlook the all-important fact that had it not been forthe advent of the digger, that "newiniquity".. our Dominion would not sospeedily have attained the forwardposition she new occupies. <i>(Dec 30, 1909,Otago Daily Times,8</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> [Old Identity] was the name applied by the people of Victoria, who flocked hither in thousands on the discovery of gold [in 1862], to those who had been in the province before that time. Those honest folks, the tenour of whose ways had previously been very even, were greatly startled by this immense and sudden influx of population. 16,000 persons landed in three months, andeverything went up to worse than famine prices. It is alleged that one minister actually prayed that the stream of rogues and vagabonds which was flowing into the country might be stayed, but this I believe to have been a base invention of the `new iniquity', as the new-comers were nicknamed. <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,25</i>)

>  <b>REEVES</b> The canny Scotch `old identity' was swallowed up in the `new iniquity', as they mutually styled each other. <i>(1892,Homeward Bound,3</i>)

>  `the new iniquity' was the Otago settlers' name for the unwelcome Australian immigrants who arrived in the 60's; ... <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The,18</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  <b>AYSON</b> Thomas had had a taste of `the new iniquity' before leaving for the goldfields. An old couple named Reid lived in a sod hut on the outskirts of Milton. <i>(1937,Thomas,33</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> New Iniquity. Australian immigrants: New Zealand (mostly Otago): coll.: ca. 1862-80. Opp. old identity, q.v. at identity. Morris. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,557</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> Dunedin became the largest town in the country, while, to the horror of the `Old Identity', saloons, billiard rooms, gambling dens and dance halls sprang up to attract the gold of the `New Iniquity'. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,104</i>)

>  <b>MCGILL</b> new iniquity immigrants, specifically from Australia to the Otago goldrush areas, 1860s to 1880s; the opposite of `old identity'. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,75</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (non-Otago) a newcomer to a neighbourhood (as part of an influx)

>  [Wanganui] We got new doctors ... lawyers ... Jew pedlars ... et hoc genus omne, and this infusion of `new iniquities' has fairly bedeviled the place. <i>(Jul 3, 1871,Evening Post,2</i>)

### New Leinster
 
 <i>n.</i> Stewart('s) Island (see also MIDDLE ISLAND) see also cit. NEW ZEALAND 1859 [hist.]

>  And we do hereby declare, that from henceforth the said islands shall be known and designated as the colony of New Zealand, and the principal islands, heretofore known as or commonly called the `Northern Island', the `Middle Island', and `Stewart's Island', shall henceforth be designated and known respectively as `New Ulster', `New Munster', and `New Leinster'. 1840 Letters Patent isusued on 16 November: quoted from The Ordinances of New Zealand, Domett 1850 pp.4-8 in 1971 Speeches and Documents 54 And we do hereby declare, that from henceforth the said Islands shall be known and designated as the Colony of New Zealand, and the principal Islands,here to fore known as, or commonly called, the `Northern Island,' the `Middle Island,' and `Stewart's Island,' shall henceforward be designated and known respectively as `New Ulster,' `New Munster,' and `New Leinster.' ... <i>(Nov 16, 1840,Charter for Erecting the Colony of NZ</i>) [NOTES: in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 8]

### New Munster
 
 <i>n.</i> the South Island (see also MIDDLE ISLAND) see also cit. NEW ZEALAND 1859 [sic - ? 1840 Letters Patent issued on 16 November: quoted from The Ordinances of New Zealand, Domett 1850

>  <b>RUTHERFORD</b> And we do hereby declare, that from henceforth the said Islands shall be known and designated as the Colony of New Zealand, and the principal Islands, here to fore known as, or commonly called, the `Northern Island,' the `Middle Island,' and `Stewart's Island,' shall henceforward be designated and known respectively as `New Ulster,' `New Munster,' and `New Leinster.' ... <i>(1971,Speeches & Documents,54</i>)

>  And we do hereby declare, that from henceforth the said islands shall be known and designated as the colony of New Zealand, and the principal islands, heretofore known as or commonly called the `Northern Island', the `Middle Island', and `Stewart's Island', shall henceforth be designated and known respectively as `New Ulster', `New Munster', and `New Leinster'. <i>(Nov 16, 1840,Charter for Erecting the Colony of NZ,8</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> On 28th January, 1848, [Grey] appointed and swore in some of the officers of the Southern Province, which is called `New Munster'; and on the 10th of March proclaimed as the boundary between that and `New Ulster', or the Northern Province, the parallel of latitude running ... about 39[degrees] 46' S. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,86</i>)

>  New Zealand is divided into two provinces, New Ulster, and New Munster, each of which is under a Lieutenant-Governor, who enacts ordinances for the province over which he presides, with the advice of a Legislative Council, composed of the officers of Government, together with a few persons nominated by the crown from amongst the inhabitants. A Governor-in-Chief rules the whole of New Zealand and is assisted in his legislation by a Council constituted similarly to the provincial Councils. At present, the Governor-in-Chief holds the subordinate office of Lieutenant-Governor of New Ulster , in addition to his higher Commission: and Mr Eyre is the Lieutenant of New Munster, the southern province. <i>(Feb 1, 1851,Lyttelton Times,2</i>)

### New Plymouth nuts
 
 <i>n.</i> (recipe)

>  NEW PLYMOUTH NUTS ... butter ... golden syrup. Melt ... add ... soda ... boiling water ... sugar ... flour ... cocoanut ... wheatweal. Place small daubs ... bake ... These have a flavour all their own. <i>(1938,Souvenir Book of Cookery Recipes,44</i>)

### new settler
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A large contingent of new settlersreached Wellington on Sunday evening bythe steamer Corinthic from England. <i>(Jan 11, 1927,Otago Daily Times,8</i>)

>  A further 327 new settlers, due here inthe Rangitata next week, include 237 menand women who have been granted eitherfree or assisted passages. <i>(Jul 23, 1948,Southern Cross (Wgton),00</i>)

>  <b>BARRY</b> In a comparatively short time the whole of the area was sold to the `new settlers' as they were called by those already farming north of the Mataura-Glendhu road. <i>(1966,In the Lee of the Hokonuis,161</i>)

### New Ulster
 
 <i>n.</i> the North Island (cf also MIDDLE ISLAND) see also cit. NEW ZEALAND 1859 1840 Letters Patent issued on 16 November:

>  We were here first - we called the place New Munster, New Leinster, New Ulster, but being paddies we forgot to write it down. <i>(1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>) [NOTES: Check month and day]

>  <b>RUTHERFORD</b> And we do hereby declare, that from henceforth the said Islands shall be known and designated as the Colony of New Zealand, and the principal Islands, here to fore known as, or commonly called, the `Northern Island,' the `Middle Island,' and `Stewart's Island,' shall henceforward be designated and known respectively as `New Ulster,' `New Munster,' and `New Leinster.' ... <i>(1971,Speeches & Documents,54</i>) [NOTES: quoted from The Ordinances of New Zealand, Domett 1850 pp.4-8 in 1971 Speeches and Documents 54]

Select Documents 8]

New Ulster, New Munster and New Leinster; but as no reference is made in recemt communications to these appellations, which are not very euphonious, and are certainly rather perplexing, from the similarily of their terminations, it would appear they are not calculated to please the public taste <i>(1841,New Zealand, S. Aust. and NSW,319</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> On 28th January, 1848, [Grey] appointed and swore in some of the officers of the Southern Province, which is called `New Munster'; and on the 10th of March proclaimed as the boundary between that and `New Ulster', or the Northern Province, the parallel of latitude running ... about 39[degrees] 46' S. ] <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,86</i>)

>  New Zealand is divided into two provinces, New Ulster, and New Munster, each of which is under a Lieutenant-Governor, who enacts ordinances for the province over which he presides, with the advice of a Legislative Council, composed of the officers of Government, together with a few persons nominated by the crown from amongst the inhabitants. A Governor-in-Chief rules the whole of New Zealand and is assisted in his legislation by a Council constituted similarly to the provincial Councils. At present, the Governor-in-Chief holds the subordinate office of Lieutenant-Governor of New Ulster, in addition to his higher Commission: and Mr Eyre is the Lieutenant of New Munster, the southern province. <i>(Feb 1, 1851,Lyttelton Times,2</i>)

### New Z.
 
 <i>n.</i> New Zealand [ORIGIN: abbreviation]

>  ... the cart being the most extraordinary in New Z. <i>(Jan 26, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,27,1950 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> New Zealand [ORIGIN: abbreviation]

>  <b>REVANS</b> New Z. Library <i>(1839,Letter,12</i>) [NOTES: 1839 Revans to Chapman 12]

>  ... the most extraordinary in New Z. <i>(Jan 26, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,27,ed. Richards 1950 ed.</i>)

### New Zealand
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) the New Zealand language (Maori)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> We had prayers both in New Zealand and English. The Natives were very attentive and repeated the sentences in New Zealand correctly. <i>(1823,Journals and Correspondence,252,ed. Barton ed.</i>)

>  Since I left England I have only studied New Zealand and Hebrew, and have found no kind ofdifficulty with these. <i>(Sep 1, 1843,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1843 Sep 1 letter in 1969 Macmorran Octavius Hadfield 181]



 
 <i>adj.</i> As an attributive from the Country's name, it had in very early times, a connotation NATIVE, MAORI (cf NEW ZEALANDER) often with suggestions of roughness, wildness, or barbarism. It was quickly adapted (see 1840 entry) to include the white settlers and the civilised homeland they were preparing, a sense and use which obliterated the earlier. Many uses (eg New Zealand Company (1839), New Zealand Gazette) were merely defining, and had, of themselves, no racial reference.

>  We had not yet sufficient experience of New Zealand troops to trust them too far.' <i>(Oct 6, 1769,Cook's Journals,1/570,1955 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1769 Oct 6 W.B. Monkhouse Journal in Cook's Journals (1955)]

>  `New Zealand War Canoes bidd defiance to the Ship' <i>(1955,Cook's Journals,facing 1/208</i>) [NOTES: 1769 Sydney Parkinson Caption to a drawing in B.M. Add. MS. 23920 50 in 1955 Cook's Journals facing 1/208]

>  <b>FORSTER</b> The New-Zeeland ladies never failed to put on the same kind of rouge mixed with grease, before they come on board to offer their uncouth favours to our sailors. <i>(1778,Observations Made During a Voyage,591</i>)

>  <b>ELDER</b> `11th. - In the evening Terra told us that he made a fire to cook potatoes and to eat a New Zealand man. <i>(Aug 11, 1815,Marsden's Lieutenants,107,1934 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1815 Aug 11 John King in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 107]

>  <b>McCRAE</b> While the ship was under weigh Morachie and Echoie two remarkably fine looking New Zealand boys ... came on board to take leave of their friends. <i>(1820,Journal,12,1928 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CRUISE</b> During our visit to Benny, he entered into a long argument, to prove the superiority of the New Zealand arms over the British firelock; ... <i>(1823,Journal,60,1957 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1820 Mar 20 in 1823 Cruise Journal (1957) 60]

>  <b>CRUISE</b> During our visit to Benny, he entered into a long argument, to prove the superiority of the New Zealand arms over the British firelock; ... <i>(1823,Journal,60,1957 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1820 Mar 20 in 1823 Cruise Journal (1957) 60]

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> The New Zealand ladies, though the name of fashion has never reached their ears, are quite as fond of showing off their charms ... as our own fair countrywomen; ... <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/178</i>)

>  <b>SHEPHERD</b> He saw a village built in the New Zealand Style and about 20 or thirty Natives, said they behave sivil to him. <i>(Mar 22, 1826,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1826 Mar 22 Shepherd Journal in 1940 Howard Rakiura 363]

>  <b>BEGG</b> An old man named Oree Pahbah ... saluted us, by touching noses (the New Zealand custom). <i>(1979,World of John Boultbee, The,185</i>) [NOTES: 1827 Boultbee Journal in 1979 Begg 185]

>  <b>McNAB</b> ... - The New Zealand Temple is a box made of wood, the size of which is as follows: <i>(1908,Historical Records,1/708</i>) [NOTES: 1830 Aug 4 Rev. S. Leigh in 1908 McNab Historical Records 1/708]

>  <b>FORSTER</b> [He] has likewise obligingly communicated to me another of the New Zeeland music, which will be sufficient to give an idea of the taste of the people. He ... assured me that there appeared to be some display of genius in the New Zeeland tunes ... <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,2/477</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> ?Maori christians,? a missionary settlement Mr. Marsden entered with much apparent interest,upon the idea of a New Zealand settlement in Port Jackson; that is to say, within the Colony. ... [We think] that it should not be within one hundred miles of Sydney; that the natives should not be subject to visits from the curious intruders, ... [May 5 54] The natives have had much conversation, relative to the projected settlement in New Holland; ... They wish to be distinct from the whites or blacks, to retain their own teachers. <i>Ibid.</i>314 [1833 May 17]At 3 the natives were served with their repast of beef potatoes and stirabout. As our boys have had some experience in this important duty ... our visitors were ... surprised at the order and expedition with which this assemblage of New Zealand rank was supplied, as the feast consisted of bout 800 dishes constructed of a plant similar to the flag. <i>Ibid.</i> 314. [1833 May 17] At 3 the natives were served with their repast of beef potatoes and stirabout. As our boys have had some experience in this important duty ... our visitors were ... surprised at the order and expedition with which this assemblage of New Zealand rank was supplied, as the feast consisted of bout 800 dishes constructed of a plant similar to the flag. <i>(Apr 9, 1827,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,51,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CRUISE</b> He [a native] entered into a long argument, to prove the superiority of New Zealand arms over the British firelock; and by way of exemplification, he took up a spear. <i>Ibid.</i> 69 While employed in examining this monument of New Zealand perfidy [the looted wreck of the Boyd] a canoe came up to us. <i>(Mar 20, 1823,Journal,60</i>) [NOTES: 1823 (1820 Mar 20) Cruise Journal (1957) 60]

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> [his usual word native] New Zealanders are a fine Race of People in this quarter of the Northern Island (Ea hei nomawe) in the Hokianga River, but filthy dirty, stink as bad as Caffres. <i>Ibid.</i> 40 I was certainly ashamed that Europeans could degrade themselves so before their New Zealand Boys, but so they did. ... <i>Ibid.</i> 41 [The Chief] asked me if I had a Waheinee [wahine] ? No. Would I like one? Certainly. Then take my daughter. Which I did in New Zealand fashion. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),39,1963 ed.</i>)

>  An old man named OOree Pahbah came to us as we were walking up to the village, who saluted us by touching noses (the New Zealand custom). <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,68,1986 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> As soon as a child is born, it is wrapped up, and laid to sleep in the verandah, which most of the New-Zealand houses possess; ... <i>Ibid.</i>163 [spg =Maori] I far prefer the New-Zealand, to the Friendly-Island canoes: ... a fleet of a hundred New-Zealand vessels. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),81</i>)

>  ...his wife, a New Zealand woman, 3 children and two New Zealand boys. <i>(1836,Rhodes Whaling Journal,34,1954 ed.  Straubel ed.</i>)

>  This Day one of the Newzeland Whoman got Young Boay <i>(Apr 6, 1837,Piraki Log,51</i>)

>  The native war-dance, accompanied by those terrific yells which are so well qualified to exhibit the natural ferocity of the New Zealand character, was exhibited for my amusement;.. <i>(1841,GBPP,(No.311) 11</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Feb 17 Hobson Report in GBPP House of Commons 1841 (No.311) 11]

>  <b>F.MATHEW</b> The arts of civilization have been introduced, and in a New Zealand Pah you will see land cultivated in a manner which would not disgrace a farmer in many parts of England. <i>(Feb 16, 1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Feb 16 F.Mathew Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 54]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Thus whilst Xt. is the strength and support of the learned and enlightened believer of England he does not disdain the heart of a poor New Zealand child [Poari], <i>(Mar 19, 1847,Journal,V/5</i>)

>  <b>EARP</b> ... according to New Zealand notions of rank he being the highest in the scale, and his tormentor being regarded as a cookee, or slave, the lowest. <i>(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles),50</i>)

>  There was not the slightest degree of shilly shallying - the tomahawk and the mere* going to it with true New Zealand zest; ... <i>(1864,Von Tempsky Memoranda,167</i>)

>  <b>BUDDLE</b> Manorial rights are imaginary rights when claimed for New Zealand Chiefs. <i>(1860,Maori King Movement,19</i>)

>  <b>LADY MARTIN</b> Nor was he like some New Zealand and English boys, smart without being clean. His shirt and boots were spotless. <i>(1884,Our Maoris,134</i>)

>  From a close examination ... of their poetry, it is apparent, that the New Zealand poet had taken some pains towards rhythm ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/47</i>) [NOTES: Colenso Maori races]

>  <b>CRAIK</b> And there was the New Zealand woman, a trained nurse who stood at the ready for those who had need of her ... <i>(1830,New Zealanders, The,99</i>)

>  <b>CRAIK</b> The circumstances ... are all illustrative of the New Zealand character. <i>Ibid..</i> 115 In some cases, the wounds which the New Zealand women inflict on themselves are intended to express their grief for friends who have perished in the war; ... <i>(1830,New Zealanders, The,99</i>)

 <i>adj.</i> of the country as a geographical entity

 <i>adj.</i> with connotation European, English-speaking

>  FOREST BIVOUAC. ... Strengthened by the usual New Zealand food of roasted potatoes and `damper', we set off ... but soon found ourselves bewildered ... in the surveyor's lines which crossed one another in all directions in the wood. We then resorted to the usual expedient of torches made of `karehao' (supple Jack) or `katoa'; ... it was [agreed] ... that we should light a fire and bivouac for the night. <i>(1851,New Zealand (various authors/titles),Part V (Church in the Colonies XX) 76</i>) [NOTES: 1848 Aug 30 Selwyn in 1851 NZ Part V (Church in the Colonies XX) 76]



 
 <i>adj.</i>

New Zealand, Brit. dominion in the south Pacific ocean] 1 : of or from New Zealand : of the kind or style prevalent in New Zealand 2 : of, relating to, or being the biogeographic region or subregion of the Australian region that includes New Zealand and a few adjacent islands <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1524</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> The energising force in New Zealand life up to now has been this myth of the pioneer, the frontier ... <i>(1976,Pacific Star,19</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the country (See [cf MAORILAND](m-mar.md#maoriland),[ENZED](ens-eye.md#enzed))

>  The name <i>New Zealand</i> is sometimes wrongly attributed to Tasman himself. <i>(Aug 2000,NZWords,4 : 1</i>)

>  A gentleman expressed a wish to go and live three years at Otaheite or New Zealand <i>(Apr 29, 1776,Boswell's Life of Johnson,751,1953 ed.</i>)

>  The inhabitants of Otaheite and New-Zealand are not in a state pf pure nature; for it is plain they broke off from some other people. <i>(1776,Boswell's Life of Johnson,751</i>)

>  The inhabitants of Otaheite and New-Zealand are not in a state pf pure nature; for it is plain they broke off from some other people. <i>(1776,Boswell's Life of Johnson,751</i>)

>  [Secret Instructions from the Admiralty] ... or fall in with the Eastern side of the Land discover'd by Tasman and now called New Zeland. <i>(Jul 30, 1768,Cook's Journals,1/cc/xxxii,1955 ed.</i>)

>  On the sixth of october [sic] 1769 the Endeavour Bark first made the land of New Zeeland:... <i>(Oct 8, 1769,Cook's Journals,I-564,1955 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1769 Oct 8 W.B. Monkhouse in 1955 Cook Journal]

>  Part of the East Coast [slip for West] of this Country was first discover'd [check] by Abel Tasman in 1642 and by him call'd New Zeland. <i>(Mar 31, 1770,Cook's Journals,1/274,1955 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> Our Ewes intended as presents to the Inhabitants of New Zeeland or New Holland, began to be ill ... <i>(Dec 9, 1772,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/191</i>)

>  Intending to put into Dusky Bay or any other Port I could find on the Southern part of Newzealand', ... [fn1] AH. Tavai- poenammoo. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/131 As there is no Port in New Zealand I have been in ... <i>(Mar 26, 1773,Cook's Journals,2/109,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> We talked of having some men of inquiry left for three years at each of the islands of Otaheite, New Zealand, and Nova Caledonia, so as to learn the language ... . He [Capt. Jas. Cook] gave me a distinct account of a New Zealander eating human flesh in his presence and in that of many more aboard, so that the fact of cannibals is now certainly known. <i>(Apr 18, 1776,Ominous Years, The,341,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> [New Zeeland] As we stood northward, we also observed more seals ... which came from the coast of New Zeeland. <i>Ibid.,</i> 1/129-130 [New-Zeeland] ... the resemblance it bore to the fir tribe ... soon convinced us that it was fitter to serve the purposes of [130] the American spruce-tree, and that a palatable and wholesome liquor might be brewed from it, as a kind of substitute for spruce-beer. ... The spruce of New-Zeeland is a very beautiful tree, and conspicuous on account of its pendant branches ... <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/119</i>)

>  <b>RUTHERFORD</b> And we do hereby declare, that from henceforth the said Islands shall be known and designated as the Colony of New Zealand, and the principal Islands, here to fore known as, or commonly called, the `Northern Island,' the `Middle Island,' and `Stewart's Island,' shall henceforward be designated and known respectively as `New Ulster,' `New Munster,' and `New Leinster.' ... <i>(1971,Speeches & Documents,54</i>) [NOTES: 1777 G.Forster Voyage Round the World 1840 Letters Patent issued on 16 November: quoted from The Ordinances of New Zealand, Domett 1850 pp.4-8 in 1971 Speeches and Documents 54]

>  <b>YATE</b> Puriri (Vitex littoralis) - This tree, from its hardness and durability, has been denominated the New-Zealand Oak; ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),44</i>)

>  The consequence [of ignoring the South Island] is, that in works which profess to describe New Zealand, the largest island of the three is not described at all. ... But it seems never to have occurred to the gentlemen who have published their `New Zealands', that it was necessary to see more than a very small portion of it, in order to describe the whole. <i>(Jul 20, 1844,Nelson Examiner,20 et seq.</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 231]

>  New Zealand is divided into two provinces, New Ulster, and New Munster, each of which is under a Lieutenant-Governor, who enacts ordinances for the province over which he presides, with the advice of a Legislative Council, composed of the officers of Government, together with a few persons nominated by the crown from amongst the inhabitants. A Governor-in-Chief rules the whole of New Zealand and is assisted in his legislation by a Council constituted similarly to the provincial Councils. At present, the Governor-in-Chief holds the subordinate office of Lieutenant-Governor of New Ulster , in addition to his higher Commission: and Mr Eyre is the Lieutenant of New Munster, the southern province. <i>(Feb 1, 1851,Lyttelton Times,2</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Of the three islands forming the Zealand group, Rakiura, or the small South Island, is the only one which possesses a native name. Since the days of Cook, the North Island has been named on old maps Eaheinomawe, and the Middle Island Tavai-poenammo. These names originated thus. When the great navigator asked the natives the name of the North Island, he was told that it was `a thing fished from the sea by Maui,' - He mea hi no Maui; and that the Middle Island was Te wahi pounamu, or `the place of the greenstone.' When New Zealand became a British colony, the first governor, who was an Irishman, proclaimed that the North, Middle, and South Island were thenceforth to be denominated respectively New Ulster, New Munster, and New Leinster; and these names he selected for the colony because New Zealand, like Irelands, had no toads. This modern method of naming places by applying the epithet `New' to countries which are as old as the originals is not only incorrect, but possesses many disadvantages. The Constitution Act of 1852 discarded this unsuitable nomenclature, and the North, Middle, and Stewart's Islands are the names now invariably given to them by the settlers, and those by which they are familiar toseamen. The South Island was named in honour of the sealer who, in 1808, discovered its insularity. Sentimental settlers designate New Zealand the Britain of the southern hemisphere, on the same principle that the Dutchman Tasman, who discovered the country, called it New Zealand. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/4</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ... I have accordingly commenced my history of our avifauna with an account of the New-Zealand Crow. (humorously) New Zillund, etc [discuss this pronunciation] <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/xvi</i>) [NOTES: usu. spg: also North-Island]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> New Zealand, ... This name was given to the colony by Abel Jansz Tasman, the Dutch navigator, who visited it in 1642. He first called it Staaten-land. It is now frequently called Maoriland(q.v.). - <i>(1898,Austral English,320</i>)

>  fn Since the pig was introduced by Captain Cook ... [fn] Hence modern Newzealand slang a Captain Cook or Captain Cooker, for a wild pig. (Current since the late 19th century.) Also, slang, Pig-islands, Newzealand, Pig-islander, a Newzealander. <i>Ibid.</i> 1946 55/141 ... Words that appeared to me to have become current in English, at any rate as written and spoken in Newzealand. Neither ... do we Newzealanders in our ordinary writing; <i>(Jun 1945,Journal of the Polynesian Society,54/225</i>)

>  It is no surprise that a conservative organisation such as the RSA has taken exception to the use of the phonetically pleasant `Aotearoa' instead of the clumsy `New Zealand' or colloquial `Newzyullind'. <i>(Jul 1, 1988,Dominion,10</i>) [NOTES: (Davis Jones)]

>  `The name Aotearoa was made popular around 1898 with the publication of William Pember Reeves' book The Long White Cloud - Aotearoa,' historian ... Sir Keith Sinclair says. After 1870, most Maori referred to the country as Niu Tirani - a result of Pakeha settlers calling the three islands New Zealand. Sir Keith said that in all the written Maori he had read from last century the word Aotearoa had been used in only one newspaper. Te Wanaanga, published in Napier, used Aotearoa in the 1870s. Sir Keith said Maori often referred to New Zealand by using `te motu nei' (in this island). Sir Keith said the literal translation of Aotearoa was `land of the long twilight'. The name may have come about because the early Polynesians were not used to the long evenings in New Zealand. <i>(Jul 17, 1988,Sunday Star Times,A6</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> `That's what they reckon. Nothing like it anywhere else in N'yerzillun.' <i>(1980,Photo-Finish,31</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> She is left with several ... children, who are dressed in the Maori costume, and know no language but that of New Zealand. <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/293</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> As a separable qualifying prefix to, or in loose combination with, the common names of fauna and flora to denote the indigenous variety distinct from English or European varieties to which it may bear some real or imaginary resemblance; or to denote their origin or habit at in the New Zealand bio-geographic region. In colloquial N.Z. speech often omitted, and is interchangeable with NATIVE and to some extent with MAORI. In this glossary it is considered aseparable prefix when used with most names of specific plants etc. This use is obsolescent in mostcases. Current uses are underlined: -->PLANTS: Acacia, Anise, Aniseed, Arbor Vitae, Ash, -->BEECH, BIDDY-BID, BIRCH, blue-bell, Bramble, BROOM, BURR, CANE, CARROT,CEDAR Celery, Clematis, Coffee, COTTON, COTTONWOOD, CURRANT, Cypress, Daisy, EASTER ORCHID, Edelweiss, EYEBRIGHT, FLAX, FUCHSIA, GRAPEFRUIT, GRASS (seeGRASS ENTRY), HAREBELL, HEMP, Hawthorn, Holly, Honeysuckle ICE PLANT, JASMINE, LABURNUM, LAUREL, LILAC, LINSEED, MINT, MISTLETOE, MULBERRY, MYRTLE, OAK, ORANGE BLOSSOM, Palm, PASSION FLOWER, PINCUSHION, PINE,PINEAPPLE, PLUME GRASS, PRIMROSE, SCREW PINE, SPINACH, STRAWBERRY,TEAK, THORN, VINE, WIND GRASS, YELLOW PINE, NEW ZEALAND -->in general combinations see also BUSHMEN, -->as a distinguishing epithet in the names of plants see also ACACIA, [?talicise current usage: ? or obs usage] ACACIA, ACAENA, ANISE, ANISEED, ARBOR-VITAE, ASH, ATROPIS, BEDSTRAW, BEECH, BEGONIA; BIDDY-BID, BIRCH, BLUEBELL, BOX, BRAMBLE, BROOM, BURR, BURR-REED, CALCEOLARIA, CALTHA, CANE, CARROT, CEDAR, CELERY, CHICKWEED, CHRISTMAS TREE, CLEMATIS, COFFEE, CONVOLVULUS, COTON, COTTONWOOD, CRESS, CURRANT, CYPRESS, DAISY, DAMSON,DANDELION, DAPHNE, DOCK, DONATIA, EDELWEISS, EYEBRIGHT, FERN (various, see entry), FIG, FIR, FLAX, FLAX-PLANT, FUCHSIA, GRAPEFRUIT, GRASS (many, see GRASS entry), GUM-TREE, HAREBELL HAWTHORN, HEMP, HERPOLIRION, HIBISCUS, HICKORY, HOLLY, HONEY-SUCKLE, HYDROCOTYLE, ICEPLANT, JASMINE; BUTTERCUP, KAIKOURA; LABURNUM, LAUREL, LIGNUM VITAE, LILAC, LILY, LINSEED, MARSH MARIGOLD, MINT, MISTLETOE, MULBERRY, MUSK, MYRTLE, OAK, OAK ELM, OLIVE, ORACHE, ORANGE BLOSSOM, ORCHID (various), PACHYCLADON, PALM, PASSION-FLOWER; PENNYWORT, MARSH; PINCUSHION; PINE (various, see entry), PINEAPPLE, PLANTAIN, PRIMROSE, PRIVET, REED, SANDALWOOD, SCREW-PINE; SPINACH, SPURGE, STRAWBERRY,TEAK, THORN, VINE, VIOLET, WATER-PIMPERNEL, WILLOW; WINEBERRY, YEW -->BIRDS: CROW, DOTTEREL, PIPIT, ROBIN, THRUSH, TURKEY, -->animals: BAT, DOG, FROG, FUR SEAL, RAT -->fish: COD, COWRY (shell), GURNARD, HAKE, LATCHET, MINNOW, SHARK (various), SMELT, SOLE, WHITEBAIT

### New Zealand Alliance
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The New Zealand Alliance Convention opened to-day, Mr Wesley Spragg (Auckland), president, in the chair. The report presented stated that the outbreak of war injuriously affected campaign work and the vote for prohibition and no-license. Approval was expressed of the refusal of the Government to allow wet canteens at Expeditionary Force camps. <i>(Apr 14, 1915,Ashburton Guardian,2</i>)

>  The liquor traffic, in the "Evening Post" of October 11, and in other papers, challenges the New Zealand Alliance to produce "any evidence that the American people have ever voted or had an opportunity of voting upon the question of National Prohibition." <i>(Oct 21, 1922,NZ Truth,10</i>)

>  Government acted thus, because after making a count they found that the publicans had more noses in the House than the teetotallers; and the object of the New Zealand Alliance for the Reform of the liquor laws was to let Mr Sheehan and those of his sort see that the teetotallers had as many noses in the House as the publicans. <i>(Mar 25, 1879,Bruce Herald,5</i>)

>  At the annual public meeting, held under the auspices of the New Zealand Alliance for the supression of the sale of liquor, resolutions were adopted pledging an offer of support in the endeavour to have the principle of prohibition represented in the next Parliament, protesting against compensation to publicans whose houses were closed by popular veto, and resolving to take steps for educating the people upon the temperance question ... <i>(Dec 3, 1890,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Like Father Hays and the British Alliance, our grand ideal is: local option for all, total abstinence for the individual, and no-license , or prohibition for the State. This has been the paramount aim of the New Zealand Alliance since its inception. <i>(May 11, 1905,West Coast Times,3</i>)

>  ".. In the long run, though not now, the New Zealand Alliance may prove too strong for the publican party." <i>(Jul 19, 1871,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  He moved, "That this representative meeting of the New Zealand Alliance draws the attention of the Government to the request made by the Maoris at the congress held at Wellington in 1908, when the principle of local option should be extended to the Native race, and earnestly hopes that the numerous petitions presented by the Maoris to the Prime Minister will be favorably considered." <i>(Jun 28, 1910,Poverty Bay Herald,6</i>)

>  Bitter complaint was made.. as to the conduct of the New Zealand Alliance in regard to the Tauranga election. <i>(Oct 8, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,19</i>)

>  Some startling allegations as to the drinking habits of Maoris in Taranaki are contained in a petition presented to the House by Mr Atkinson from the Executive of the New Zealand Alliance. The petitioners forwarded with the petition a sworn declaration by the Rev. R. Haddon, of Hawera, Wesleyan Maori missionary, who is a half-caste, in reference to the disastrous effects of drink upon the Maoris of Taranaki. <i>(Oct 4, 1901,Poverty Bay Herald,4</i>)

>  Among the Members of Parliament pledged to the New Zealand Alliance are a number who represent "Continuance" constituencies. This, in itself, is sufficient indication of the undemocratic policy of the Alliance, in attempting to force its opinions over the heads of the people. <i>(Oct 11, 1928,NZ Truth,10</i>)

### New Zealand Cross
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There were Crimean decorations there and New Zealand Crosses, as well as the more plentiful Maori War medal. <i>(Aug 10, 1912,NZ Free Lance,4</i>)

>  Now, therefore. His Excellency the Governor.. doth by this present Order institute a Decorative Distinction to be conferred on... Members of the Militia, Volunteers and Armed Constabulary, who may particularly distinguish themselves by their bravery in action, or devotion to their duty while on service... The decoration shall consist of a Silver Cross, with the name of the Colony and the name of the recipient engraved thereon. <i>(1869,NZ Gazette,II.Mar.</i>)

>  Major Ropata.. wore at his side the handsome sword presented to him by the Queen, and on his breast the New Zealand Cross. <i>(May 28, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,22</i>)

>  The public intimation of the Government intention to decorate Mr. ** with the New Zealand Cross has caused a very great amount of astonishment, indignation, and disgust, not only among the present holders of the New Zealand Cross, but also among Volunteers who rightly consider it their bounden duty to protect the dignity of this much-prized decoration. <i>(1898,AJHR,H-31Q/1</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Mair was the only pakeha ... in the action that won him a captaincy and the New Zealand Cross. <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,102</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> NEW ZEALAND CROSS, 1869. [heading] A silver maltese cross with a six pointed star in gold upon each limb. ... Institution, Order of the Governor in Council, dated 10 March, 1869. New Zealand Gazette 1869, p. 127. [229] This cross was instituted by the Colonial Government and awarded to members of the local Militia, Volunteers, and Constabulary for acts of bravery and distinction. <i>(1941,Numismatic History of NZ,228</i>)

>  A rare New Zealand Cross, awarded for bravery during the New Zealand land wars, is the centre-piece of a major auction of coins and medals in Melbourne later this month. The cross, described as a non-military version of the Victoria Cross, is one of only 23 awarded to the militia, volunteers and armed constabulary during the wars, ... <i>(Jul 3, 1984,Dominion,6</i>)

>  His Excellency the Governor.. doth bythis present Order institute a DecorativeDistinction, to be conferred on membersof the Militia, Volunteers or ArmedConstabulary. who may particularlydistinguish themselves by their braveryin action, or devotion to their dutywhile on service... The decoration shallconsist of a Silver Cross, with the nameof the Colony and the name of therecipient engraved thereon. <i>(1869,NZ Gazette,127</i>)

>  The Speaker announced that he hadreceived from His Excellency the Governora message, transmitting the New ZealandCross of Honor (sic) Bill. <i>(1989,NZ Parliamentary Debates,VI/662</i>)

### New Zealand crow
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) kokako (See [kokako](kiw-kwi.md#kokako))

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>Kokako</i>; called, by some, the New-Zealand crow. ... It has a strong black beak ... and a small brilliant light-blue flap hanging down on each side, from the ear. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),64</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> <i>29. Callaeas cinerea. Lath.</i> ... Kokako of the natives. - New Zealand crow. <i>Yate. Dieffenbach</i>. Kakako of the natives. <i>Polack. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/19</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ II/191]

>  <b>DRURY</b> BIRDS MET WITH IN PELORUS ...New Zealand Crow - Kokako of the natives. <i>(1855,Sailing Directions,65</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> The first is the kakako (Callaeas cinerea), the New Zealand crow; generally a pair together; ... <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,139</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> It [the Otira Pass area] is also the haunt of the New Zealand crow, a shapely bird, purple, shot with black, and reddish yellow rings under the eyes, very tame, ... <i>(Jul 20, 1868,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,130,1914 ed.</i>)

>  Callaeas cinerea. Kokako. New Zealand Crow. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/160</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Kookako</i>, n. <i>collaeas</i>[sic] <i>cinerea</i>, or <i>New Zealand crow. </i> <i>(1871,Dictionary of the NZ Language,57</i>)

>  In this forest ... are found ...the rarer <i>Glaucopis cinerea</i> or Kokako, commonly called the New Zealand Crow. <i>(1872,TrNZI,4/206</i>) [NOTES: (Travers NZ Birds)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> ... I have accordingly commenced my history of our avifauna with an account of the New-Zealand Crow. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,I/xvi</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Although the New Zealand Crow, the New Zealand Thrush were still to find [in Nelson province], Bush Canary at any rate were plentiful. <i>(1936,NZ Naturalist,57</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Kokako,</i> n. <i>Callaeas cinerea cinerea</i> and <i>C. Cinerea wilsoni</i>, New Zealand crow. <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,129</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> ... higher up still there was the ding dong of the New Zealand crow, a bird which I have never been lucky enough to see, and have heard a few times only on Te Aroha; ... <i>(1973,Once Is Enough,14</i>)

>  Sir James Hector, in drawing attention.. to a very fine specimen of the New Zealand crow or jay. as it should more properly be called, made interesting references to the complete isolation of the orange wattle crow, in the South Island, and the blue wattle crow in the North Island. <i>(Dec 21, 1897,Otago Daily Times,10</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> Higher up still there was the ding dong of the New Zealand crow, a bird which I have never been lucky enough to see, and have heard a few times only on Te Aroha. <i>(1986,Countless Signs,165</i>) [NOTES: From Frank Sargeson (1973) : Once is Enough]

### New Zealand Day
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) a short-lived replacement name for Waitangi Day

>  <b>HAYWARD</b> Mr K. announced at the Waitangi Day celebrations that henceforth it would be called New Zealand Day and become a public holiday,* `though I think that this will still often be called Waitangi Day rather than New Zealand Day. ...' [fn]* On 6 February 1976 the day again became officially known as Waitangi Day. <i>(Feb 13, 1973,Diary of the Kirk Years,112,1981 ed.</i>)

### New Zealand death
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) death by drowning, especially when crossing rivers on horseback [1800s]. [aka national death]

>  <b>PASCOE</b> Had he drowned, it would have been said that he had died 'the New Zealand death.' <i>(1959,Great Days in NZ Exploration,67</i>)

>  <b>PICKERING</b> Freshes were lethal for many early colonists and drowning became so commonplace that it was called the New Zealand death or New Zealand disease. <i>(1988,Hills, The,23</i>)

>  <b>IRELAND</b> The colonel's name does not appear on the list of drownings - the "New Zealand death' as it was called. <i>(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,24</i>)

>  Drowning was once termed "the New Zealand death" because in the young colony it became the biggest killer of settlers chancing swollen rivers like the Rakaia. <i>(Jul 2000,NZ Geographic,No. 47/86</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> In the early days .. so many persons were drowned that drowning was often described as 'The New Zealand Death.' <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,24</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> 'The New Zealand death' he knew was the old name for drowning in a river. <i>(1984,Tales of the Coast,16</i>)

>  <b>MCGILL</b> New Zealand death, the: drowning. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,71</i>)

>  <b>Relph</b> Isolation was a real problem in the absence of roads, and increased by the danger of crossing the larger rivers. Indeed, drowning was known as 'the New Zealand death'. <i>(2007,From Tussocks to Tourists,77</i>)

>  Drowning has ben termed "the New Zealand death," and of the numbers who annually in New Zealand are the victims of drowning accidents, the Wanganui River and Casltecliff beach claim a fair share. <i>(Jan 30, 1912,Wanganui Chronicle,4</i>)

>  A river-ford death already figures in the holiday casualties. The fords of rivers generally claim a strong, efficient type of victim, because other types usually do not take a risk at an obviously tricky ford; but it was different in the roadless, bridgeless days, when all backblockers had to choose between the ford or remaining in the wilderness, and it was then that drowning in a river became known as "the New Zealand death." <i>(Dec 27, 1935,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  Fatalities to persons crossing rivers in this colony have been so frequent that they have not inaptly been classed under the heading of "The New Zealand Death." <i>(Aug 15, 1903,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

 <i>n.</i> the anti-nuclear policy (from an American viewpoint)

 <i>n.</i> = Maori dog

### New Zealand English
 
 <i>n.</i> the distinctive accent, vocabulary, etc. of New Zealanders

His presidential address, recently delivered at the conference in Wellington of the Secondary Schools' Assistant Teachers, is ... the more interesting. The subject was `New Zealand English'. <i>(Aug 10, 1910,Triad, The,37</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Language in Australia and NZ 152 Mullock is less frequent and retains a specialist sense of `rubbish from a mine'. It is not common in general New Zealand English, but mullock rocks, `waste material from quartz mines', occurs in a West Coast context. <i>(1966,English as it is spoken in NZ</i>)

>  There are entries [in The Macquarie Dictionary] ... for Australian English, Australianism and Strine, but none for New Zealand English, New Zealandism or New Zild. 2.That variety of English spoken and written by native-born New Zealanders; the totality of English spoken and written; the English language as it is found in the speech and writing of native-born New Zealanders. by native-born New Zealanders. <i>(Jan 21, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>) [NOTES: Deverson]

### New Zealand ermine
 
 <i>n.</i> fur from a stoat [A weasel has a black-tipped tail and brown fur which turns white in winter. In England it is called a stoat while it has its brown coat.]

In consequence a new article of the fur trade called the New Zealand Ermine has been produced, and such coats are valued at 180 pounds each. <i>(1950,Introduced Mammals of NZ,74</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [WW1]

### New Zealand falcon
 
 <i>n.</i> an indigenous bird of prey, of which there are three forms: the bush falcon, the eastern falcon, and the southern falcon.

>  <i>Falco novaeseelandiae</i>, New Zealand<i> Falcon</i> [check] <i>(1948,NZ Forest Inhabiting Birds,16</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> NEW ZEALAND FALCON Falco novaeseelandiae <i>Other names</i>: Bush Hawk, Sparrow Hawk, Quail Hawk, Karearea. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,99</i>)

>  120. Falco novaeseelandiae Gmelin, 1788 <i>New Zealand Falcon (Karearea) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,39,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The spot on Sugarloaf where weird old Jimmy Gilfillan had his hut and spent all his time shooting every sparrowhawk (New Zealand falcon) he could find, declaring: `They fly down and pick your eyes out.' <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,122</i>)

>  <i>New Zealand Falcon Falco novaeseelandiae</i> GMELIN, 1788 ...[155] OTHER NAMES: <i>Karearea, sparrowhawk, bush hawk, quail hawk, eastern falcon, southern falcon, bush falcon. ... Bush falcon: In the North Island confined to the main ranges, ... Eastern falcon: The main ranges of the South Island ... Southern Falcon: Fiordland, the Auckland Islands, and Stewart Island. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,154</i>)

>  The Raptor Association, through contact with pigeon clubs, has been told by several racing pigeon enthusiasts that they have shot native New Zealand falcons attacking their pigeons. <i>(Aug 21, 1999,Evening Post,4</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. Maori fashion](m-mar.md#maori-fashion),[native fashion](n-new.md#native-fashion))

Each of them had to pay for the tattooing. <i>(1934,Marsden's Lieutenants,136</i>) [NOTES: 1817 Mar 25 Kendall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 136]

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> House cold, wet and Muddy, determined to built a Chimney New Zealand fashion. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),57,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McDONNELL</b> Cordage and fishing-lines, made from good New Zealand flax, has been proved to be far more durable than any made from European hemp. <i>(1834,Extracts From a Journal,19,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> <i>Phormium tenax</i>, or New Zealand flax. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,145</i>)

>  These plains are all covered with New Zealand flax, fern, or long grass, with here and there a large cabbage tree, and are all surrounded with hills, from whence issue a great many little streams, some of whose courses being stopped spread over the adjoining ground and form what are called swamps. <i>(Apr 16, 1849,Letters from Otago,30,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MACKIE</b> The N. Zealand flax - <i>Phormium tenax</i> -was growing abundantly by the road side - excellent rope is made from it ... <i>(Feb 2, 1853,Traveller under Concern,74,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The <i>phormium tenax</i>, or New Zealand flax, is the most useful plant that country affords. ... Of the leaves of the <i> phormium</i> ... the natives make all their common garments ... <i>(1866,Polynesia,46</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Flax, New Zealand.<i>Phormium tenax</i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),764</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> <i>[P. tenax] </i>is the long-familiar `New Zealand Flax' plant or `Flax Lily' [fn `Treasury of Botany,' 1866]; terms which must now be held to include <i>P. Colensoi ... </i> <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,79</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> The whole [of the temporary Maori shelter] was then covered by layers of <i>toi-toi</i>, a tall fibrous rush-plant resembling pampas grass, tied in position with the ubiquitous <i>harakiki</i> (New Zealand flax) <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,89,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <i>native flax n 2</i> : NEW ZEALAND FLAX <i>Ibid.</i> 1524<i> new zealand flax or new zealand hemp n, usu cap</i> N & Z 1 : a tall New Zealand herb (<i>Phormium tenax</i>) having erect, sword-shaped leaves and scarlet or yellow flowers 2 : the strong fiber from the leaves of New Zealand flax used chiefly for cordage, twine, and mattings <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1506</i>)

>  <i>flax, New Zealand n</i>. plant in New Zealand yielding a strong fibre, growing to height of nine feet. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),616</i>)

>  <i>flax, New Zealand ... n.</i> plant in N.Z. yielding strong fibre, growing up to nine feet. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),8</i>)

>  <i>New Zealand flax [s] Phormium tenax</i> Forst. ... 52 <i>New Zealand flax [=] rauhuia [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,52</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>PHORMIUM TENAX, New Zealand flax, harakeke. </i> <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,180</i>)

>  N.Z. Flax. Phormium tenax, Forst. <i>(1868,TrNZI,1/III/50</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <i>Phormium tenax</i> N.Z. flax <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/126</i>)

>  <b>WEBSTER</b> NEW ZEALAND FLAX, n. A plant, the phormium tenax, having broad, stiff leaves, ofextraordinary strength, whose fibres make excellent cordage. - Loudon. <i>(1854,Dictionary of the English Language,672,(5th ed) ed.</i>)

>  <b>COLENSO</b> In that country where the New Zealand flax grows everywhere wild. <i>(1888,Fifty Years In Maoriland,5</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Rauhuia, New Zealand flax, or <i>Linum monogynum</i> was the country's first indigenous export, if you don't count dried heads. <i>(1980,Ghost Towns of NZ,221</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) <i>Phormium tenax</i>, the native flax [farmed extensively in 1800s and 1900s.]

### New Zealand grapefruit
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [goldfruit](go-gra.md#goldfruit))

>  When we were living [in South Africa] in the 1960s, we heard of a citrus orchard in Rustenberg that grew hybrod oranges named Rex Union oranges, famous for making excellent marmalade. We went to this pretty little country town and found an orchard named 'Dunedin' and bought a bag of the oranges... What surprised us was that the Rex Union oranges seemed to be identical to our New Zealand grapefruit. Many years later.. I was reading about Morrison's Orchard in Warkworth and was struck by the statement that the original grapefruit tree had come from Kawau Island and that Sir George Grey couldn't remember where he had got the plant. Grey had been Governor of Cape Province, South Africa, from 1854-1861 and was known to have brought many South African plants to New Zealand. Are 'New Zealand grapefruit', 'Rex Union oranges'? <i>(Oct 26, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>) [NOTES: Letter from Jill Williamson]

>  One Aucklander.. was unable to obtain a New Zealand grapefruit at any of the hotels at which he stayed. <i>(Feb 9, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,78</i>)

>  The New Zealand grapefruit is probably the most successfully grown orange in New Zealand. <i>(Apr 27, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,79</i>)

### New Zealand Institute
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  An Act to establish an Institute for the Advancement of Science and Art in New Zealand... The short Title of this Act shall be "The New Zealand Institute Act, 1867"... This fish and the others mentioned are known to fishermen, auctioneers, retailers and the public generally as Flatfish. ### New Zealand Medal
 
 <i>n.</i> [The New Zealand Medal (its correct name, not Maori War Medal) was awarded to those who had actually been under fire in any engagement from 1845 through 1847 or from 1860 through 1872. It was approved for award to British troops in 1869 and six months later extended to colonial forces.]

>  <b>TINNE</b> most of them distinguished themselves ... during the war, which are but ill rewarded by the universal New Zealand medal, and a most scanty pay. <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,32</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> THE NEW ZEALAND (MAORI WAR) MEDAL. [heading] ... The Gazette issued at Auckland on 23 October, 1869, p. 590, notifies the extension to the Colonial Forces of the grant for the decoration conferred `for the War in New Zealand to all persons whether belonging to the local forces or loyal native tribes, who were actually under fire in any engagement with the enemy, or were otherwise conspicuous for distinguished service in the field.' <i>(1941,Numismatic History of NZ,228</i>)

### New Zealand Pierrots
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Suitably clothed, and altogether in theright frame of mind, the New ZealandPierrots now proceeded along the road tosuccess and their fame spread. <i>(Mar 27, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,IV/40 : 81</i>)

>  Another clever coterie of merrimentmerchants are the "N.Z. Pierrots," orBase Concert Party. <i>(Jul 5, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,IV/47 : 248</i>)

### New Zealand pigeon
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) the NZ wood-pigeon, also called a dove, kereru any of a family of fast-flying birds with a compact body, small head and short legs. [wood pigeon, kereru see also native, New Zealand, wild pigeon <i>Hemiphaga novaeseelandiae</i>, bush pigeonwood-pigeon; kuku, kukupa; kereru; (white-)breaster] (See [wood pigeon](../W#wood-pigeon),[kereru](kee-kiw.md#kereru))

>  <b>FORSTER</b> In the morning ... I went ... to the Indian Cove, & shot there two new Kingfishers [[ed. fn] Now the new Zealand Kingfisher ...], some new yellow headed Flycatchers [[ed. fn] Now the Yellowhead <i>Mohoua ochrocephala</i> (Gmelin, 1789).], a fine large green and brown Pigeon [[ed. fn] Now the New Zealand pigeon ...] ..., a small Perrokeet, green with a red forehead ... [ed. fn] Now the Red-crowned Parakeet ... <i>(Apr 2, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/245,1982 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1773 May 11 see also HAWK]

>  <b>FORSTER</b> A very fine species of crayfish (<i>cancer homarus</i> Lin.) longer than the lobster, some shell-fish, and ... a cormorant, duck, pigeon, or parrot gave us an agreeable variety at our table ... <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/144</i>)

>  <b>SAV AGE</b> ...the only [bird] species I saw that struck me as new, was a pigeon, of beautiful plumage, large size, and delicious eating. <i>(1807,New Zealand (various authors/titles),10</i>)

>  <b>CRUISE</b> We saw a few small birds of very rich plumage, and shot some wild pigeons. <i>(Mar 14, 1820,Journal,56,1957 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1823 (1820 March 14) Cruise Journal (1957) 56]

>  <b>BISCOE</b> [At Chathams] to my great surprise saw a large Black Cat, I likewise shot some very large white & blue pigeon. <i>(Dec 2, 1831,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1831 Dec 2 J.Biscoe Journal in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 420]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> All were very kind; they had prepared a feast of Pigeons, <i>Pipis</i> and <i>Kumara.</i> <i>(May 21, 1833,Early History of the Dovedale Valley,315,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> The Pigeon or Wood Quest (Kukupa) is a very beautiful bird. <i>(1840,Journal of Ensign Best,222,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> The pigeon is larger than the English, and far handsomer. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,9/138</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> In the lower regions the forest was much enlivened by birds. I shot some parrots [fn Nestor meridionalis. Kaka of the natives.], pigeons [fn Columba spadicea, Lath. Kukupa.], and wattle-birds [fn Glaucopis cinerea, Gmel. Kokako.]. Among the branches of the trees hopped the neat fantail flycatcher [fn Muscicapa flabellifera, Gml. Piwakawaka.], and a social bird of a yellow colour, which has been depicted by Forster [fn Orthornyx heteroclytus, Lef. Popokatea.], and which had much the habits of the finch. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/29</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Several kinds of birds are indigenous to the woods and waters; among them the oyster-catcher, bittern, kingfisher, cormorant, quail, wild-duck, mocking or [16] parson bird, parrots, parroquets, woodhen, pigeon, etc. (the [sic] gigantic Moa bird is believed to be now quite extinct). <i>(1867,Twelve Years in Canterbury,15</i>)

>  I noticed the following:- Pigeon, tui, korimako, kaka, ruru, piwakawaka, toutouwai, kingfisher, duck and teal in the lakes, and pukeko in the swamp, and various sea birds. <i>(1884,TrNZI,17/419</i>) [NOTES: Gold-Smith) [pl]]

>  54. <i>Carpophaga novae-zealandiae</i>. Pigeon, Kuku. <i>(Jul 1885,NZ Journal of Science,483</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Kuku,</i> n. <i>pigeon; </i> <i>(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,78</i>)

>  <i>pigeon</i> ... (also <i>New Zealand pigeon</i>) green and bronze NZ bird. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,568</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 857]]

>  The stillness is unbroken save by the soft note of the pigeon. <i>(Oct 11, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

>  A wild pigeon had just been feeding on berries that seemed to have been steeped in turpentine. <i>(Dec 17, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> Pigeons live on berries of different Trees, ...<i>Ibid.</i> 56 When in the Woods she looked out for the (Coo Coupers) [<i>kukupa</i>] or Pigeons, and had a sharp eye. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),34,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McDONNELL</b> The <i>Pigeon</i> and <i>Zooe</i> are perhaps the handsomest - the former is really a magnificent bird - almost as large as a barn-door fowl, and equally invites the attention of the Ornothologist and the epicurean - the latter has two feathery [35] wattles pendent from its ear, and some few of its notes are not unlike those of the wattle bird of Van Diemen's Land. <i>(1834,Extracts From a Journal,34</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) kereru [ORIGIN: Maori] (See [kereru](kee-kiw.md#kereru))

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> ... the berries of which form the favourite food of the beautiful New Zealand pigeon, the plumage of which displays all the colours of the rainbow. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/27</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> ... one of the beautiful New Zealand pigeons (<i>carpophaga Novae Selandicae</i>), ... would flutter away into the darker recesses of the forest. <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/30</i>)

>  <b>ELDER</b> The New Zealand pigeons are as small as half-grown fowls. We had them dressed and had a plentiful dinner and something to spare. <i>(Aug 3, 1819,Marsden's Lieutenants</i>) [NOTES: 1819 Aug 3 William Hall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 223]

>  <b>McDONNELL</b> The New Zealand pigeon is as splendidly beautiful in plumage as it is exquisitely delicious to the taste. <i>(1834,Extracts From a Journal,16,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> CARPOPHAGA NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. (NEW-ZEALAND PIGEON.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Kuku, Kukupa, and Kereru. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,157,1888 2nd edn 1/229 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DURRELL</b> [NZ Pigeon]. <i>(1966,Two in the Bush,45</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> NEW ZEALAND PIGEON <i>Hemiphaga novaeseelandiae Maori names</i>: Kereru, kuku,kukupa <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,168</i>)

>  <i>211.a. Hemiphaga novaeseelandiae novaeseelandiae</i> (Gmelin, 1789) <i>New Zealand Pigeon (Kereru) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,57,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>New Zealand Pigeon Hemiphaga novaeseelandiae</i> GMELIN, 1789) ... [239] <i>Chatham Island Pigeon H.n. chathamensis</i> (ROTHSCHILD, 1891) OTHER NAMES: <i>Kereru, kuku, kukupa. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,236</i>)

>  An interesting feature is the number of New Zealand birds - the kukupa, or New Zealand pigeon, the tui, kaka, and others. <i>(Jan 11, 1897,Evening Post,5</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> the native huia

>  Huia, a New Zealand pigeon, whose feathers are worn by chiefs only. <i>(Oct 26, 1893,Otago Witness,39</i>)

### New Zealand pine
 
 <i>n.</i> an old word for kauri(-pine).

>  From the great increase of population at Melbourne, and the consequent demand for house room, very large supplies of timber are required; and the great superiority of New Zealand pine over the hard woods of Australia, from the facility with which it is worked, is so great a consideration where carpenter's wages are 20s a day, as to cause it to be in universal request. <i>(May 4, 1853,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,2</i>)

>  .. New Zealand pine, assorted, 9s 3d to 9s 9d. <i>(May 3, 1870,Wellington Independent,3</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> ... the Sawn Plank is very cheap in the Colonies and lately there is some prejudice against the New Zealand Pine. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),48</i>) [NOTES: see also p. 33]

>  <b>McDONNELL</b> Her spars, one and all, were of New [19] Zealand pine (cawdie); they were faultless. <i>(1834,Extracts From a Journal,18,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MATHEW</b> ... here I first saw the famed Koudi tree which is now so extensively used for masts and spars under the name of New Zealand pine. It is a magnificent tree certainly, but not at all like our ideas of Pine, as it has a fine branching head quite different to any of the fir species. The Coudie or Courie [<i>kauri</i>], as it is called, the New Zealand Pine, growing 80 or 90 feet without a branch .... <i>(Apr 25, 1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Apr 25 Sarah Mathew Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 126]

>  In the new duties on timber New Zealand pine is exempt, and 1s 6d per hundred lineal feet is imposed on boardings. <i>(Aug 1, 1892,Hawke's Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  It is estimated that the tariff will affect butter manufacturers using New Zealand pine to the extent of aGBP10,000 per annum. <i>(Aug 15, 1907,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  Her spars, one and all, were of New Zealand Pine (cowdie) [sic]; they were faultless. <i>(Jul 18, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  We are glad to hear that 25,000 feet of New Zealand Pine will be shipped from here in the <i>Mary Anne</i> to Sydney. <i>(Jul 5, 1845,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,2</i>)

### New Zealand pipit
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> ANTHUS NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. (NEW -ZEALAND PIPIT.) ... Native names. Pihoihoi and Whioi; `Ground-Lark' of the colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,132,1888 2nd edn 1/63 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> <i>The Ground Lark</i> There are several species ... that may be called homestead birds, such as the New Zealand Pipit, or Ground Lark, the Waxeye, the Warbler and the Fantail. <i>(1927,Birds of New Zealand,133</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> NEW ZEALAND PIPIT (<i>Anthus novaeseelandiae</i>), also called the ground lark, is a common bird throughout the country. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,86</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> NEW ZEALAND PIPIT <i>Anthus novaeseelandiae Other names</i>: Pihoihoi, Sandlark (Chathams). ... The Skylark, with which it is commonly confused, lives in pasture land where the Pipit is generally absent, but the ranges of the two species overlap in rough open country, etc. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,216</i>)

>  <i>246.a. Anthus novaeseelandiae novaeseelandiae</i> (Gmelin, 1789) <i>New Zealand Pipit (Pihoihoi) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,65,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Pipit Anthus novaeseelandiae</i> (GMELIN, 1789) ...<i> New Zealand Pipit A.n. novaeseelandiae</i> (GMELIN, 1789). <i>Auckland Islands Pipit A.n. aucklandicus</i> GRAY, 1862. <i>Antipodes Pipit A.n. steindachneri</i> REISCHEK, 1889. ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Pihoihoi, groundlark, native lark. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,270</i>)

>  <b>Moon</b> The New Zealand pipit is widely distributed throughout the country. It inhabits rough pastures, riverbeds, sandy beaches and forest margins. <i>(2002,Birds of New Zealand,111</i>)

 <i>n.</i> Hector's dolphin

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> HECTOR'S DOLPHIN ... Confined to New Zealand . . . <i>Other names: Cephalorhynchus hectori</i>; native dolphin, New Zealand porpoise. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,305</i>)

### New Zealand Post
 
 <i>n.</i> the Post Office (1988-)

### New Zealand primrose
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Ourisia macrophylla</i>, New Zealand primrose

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> The beautiful white flowers, and large cordate leaves, bear some slight resemblance to those of the Chinese Primrose, and it is possibly on this account that <i>O[uris]ia macrophylla</i> is called by colonists , [misnamed locally,] the New Zealand Primrose. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,385,1940 p. 411 ed.</i>)

### New Zealand quail
 
 <i>n.</i> koreke (extinct)

>  The rapid disappearance of the New Zealand quail from this island is a subject of great regret to our sportsmen. <i>(Jul 7, 1866,Daily Southern Cross,5</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> ... they [dogs] are perhaps the cause that the New Zealand quail (Coturnix Novae Zelandiae, Quoy et Gaim.) is so scarce in the northern island, - and also the guana. <i>(1843,Travel in NZ,i/417</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> COTURNIX NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. (NEW-ZEALAND QUAIL.) ... <i>Native names</i>.- Koreke {and Kokoreke}. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,1/161, 1/225</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Koitareke n. New Zealand Quail; coturnix Novae Zelandiae. </i> <i>(1871,Dictionary of the NZ Language,57</i>) [NOTES: 3rd ed.]

>  <b>WHITE</b> The New Zealand Quail, which was very plentiful, is nearly extinct now: Bush-fires have burnt it, and dogs, cats, and rats have eaten it, but the Australian and Californian varieties have filled its place. <i>(1905,My NZ Garden,69</i>)

>  Related very closely to the still surviving Australian stubble quail, the New Zealand quail was once common in parts of the North and South Islands. Being a species living mainly in grasslands, it was more abundant in the South Island on its eastern side than in the North where its distribution was far more localised. By the mid-nineteenth century the koreke, as it was known to the Maori, was already uncommon in the North Island, though at about the same time it was still abundant in parts of Nelson and Otago. By 1870 it was regarded as having been completely exterminated in the North, and five years later the South Island populations had vanished too. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/7</i>)

>  124 <i>Coturnix novaezelandiae novaezelandiae</i> Quoy and Gaimard, 1830 New Zealand Quail ... extinct since about 1870.(Koreke) <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,40</i>) [NOTES: 1980 ed.]

>  The New Zealand quail (Coturnix novae-zealandiae) is often cited as showing how rapidly a species will become extinct. <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/207</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### New Zealand rat
 
 <i>n.</i> kiore

>  Sent him a specimen of N.Z. rat by Pirikawau. <i>(Dec 15, 1850,Torlesse Papers,181,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> ... the almost extinct New Zealand rat is like the rats found in the Navigators' Islands and Rarotonga. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/59</i>)

>  The difference was that the lemmings drowned themselves to end their journey, while the New Zealand rat apparently moved from one district to die in the next. <i>(Apr 10, 1934,Feilding Star,7</i>)

### New Zealand Rhine
 
 <i>n.</i> the Whanganui River

>  And quite recently the Under-Secretary for Lands ... in reply to criticism of the policy of... witholding ... certain lands on the Wanganui watershed, very wisely pointed out that, unless the forest in that locality were ... protected, the famous `New Zealand Rhine', not only as a beauty spot, but as a navigable river, would soon be a thing of the past. <i>(1911,TrNZI,43/446</i>)

>  <b>HOWE</b> The Wanganui River is known as `the Rhine of New Zealand' <i>(1913,Travel Letters,84</i>)

### New Zealand robin
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment)

>  The New Zealand robin (curiously the South Island and not the North Island variety) hopped round the traveller freely. <i>(Oct 20, 1906,Star, The,4</i>)

>  Across the path ... comes the bush, full of birds singing all day, tuis and bell-birds and New Zealand robins ... <i>(Sep 8, 1851,Letters from Canterbury, NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1851 Sep 8 C. Godley ?Letters from Canterbury in 1947 Reed Story of Canterbury 100]

>  <b>DRURY</b> BIRDS MET WITH IN PELORUS ... New Zealand Robin - common in the bush <i>(1855,Sailing Directions,66</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> Amongst the small inhabitants ... is the Totoara, the New Zealand robin, which is the first to welcome the explorer in the wilderness, and which remains steadily near the camp. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,140</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> Many of [the native birds] have English names, which I found very tantalising, especially when the New Zealand Robin was announced, and I could only see a fat little ball of a bird, with a [94] yellowish-white breast. <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,93</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Toutou (<i>Miro longipes</i>) is a small grey and white bird, which some people have said is called the New Zealand robin. It is to be seen in the bush now and then, and seems tame, but <i>we</i> prefer to call another species <i>our</i> robin. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/224</i>)

>  PETROICA. - New Zealand Robin. ... The song is very melodious, especially that of <i>Petroica longipes</i> (North Island Wood Robin). <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/102</i>) [NOTES: (Reischek)]

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> NEW ZEALAND ROBINS (<i>Petroica australis</i>, ... ) are divided into various subspecies: the North Island <i>longipes</i> is a bit smaller and duller, and rather more mottled, than the South Island <i>australis</i>, the Stewart Island one is<i> rakiura</i>. The Chatham Island robin is <i>P.traversi. </i> <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,279</i>)

### New Zealand Romney
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a New Zealand-bred sheep, developed from the Romney Marsh breed.

>  <b>MEADOWS</b> In 1956, in recognition of the changes it had undergone from its original ancestors, the name of the breed was changed to New Zealand Romney. <i>(1997,Sheep Breeds of New Zealand,66</i>)

>  The breed [Romney] changed to suit its new surroundings to become, over the years, a distinct breed - the New Zealand Romney. <i>(2001,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: www.sheepworld.co.nz/SheepFarming.htm]

### New Zealand snail
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) ['predication; in cit.? (predation?)]

>  Life is tough for the New Zealand snail. <i>Powelliphanta marchanti</i> is one of many large endemic carnivorous snails whose numbers are in decline due to habitat loss and predication from introduced pests. <i>(Jul 2005,Conservation Action,23</i>)

### New Zealand spinach
 
 <i>n.</i> a spinach-like edible native plant, Tetragonia tetragonoides (formerly expansa) or T. trigyna

>  For example, so-called New Zealand spinach (<i>Tetragonia tetragonoides</i>), whose seeds are still sold by that name as a native vegetable, is a strand plant found naturally from Japan south across the Pacific to Australia and New Zealand. <i>(Mar 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 50/24</i>)

>  New Zealand spinach has more bulk than other varieties. <i>(Sep 27, 1999,Dominion,9</i>)

>  GARDEN SEEDS. PEAS, broad beans, dwarf French beans ... and New Zealand spinach. <i>(Jul 27, 1859,Lyttelton Times,8</i>)

>  New Zealand spinach, which is supposed to be hardier than ordinary spinach and is used as a substitute for it, is in every way inferior. <i>(Oct 13, 1928,Evening Post,20</i>)

>  The subject of soil management for intensive cropping with vegetables, fruit, herbaceous borders, and lawns, was then dealt with more fully, and vegetables specially suitable for a sandy coastal soil were mentioned, with a special reference to the New Zealand spinach. <i>(Jul 17, 1935,Evening Post,20</i>)

>  New Zealand spinach does well now if the seeds are soaked first in hot water. <i>(Dec 29, 1941,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  ..the sea celery.. for example, tastes similar to our own, while Australia's warrigal greens are one and the same as our New Zealand spinach. <i>(Oct 25, 2003,Dominion Post,E6</i>)

>  What we call kokihi or rengamutu you probably know better as New Zealand spinach or Warrigal cabbage (<i>Tetragonia tetragonoides</i>). <i>(Oct 2000,Growing Today,37</i>)

>  New Zealand spinach may be sown now, or in some localities plants may be obtained from the sea beach. <i>(Sep 1, 1870,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  For the first time in the Colony we have seen New Zealand spinach, a very fine parcel of which was exhibited by Mr. Hendry. <i>(Jan 30, 1863,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  New Zealand spinach, Tetragonia trigyna, a succulent plant native to the Chatham Islands. was boiled and eaten by whalers for a month to rid them of scurvy. <i>(Aug 1991,NZ Gardener,29</i>)

>  Among the species being grown in the new reserves are <i>Muehlenbackia astonii</i> (shrubby tororaro), <i>Euphorbia glauca</i> (shore spurge, thought to have been extinct in the Wellington region for 20 years, then rediscovered in 1996/96 on a rocky stack off Kapiti Island), <i>Discaria tomentosa</i> (a species of anchor plant, one of Wellington's - and the North Island's - rarest plants), <i>Hebe speciosa</i> (showy hebe) and <i>Tetragonia tetragonioides</i> (New Zealand spinach). <i>(Jan 2004,NZ Geographic,No. 67/11</i>)

>  <b>CROWE</b> (New Zealand spinach is found on coastal sands, dunes and stony beaches; it does not have a red, fleshy fruit, but a dry seed capsule)... While beach spinach is native only to this country, New Zealand spinach is , in spite of its name, native also to Tasmania, Australia, the Pacific Islands, Japan and southern South America, having been used as a vegetable in south Brazil, Mauritius, Tongatapu and Kanaky (New Caledonia). It is eaten also as a vegetable side dish in Indonesia (especially on Java), where it is sometimes grown for sale. <i>(1995,Which Coastal Plant?,30</i>)

>  New Zealand spinach is a native plant that Captain Cook used to treat his crew for scurvy. It is a coastal plant that scrambles on sand dunes, and will thrive in a dry sunny corner of your garden. <i>(May 1, 2008,NZ Herald,D2</i>)

### New Zealand sprat
 
 <i>n.</i>
