### New Zealand War(s)
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> the series of armed clashes between Maori and Pakeha in the 19th century. [New Zealand War (singular) in 19th-c. citations below] (See [Land Wars](l-lay.md#land-wars),[Maori War(s)](m-mar.md#maori-wars)))

>  ...there have been, he suggests, a number of worthwhile books about the New Zealand Wars. <i>(Jul 29, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

>  <b>BARBER</b> But it was not until the Second New Zealand War, the so-called land war, that the Queen's Maori soldiers came into their own. <i>(1984,Red Coat to Jungle Green,38</i>)

>  Their simple act of wading ashore onto the western banks of the independent Te Rohe Potae effectively ended the complex New Zealand Wars. <i>(Oct 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> I know there are people in England who say that the New Zealand War was purely agrarian, arising out of disputed lordship and ownership of the soil. <i>(1871,Autobiography,59</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> I know there are people in England who say that the New Zealand War was purely agrarian, arising out of disputed lordship and ownership of the soil. <i>(1871,Autobiography,59</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Having a little discussion about the New Zealand war, and to whose fault, that of the colonists or to the Maories, it was attributable; ...<i>Ibid.</i> 107 Thanking me in his letter [the Governor, Sir George Bowen] went on to urge me to `write a novel on the Maories and the New Zealand war' saying that he had pressed the same thing on Anthony Trollope. <i>(Aug 28, 1873,Diary,102,1953 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> Preface: This sketch of the Maori War ... A Sketch of the New Zealand War -<i>Ibid.</i> 169The New Zealand War lasted about ten years. <i>(1899,Sketch of the NZ War,title page</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> There is at least one sense, however, in which Cowan can be said to be ahead of his time. He steadfastly clung to the term `New Zealand Wars'. He rejected the more popular but misleadingexpression `Maori Wars', which implied that Maoris alone were responsible for the outbreaks of conflict. From the 1960s historians have explored more neutral and more accurate terms to characterise the period. Some opted for `Land Wars', but this is not entirely satisfactory because issues were involved in addition to that of land. Others suggested `Anglo-Maori Wars' but this too is less than adequate; the English were not the only Europeans involved and. as already noted, Maoris fought on both sides. For this latter reason `Maori-Pakeha Wars' is also unsuitable. ... If they are referred to as the New Zealand Wars they cannot be confused with any others, particularly if earlier actions involving Maoris alone are designated `tribal wars'. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,xi,1983 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1983 Michael King in 1922 Cowan NZ Wars (1983) xi]

>  <b>BARBER</b> Maori, Colonial and Imperial participation in the New Zealand wars in the 1840s and 1860s, ... marked the first 50 years of the army's life. <i>(1984,Red Coat to Jungle Green,8</i>)

>  But the most notable of the grievances was the raupatu - the confiscation of Maori land during the New Zealand wars of the 1860s in Taranaki, Waikato, Bay of Plenty, and the East Coast. <i>(1986,Na To Hoa Aroha,1/33</i>) [NOTES: Sorrenson]

### New Zealand whisper
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PEGLER</b> They had had a most enjoyable tea, and he thanked those ladies who had kindly prepared it. They had been favoured with beautiful weather, and by their visit to the Castle they had learned something more about their history. He thanked them again most heartily, and said he wished their two Maori chiefs were present to give them their native song, but they had left the country. But they would endeavour to sing in the Maori tongue `For he's a jolly good fellow.' Led by Mr. Bray, the New Zealanders then sang `For he's a jolly good fellow ' with much effect. They then gave the `New Zealand Whisper' for the ladies, after which `God Save the King' was sung by all present, Mr. Naylor (of St. George's Chapel Choir ) leading. Three cheers were next given for the visitors, finishing with an `English whisper' for the New Zealand ladies. A most enjoyable time then terminated with all joining in `Auld Lang Syne'. <i>(1926,Dominion Bowlers' Book,48 (?18)</i>)

### New Zealander
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) a Maori

>  <b>MACKIE</b> A customhouse boat came off to us manned by N. Zealanders or as they are called here Maoris (pronounced Mouries). <i>(1973,Traveller under Concern,72</i>) [NOTES: Quote from 30/1/1853]

>  Prior to the arrival of European navigators such as Abel Tasman, Captain Cook and De Surville, the indigenous people of New Zealand had no one word for themselves as an ethnic group. Instead, they were known by their tribal names of Tuhoe, Ngapuhi, Ngati Porou, etc. For recording purposes this was too cumbersome, so in their writings the navigators referred to the inhabitants of the country as New Zealanders. The closest concept the people had for such an all-encompassing label was tangata whenua, people of the land. But this did not come into general use until later as it had a more specific meaning in tribal relations between hosts and guests. <i>(May 14, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),100</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Chris Pugsley suggested one reason for Gallipoli's importance to us was that it was the first time large numbers of us had heard ourselves called New Zealanders and that simple fact changed out view of ourselves. <i>(Apr 27, 2005,Capital Times,Vol 30 / No 29 / p19</i>)

>  I observed that the term `New Zealander' is already employed in this district; only, in lieu of `Noui-Tireni", as the natives of the Bay of Islands pronounce it, they say `Noui-Tirangui' [NuiTirangi] [sic], which gives the word more of an indigenous sound. The word pakeha serves themto signify all whites, whom they also call `Iouropi' (Europeans). I did not observe that they had a special name for the English. <i>(1909,TrNZI,41/139</i>) [NOTES: Smith transl. D'Urville's Visit to Tolaga Bay in 1827]

### New Zealandisation
 
 <i>n.</i> the process of phasing out JOINT VENTURES between New Zealand and foreign fishing companies that followed the declaration of the 200-mile exclusive economic zone, and entailing the acquisition and operation of deep-sea fishing vessels by New Zealand companies.

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> In 1982 the government took its legislative pen and struck foreign vessels and joint ventures from the tax-credit list ... It was also part of the New Zealandisation process, applying additional pressure on joint ventures to make a commitment to the industry.<i>Ibid.</i> 351 By 1981 the major fishing companies ... were building up substantial fleets. Initially, much of their catching capacity had been through joint ventures, but increasingly they used their own vessels ... While this investment set the foundation for the New Zealandisation of the deepwater fishery, it increased pressure on the inshore fishery. <i>(2004,Hooked,278</i>)

>  Talley is known as a suporter of what the industry calls "New Zealandisation" replacing foreign vessels and crew with domestic capacity. <i>(Sep 19, 2004,Sunday Star Times,D1</i>)

>  He said it was an error to assume "New Zealandisation equates with the natural interest" and wrong to argue that "New Zealand ownership of all quota, no matter what the circumstances, will automatically be in the national interest." <i>(Feb 7, 2002,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  He has long been an advocate of what the industry calls "New Zealandisation" - building up New Zealand's fishing capability to the point where foreign capacity is required only at the margins. <i>(Sep 5, 2004,Sunday Star Times,D1</i>)

>  We have to be careful in this area of New Zealandisation of fisheries ... New Zealandisation is at the heart of this Bill. Hon Colin Moyle: This question of New Zealandisation [rather than foreign ownership] of fisheries ... <i>(Jul 15, 1986,NZ Parliamentary Debates</i>) [NOTES: H. of R. debate on Bill concerning overseas fishery interests (11.25 pm) [check Hansard: on privatisation]

>  <b>FRAME</b> They now say streams. And fields. And the Minister of Agriculture has been talking of the New Zealandisation of Fisheries! <i>(1988,Carpathians, The,30</i>)

<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>CASEY</b> Another paragraph on my unbounded praise for everything New Zealandish: the hotels, the railways, the roads ... - all the best in the world. <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,117</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> He is a handsome, slim man, thirty-nine, his bearing and dress (cocked slouch hat) and manner exactly suited to this setting. There is something distinctively New Zealandish focusing itself here. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,46</i>)

>  <b>HOWE</b> Do New Zealanders whose cultural roots are strongly European (from Scotland, Ireland, England, Yugoslavia, Holland etc.) feel as `New Zealand'ish as, say, those whose roots are strongly non-European (from the Cook Islands, Rarotonga, New Zealand Maori etc.) <i>(1985,Towards Taha Maori,3</i>)

### New Zealandism
 
 <i>n.</i> [idioms or characteristics of NZ / NZers]

>  We haven't had to give up any of our New Zealandisms to win them over. <i>(Nov 2, 2000,Evening Post,17</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [A New Zealand national spirit]

>  <b>McKAY</b> The main objection was not so much to Curnow's ill-proportioned selction, it was to the theory of New Zealand poetry on which it was based. That view Baxter and his friends regarded as `self-conscious New Zealandism' [fn Baxter's phrase in `The Kiwi and Mr Curnow' p. 29]. <i>(1990,James K. Baxter,187</i>)

>  In the late forties and fifties a numberof poets seceded from the self-consciousNew Zealandism of their immediatepredecessors and began to write simple aspeople who happened to live in a giventime and place... Mr Oliver's broadlucid account of the development of NewZealand poetry.. should provide asalutary antidote to Mr Curnow's NewZealandism. <i>(Feb 1961,Education,X(1) : 29</i>)

>  Each side has abundance of argument to demonstrate that it is the only one which has the right programme for Imperialism, New Zealandism, peace with honour, progress with prudence, and the like. <i>(Jun 7, 1915,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  Had Mr Mills possessed a very nice sense of honour he would have curbed the eagerness of his young New Zealandism and perhaps of something else in this particular instance. <i>(Jun 2, 1888,Southland Times,2</i>)

>  With the press and public men preaching assiduously the creed of "New Zealandism," the manufacturers bestirred themselves. <i>(Jun 4, 1908,Evening Post,3</i>)

### New Zealandness
 
 <i>n.</i> the essential New Zealand character of a person or thing.

>  This book, beautifully written and perceptive, only confirms the place that his work has already achieved as a representation of New Zealandness in a tradition of New Zealand literature. <i>(May 8, 1999,Dominion,22</i>)

>  Publishers around the world had turned it [the novel <i> Coal Flat </i>] down because of its great length and "New Zealandness". <i>(Apr 2010,North & South,96</i>)

>  An immigrant heritage means we're not "common" Kiwis. Immigrantness is invisible from New Zealandness. <i>(Nov 30, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  Amid the turmoil of the WRC disaster I realised how much this 'New Zealandness' means to me and how I'm the product of a Kiwi upbringing rooted in human values. <i>(Sep 8, 2002,Sunday Star Times,A5</i>)

>  .. a situation of Kiwiana as marginal differentiation in a postmodern late capitalist international culture ('New Zealandness' as the egg and beetroot in the McDonald's burger) - that is at least one way to describe the path of New Zealand writing as it has dealt with New Zealand cultural identity, at least as I have been experiencing it. <i>(Sep 2000,English in Aotearoa,12</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> I was much influenced by the West Indian writers and, feeling inadequate in my New Zealand-ness (for did I not come from a land then described as `more English than England'?) <i>(1985,Envoy from Mirror City,19</i>)

>  The interviewer puts his set questions on New Zealandness in poetry and on the literary influence of James K. Baxter and does not steer himself into prominence as much as he doeswhen he gets under way. <i>(Aug 1986,Islands,37/80</i>)

>  Tired notions of nationalism are paraded, so the building is ... not just a work of art but the first building to be true to this place, to capture the `real' New Zealandness of New Zealand. <i>(Jun 4, 1985,Dominion,11</i>)

>  The way he treated his patients, the way he put all the different ranks together, his straight-forward approach - he never lost his New Zealandness. <i>(Jul 21, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),51</i>)

>  We have a lot of brands in the market now which are New Zealand geographical indicators in their own right, or have a particular New Zealandness about them, such as Kikorangi or Aorangi [cheeses]. <i>(Sep 30, 2002,Dominion Post,C1</i>)

>  I worked then, as now, through literature, and nothing I wrote gives a clue to my native "New Zealandness" because New Zealand was not part of literature of my compulsions. <i>(May 22, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),86</i>)

>  It's partly the fact that we don't dilute our New Zealandness overseas that makes us attractive to those audiences. <i>(Feb 4, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>adj.</i>

 <i>adj.</i>

### New Zild
 
 <i>n.</i> New Zealand; New Zealand English [two senses mixed in citations below]

>  A different kind of abbreviation of the primary name is found in <i>New Zild</i> (also <i>Newzild, Noo Zild</i>), a conventional representation of the broad, maximally elided Kiwi pronunciation, which is traced in <i>DNZE</i> to the book <i>New Zild and How to Speak It</i> 1966, Arch Acker's answer to <i>Strine</i>, where <i>New Zild</i> is used both for the accent and for the country itself (although <i>DNZE</i> cites it as the name of the country only from the 1990s). Other (less clipped) renderings of the broad New Zild pronunciation recorded in <i>DNZE</i> include <i>New Zillun(d)</i> and <i>Noo Zilland</i> and, more idosyncratically, <i>NyaZilnd, N'yerzillun</i>, and <i>Newzyullind</i>. All such forms normally have either a jocular or a judgmental implication. <i>(Aug 2000,NZWords,4 : 2</i>)

>  But Australia is Oz and New Zealand is Newzild and I don't think we should confuse the two - particularly the accents. <i>(Aug 2, 1998,Sunday Star Times,A9</i>)

>  The other old favourites (Cooch, Cheeky, Hunk, Spit, Irish Murphy ...) are joined by some nasties (Vernon the Vermin, King of the Rats, and the deliciously vicious croco-pigs) to add flavour to tiny-town Newzild. <i>(Jan 31, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

>  In buying Newzild the council was setting an example to other quangos and to private enterprise. <i>(Oct 10, 1986,National Business Review,10</i>)

>  Witi Ihimaera, the author of Whale Rider, says 'I'm amazed at the transformational possibilities that Pacific creativity has offered little old New Zild. ..." <i>(Feb 2006,Metro,Issue 296: 52-57</i>)

>  However, I was unaware that the definition of Newzild had changed to "accent of the neurotically thin-skinned who are above criticism"... I will, however, brush up on my politically correct Maori pronunciation - Taipoo, Fangaray, O'armaroow, Fakatarnay; and at least beogin prectising Newzild - Unvucargoow, Heemoowtun, Weelungtun, Kroistcheerch. But what will Hyacinth Bucket think? <i>(Jun 9, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>)

>  [Hdg] NewZild rules... The Kiwi accent is a unique, developing and ever-changing one. <i>(Dec 19, 1998,Dominion,18</i>)

>  There's no mistaking novelist Ronald Hugh Morrieson territory - small-town, postwar (1949) Newzild, where the outside world arrives as parody, flashy and phoney, with feet of clay already cracking. <i>(Aug 31, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>)

>  It aims to be a musical satire of corporationist attitudes ruining Seacliffe, a previously idyllic rural town in the backblocks of Noo Zild. <i>(Jun 8, 1993,Salient,22</i>)

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> One mustn't be over-romantic, but I can barely overemphasise the significance of First Return in the literature of New Zild. <i>(1980,All My Lives,120</i>) [NOTES: 1980 Imogen De La Bere in Thompson All My Lives]

>  <b>ARCH ACKER</b> Newzilders speak Newzild. <i>(1966,New Zild,7</i>)

>  There are entries [in The Macquarie Dictionary] ... for Australian English, Australianism and Strine, but none for New Zealand English, New Zealandism or New Zild. <i>(Jan 21, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>) [NOTES: Deverson]

>  <b>OVENDEN</b> `That's French slang for a cigarette. I learned it today. I,m going to use it in Godzone and make it part of Newzild.' <i>(1986,O.E.,144</i>)

>  `For example, Chris McVeigh ... couldn't tell jokes unless he put on a cockney accent ... [sic] he couldn't time New Zild.' (meaning the accent.) <i>(Sep 1986,Metro,35</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> New Zild New Zealand the way others hear us say it, allegedly through gritted teeth. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,75</i>)

>  If you want to know how it feels to have made that journey from homophobic suburban Newzild to a tolerant new century, read <i>Long Loop Home</i>. <i>(Jun 30, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)

>  [Hdg] Forget the Europop, this is New Zild <i>(Aug 24, 2001,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  If, however, the name of the language we speak in this country was officially changed from "English" to "Noo Zild", I'd shrug my shoulders and shut up! After all, the Australians speak "Strine" rather well, don't they? <i>(Oct 31, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

>  They work with familiar material, slyly inverting cliches as well as pure New Zild like white Valiants, but they look at it from a fresh angle. <i>(Aug 23, 1992,Dominion Sunday Times,21</i>)

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> Now you must understand New Zild as sheis spoke. Elocution teachers sometimessay that New Zild is only lazy speech...In fact New Zild is a substitute forspeech, a verbal shorthand. New Zilders speak slowly as if they were boring themselves into sleep. <i>(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,181</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> (informal) of New Zealand [also Noo Zild below]

>  Jennifer Ludlam donned slippers, trackies and a broad New Zild accent to play the white-trash Connie, in <i>Potiki's Memory of Stone</i>. <i>(Jan 3, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),63</i>)

>  Her Lyn of Tawa character made us acutely aware of the New Zild accent and the Kiwi inflection. <i>(Nov 19, 2003,Capital Times,11</i>)

>  Her only comment on the writing, <i>per se</i>, is that it uses that New Zild bete noire "fancy words" - long words where good four-by-twos would do. <i>(Feb 24, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

>  Somehow their New Zealand accent is always different from your Newzild accent. <i>(Dec 23, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  A sigh of relief from the columnists and the letter writers who continue to police the spoken word for anything too ... "Newzild." <i>(Apr 7, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  In the meantime, she's interested to see how a New Zild <i>Neighbours</i> will work. <i>(Jan 15, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),33</i>) [NOTES: Listener & TV Times]

>  His flat, laconic New Zild tone is rivertalk fed by 200 years of historical tributaries: the talkback sound of the nation babbling in its sleep. <i>(Jan 16, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

>  Mel Dodge's Noelene is a diva of the New Zild accent; hanging onto her personal chunder-bucket, she leaves her voice ringing in your ears. <i>(Dec 2, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),53</i>)

>  A few years ago, his band Dark Tower got our attention with a radical reworking of the Country Calendar theme, set against slow beats and lyrics that name-checked Vegemite, Ches and Dale, the whole package redolent of long childhood summers on the sweltering plain, as Newzild as that big bottle in Paeroa, as sandflies and sunburn. <i>(Jan 22, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),63</i>)

>  middle New Zild English must be delivered with surgical precision <i>(Oct 1992,Political Review,34</i>)

>  Sharon Crosbie may be happy with the "Noo Zild" type pronunciation in general use on National Radio.. But what of the grammar".. Noo Zild pronunciation may be acceptable to some, but why is it that so many of the female presenters on TV are quite unable to pronounce the most common letter "e" and substitute an "a" in its place? <i>(Jan 2, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

>  [Hdg] Talking Noo Zild blues. The standard of English that Boris Moiseiwitsch complains of.. issimply the matey kind of spoken "Zilnd" that one hears in the pubs or work-places. <i>(Nov 28, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> `And what languages have you, Mr Thompson?' ... `English?' (I offered this in that apologetic interrogative tone that has since become the hallmark of New Zild speech.) <i>(1980,All My Lives,60</i>) [NOTES: [c.early 1950s]]

>  A recent review in the Christchurch Press reviewing a two-man show of Shadbolt and McCormick described them as, `New Zild raconteuers [sic] who vacuum epigrammaticsummaries from their experiences which are superbly indigenous.' <i>(Dec 31, 1985,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  The second tableau is as New Zild as Split Enz. <i>(Dec 3, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)

### new-chum's pigeon
 
 <i>n.</i> a moss

>  ... the long grey moss which hangs in festoons and beards from some of the trees in the heavy bush [especially in mountain country in the North Island]. One kind of the moss ... is almost white and very conspicuous in the gloom of the bush. The settlers' name for it in my part of the country is `new-chum's pigeon'. It is so called because of the imagined resemblance of the mossy bunches to the white breast of the wild pigeon; ... I'm hanged if it would deceive any New Zealander country-born or bush-bred. <i>(May 10, 1920,Quick March,39</i>)

>  <b>S. PERCY SMITH</b> This is a most beautiful little spot .. surrounded by .. belts of beautiful ngaio trees ... <i>(Jan 11, 1867,Taranaki Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1867 Jan 11 S. Percy Smith Taranaki Journal in 1922 Cowan NZ Wars 2/533]

>  Of our shrubs and smaller timber trees, several are of strikingly beautiful growth .. (e.g.) the Houhere, <i>Hoheria populnea</i> .. the Horopito <i>Drimys axillaris</i>; the Manuka-rau-riki, <i>Leptospermum ericoides</i>; the Kohuhu, <i>Pittosporum tenuifolium</i>; the Kowhai, especially the small-leaved mountain variety, <i>Sophora tetraptera</i> var.: <i>grandiflora</i>; the Koromiko-taranga, <i>Veronica</i>, several species; the Mairehau, <i>Phebalium nudum</i>; the Toro, <i>Persoonia Toro</i>; the Pukapuka, <i>Brachyglottis repanda</i>; the Northern Maire, <i>Santalum Cunninghamii</i>; the Tawari, <i>Ixerba brexioides</i>; the Tipau, <i>Myrsine Urvillei</i> and <i>M. salicina</i>; the Tangeao, <i>Tetranthera calicaris</i>; the Ramarama, <i>Myrtus bullata</i>; the Ti, <i>Cordyline australis</i>; the Kahikomako, <i>Pennantia corymbosa</i>; the Pate, <i>Schefflera digitata</i>; the Horoeka, <i>Panax crassifolia</i>: and, on the sea-coast .. the Karo, <i>Pittosporum crassifolium</i>; and the truly evergreen Ngaio, <i>Myoporum laetum</i> ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/5</i>) [NOTES: Colenso Botany North Isd]

>  The Titoki (<i>Alectryon excelsum</i>), the Akeake (<i>Dodonaea viscosa</i>), the Ngaio (<i>Myoporum laetum</i>), and above all the Rewa Rewa (<i>Knightia excelsa</i>), yield woods, out of which some very beautiful pieces of furniture have been made. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/III/47 (Buchanan Botany Otago) Ngaio. <i>Myoporum laetum</i>, Forst. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/14</i>) [NOTES: Monro]

>  Ngaio: wood light, white and tough, used for gun-stocks <i>(1873,Catalogue of Vienna Exhibition</i>) [NOTES: Morris]

>  <i> Myoporum laetum </i> (Ngaio). This is generally called kio by the colonist. <i>(Nov 3, 1892,Otago Witness</i>) [NOTES: 'Native Trees' (Morris)]

>  <b>J.G.WILSON</b> I think we should plant a hedge inside [the fence] but the best one will be Pittosporum or as they call it here [?in NZ rather than Aust] the Matipo or the Ngaio which makes I believe a beautiful hedge .. when it does not grow straggly. <i>(May 15, 1874,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1874 May 15 J.G.WILSON Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 191]

>  .. `you heard incessant cracking of 'gnias': a nut like an almond.' <i>(1881,First Voyage of the New 'Southern Cross'</i>) [NOTES: (And) [?where is this]]

>  Ngaio .. Myoporum laetum .. Wood dense and hard; used for fencing posts, rails, and ornamental work. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,12</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> (Kaio) <i>(1897,Counterfeit Seal</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Ngaio, n. </i>Maori name for a New Zealand tree, <i>Myoporum laetum</i>, Forst; generally corrupted into Kaio, in South Island. <i>(1898,Austral English,320</i>)

>  Myoporum Latum (-->Ngaio). This is generally called kio by colonists. It is very rapid-growing tree for the first five or six years after it has been planted. They are very hardy, and like the sea air. I saw these trees growing at St. Kilda, near Melbourne, thirty years ago.' <i>(Nov 3, 1892,Otago Witness</i>) [NOTES: 'Native Trees':]

>  The handsome ngaio [fn <i>Myoporum laetum</i>.], with its glossy leaves and pink-spotted flowers represents our garden verbenas. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,184</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> [<i>Myoporum laetum</i>, Forst.] <i>Ngaio. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,564</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Myoporum laetum (The Ngaio)</i>. Small tree, 10ft-40ft in height: Both islands: usually near the sea-shore. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,362,1940 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Kaio. A `popular corruption in the South Island of New Zealand of <i>Ngaio</i>,' the Maori name for <i>myoporum loetum</i>, a tree whose wood is used for gun-stocks: from ca, 1870. Morris. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,449</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> <i>ngaio</i>, a tree (Myoporum laetum). Rona caught hold of a young ngaio when the deity of the moon seized her when she called him a `cooked-head,' and the tree was taken up with her,and there she is in the moon with the tree and the calabash in which she was to bring water one night of drifting cloud. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/154</i>)

>  ngaio A handsome small New Zealand tree, growing chiefly near the sea. (Pronounce <i>ni|-o|</i>). <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),274,8th ed.</i>)

>  <i>ngaio n -s</i> [Maori] : a small tree (<i>Myoporum laetum</i>) of the New Zealand coast with edible fruit and light tough wood. <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1524</i>)

>  <i> ~ n.</i> New Zealand tree, with fine white wood [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),621</i>)

>  <i>ngaio .. n. </i>N.Z. tree, with fine white wood [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),14</i>)

>  <i>ngaio [s] Myoporum laetum </i>Forst. f. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,53</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Ngaio .. ngaio .. <i>Myoporum laetum.</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>ngaio n.</i> a small evergreen New Zealand tree. [Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,759</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 506; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 327]]

>  <b>SANSOM</b> Further round from Kelly's Beach you can find the site of the ancient Maori kaik and the cemetery, its landmark a huge ngaio tree. <i>(1982,In the Grip of an Island,140</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Under a ngaio, Mr. Bellister opened Wednesday's letter. <i>(1937,Wednesday's Children,272,1989 ed.</i>)

### ngaioing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILSON</b> ngaioing - the taking of birds in the first half of the season by wire and hooks . Ibid. 87The first half of the season is spent getting the birds from the burrows. (This is called Ngaioing). <i>(1979,Titi Heritage,[16]</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> This part of the season in which the young birds are pulled from the burrows during the daytime is called the ngaioing period. We called it the dirty period! To insert the hand right inside the burrow it is necessary to get right down on to the ground, so within a few moments of commencing work one's clothes were in a filthy condition, especially if there had been rain during the night. <i>(1979,Titi Heritage,120</i>)

### ngaione
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BROKKER et al.</b> The leaves [of the ngaio] contain mannitol and ngaione. Ngaione is an optical isomer of ipomeamarone derived from the sweet potato (cf. pomoea). Ngaione is related to furfural which has fungicidal and bactericidal powers and which has been used as a cure for athlete's foot and swimmer's itch. ... Mannitol is used medicinally. <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,69</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] an essential oil of the ngaio tree that causes poisoning in cattle [ and possibly other stock.]

>  <b>NZ VET ASSN</b> .. an essential oil, ngaione, mainly in the leaves. <i>(1971,Diseases of Domestic Animals in New Zealand,285</i>)

### ngaiore
 
 <i>n.</i> Williams 227 <i>Ngaiore </i> ... A fish. Young of smelt, <i>Retropinna retropinna. = paraki, ngore.: </i>

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> The larval smelts [<i>Retropinna</i> sp.] which run upstream at the close of the whitebait season in November are termed Ngaiore on the Whanganui ... <i>Ibid.</i> 56 Maoris at Hiruharama, Whanganui River, showed me stores of larval examples caught in the river and dried in the sun. These were salted and eaten during winter months, being there universally termed `ngaiore'. <i>(1940,Fishes NZ,53</i>)

### ngaki
 
 <i>n.</i> a taniwha (Maori mythical monster)

>  .. Paul moves past candlesticks taller than she is as she relates the story of the taniwhas Ngaki and Whataitai. <i>(Dec 8, 2004,Capital Times,p14</i>)

### ngakoikoi
 
 <i>n.</i> Williams 228 <i>Nga[a]koikoi</i>... a fish.: =KELPFISH

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> KELPFISH ... <i>Other names: Chironemus marmoratus; hiwihiwi, ngakoikoi (Maori). </i> <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,250</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> KELPFISH [<i>Chironemus marmoratus</i>] ... Other names include the Maori hiwihiwi and ngakoikoi. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,26</i>)

### ngamu
 
 <i>n.</i> a sandfly (See [namu](n-new.md#namu))

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> The sandflies, on the other hand, the Ngamu of the natives, small midges (Simulium), are most frequent on the sea coast. <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),287</i>)

### Ngapara gravel
 
 <i>n.</i> [Otago local]

>  <b>SCOTTER</b> Quartz gravels [of the Waireka/Kakanui Valleys] called Ngapara gravel, are much used to form attractive paths and drives, ... <i>(1948,Run, Estate and Farm,Introduction: no page no</i>)

### Ngapuhi
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori tribe

>  "They're kind because I wasn't cheeky. I wasn't a typical Ngapuhi showoff, you know, and I was humble." <i>(Apr 27, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)

### ngarara
 
 <i>n.</i> money-spider

>  <b>LOCKLEY</b> They're only little ngarara [insects], `money-spiders' you Pakeha call them. I'll find a real katipo for you. <i>(1980,House Above the Sea,15</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a lizard; (in Maori myth) a reptilian monster.

>  Why he should call it a nagara he did not know, for that was merely the Maori name for a lizard. <i>(Mar 15, 1890,NZ Observer,9</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Nga r|ara; General name for reptiles. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,229</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 6. <i>Hatteria punctata</i>. Gray, ... Gigantic Lizard, <i>Cook's </i>Voy., 3, I. 153., or Guana. - <i>Polack</i>, N.Z. i. 317. ... `I have been apprized of the existence of a large lizard, which the natives called <i>Tuatera</i>, or <i>Narara</i>, with a general name, and of which they were much afraid. ...' <i>Dieffenbach. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/204</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/204-205]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> ... they [the natives] tried much to dissuade me from the attempt [to ascend Mount Egmont], by saying that the mountain was `tapu', and that there were ngarara (crocodiles) on it, which would undoubtedly eat me; the mysterious bird `moa,' ... was also said to exist there. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/140</i>)

>  Lizard (Ngarara). <i>(1842,Journal of Ensign Best,353,1966 Taylor ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Was it not right to drive away the [231] ngararas, or reptiles, from them (so they called their ancient gods) <i>(Sep 10, 1851,Journal,7/230</i>)

>  ... the presiding deity has been offended: - his vengeance assuming the form of a Ngarara (lizard) enters the body of man, consumes his vitals, and thereby causes death. <i>(Aug 15, 1857,Lyttelton Times,3</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> There are six small harmless lizards in the colony, which are held in terror by the natives, from a superstition that bodies within the spirits of their deified ancestors revisit the earth ... In such terror do the New Zealanders hold all the above reptiles, that the very pronunciation of the word Ngarara, a general term for the whole race, makes the bravest warrior tremble. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/29</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> On the 11th of June we started from the cave, called by the natives Te-ana-o Matuku, respectingwhich they tell a very interesting tale of a large ngarara, a monster in the form of an enormous lizard, which formerly lived here, and devoured all who attempted to pass. Since they have become Christians, however, they have called it by another name, Te whare Taipo (the Devil's house), probably referring the formation of the cave, of which I shall speak more fully in the geological part of my report, to supernatural agency rather than to the hand of nature. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,50</i>)

>  Unu ngarara or ngarara burrows were frequently met with on the plains. ... The ngarara was darker in colour than the ruatara. They varied in size from two or three feet in length, and ten to twenty inches in girth ... <i>(1875,TrNZI,7/296</i>) [NOTES: Stack]

>  <b>HAY</b> ... the only reptiles are pretty little ngarara, or lizards (<i>Mocoa Zelandica</i> and <i>M. ornata), ... </i> <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/214</i>)

>  The ridge-boards are carved to represent a number of <i>ngarara</i>, or lizards, running along the roof, and the<i> maihi</i>, or gable-boards, have carvings of the mythological animal known as the <i>manaia</i> - probably a kind of taniwha. <i>(1895,TrNZI,27/674</i>)

>  The ridge-boards are carved to represent a number of <i>ngarara</i>, or lizards, running along the roof, and the<i> maihi</i>, or gable-boards, have carvings of the mythological animal known as the <i>manaia</i> - probably a kind of taniwha. <i>(1953,Short Stories (various authors),13</i>) [NOTES: 1901 Grace Tales of a Dying Race in 1953 Short Stories 13]

>  The use of a lizard-form as a personal ornament amongst a Maori people must have been rare, as in most cases all kinds of <i>ngarara</i> were regarded with horror and aversion. <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/112</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> [The Maori's] existence was burdened with the knowledge of huge reptilian monsters, ngarara on land, and taniwha in the water, who could speak, and roar, ... [39] and decoy with satanic duplicity! <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,38</i>)

>  <i>ngarara:</i> fabulous monster reptile. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,61</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> [Maori glossary] <i>ngarara</i> reptile, monster <i>(1969,History of New Zealand,319</i>)

>  <i>ngarara n</i>. NZ monster; extinct unidentified lizard.[M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,506</i>) [NOTES: nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]

>  The bone was supposed to belong.. to the Kumi, and extinct.. lizard of great size and ferocity, a species of the Ngarara, the Reptile Gods. <i>(1944,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),2(15) : 13</i>)

>  <b>Ihimaera</b> Then suddenly, around the corner came that iron horse, a huge ngarara, a monster, belching smoke and roaring at us. <i>(2007,Ask the Posts,256</i>)

### Ngaterian
 
 <i>adj.</i> rock classification

### Ngati
 
 <i>n.</i> short for Ngati Porou [DNZE p.536 attrib. here] (See [DNZE p.536](../D#dnze-p.536))

>  According to Ngati tradition this tree dates from time of the ancestorRerekohu who lived thirteen generations ago. <i>(Mar 29, 1987,Ephemera</i>) [NOTES: sighted on a notice on the pohutukawa Te Wahao Rerekohu (The Mouth ofRerekohu) at Te Araroa.]



 
 <i>pref.</i> first element in Maori tribal names: = people of

>  Warfare in the Rotorua lakes district in the early 1700's between Te Arawa iwi Tuhourangi, Ngati Whakaue and Ngati Pikiao climaxed with Tuhourangi being driven south to Tarawera. <i>(Nov 13, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)

>  [Caption] <i>Standing in the Sunshine</i>: a young woman from the Hippolite family of Ngati Koata poses with her bicycle for a Tyree Studio photographer in 1901. <i>(Nov 27, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),67</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> Names of tribes (iwi) were generally denoted by the prefix Ngati, meaning `descendants of', as Ngati-Raukawa the descendants of Raukawa. Sometimes sub-tribes (hapu) or [162] or small remnants of tribes used Ngati before their names. The prefix varied into Ati and Ngai as Te Ati-awa, Ngai-tahu. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,161</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (i) occasionally used for the local people or tangata whenua: 'the Ngati have placed a temporary prohibition on the use of this road.' (ii) (transferred use) a brumby, wild Maori horse (chiefly of the central North Island plateau)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> Ngati is the word most commonly prefixed to the name of an ancestor to designate his descendents. It is the compound word Nga-ati, signifying the offspring. <i>(1865,Traditions & Superstitions,225</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Here's how we're going to catch ourselves some of those long-haired, tussock-eating ngatis ... The brumbies are used to us coming and going along there now. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,54</i>)

>  ngati n. N.Z. a tribe or clan. [< Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,759</i>) [NOTES: cf. 1986 NZPOD 506; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 327]



 
 <i>pref.</i> indicating humorously or ironically a quasi-Maori tribe or entity; see cits.

>  Welcome to the world of the ngati cappucino. At number 15 on National's list, unveiled last week, Parata is the cream of a new breed of upwardly mobile Maori who enjoy many of the trappings of Western urban life, but are equally confident in the Maori tribal world... <i>Ibid.</i> C14 The ngati cappucino made its first show of force in National a year ago when Parata's husband.. rolled incumbent Maori vice-president of the party, Dale Stephens. <i>(Jun 23, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C1</i>) [NOTES: As opposed to previous use, this citation applies to Maori, not pakeha.]

>  [Hdg] Ngati chardonnay... New Zealand's first Maori-owned and branded wine company, Tohu is a partnership formed in 1998 by three Maori businesses... <i>(Dec 14, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),15</i>)

>  On that sunny afternoon our liberty and their "privilege" threw into relief Api Taylor's angry line "Ngati DB is my tribe ... Goal is my home." <i>(Jan 31, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5</i>)

>  He also revealed that his former colleagues at the Foreign Affairs Ministry are known in Maori as "ngati aorere", or the "high fliers" - apt given their location in the 25-storey HSBC (Lambton) Tower, one of Wellington's tallest buildings. <i>(Jul 13, 2004,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  Thirteen-year-old Hawke's Bay designer Te Kaihou Ngarotata moved the front row to tears with her strong and proud presentation of her streetwear Ngati Babe and Ngati Bro labels, inspired by the forest and its surroundings, and featuring prints and embroidery details of the extinct huia. <i>(Oct 30, 2003,Dominion Post,D3</i>)

>  With even iwis now objecting to local prisons, there is now a Ngati Nimby for the Government to contend with each time it tries to house the bulging prison population. <i>(May 3, 2001,Dominion,2</i>)

>  There were four companies, A Company... B. Company.. C.Company, the Cowboys and D.Company, the Ngati Walkabout." <i>(Apr 21, 2000,TV1,Waka Huia</i>) [NOTES: Quote from memory]

>  Wellington residents - the Ngati Cappucino - with their inner-city apartments and faux-European cafe society, claim to represent a burgeoning culture while slagging off the bogan enclave right next door in the Hutt Valley. <i>(May 18, 2000,Dominion,11</i>)

>  "As soon as you become Ngati Prebble, you're no longer Ngati Porou..." <i>(Feb 15, 2003,Dominion Post,A15</i>) [NOTES: Ngati Prebble i.e. the ACT Party]

>  How dreary and drab the tribe of Ngati Big Brothers is this time round. <i>(May 11, 2002,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  A few days ago at my medical centre I was asked my "ethinithicity" (sic). I was happy to be able to reply with confidence, "Ngati Pakeha Murihuku". <i>(Apr 17, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>)

>  The first of these was Saul, from Ngati Benjamin... <i>(Nov 9, 2002,Dominion Post,A13</i>)

>  <b>Dave Armstrong</b> 'Typical,' thundered Tipene angrily. 'Forget Ngapuhi - the biggest iwi in this country is Ngati Bloody Welfare.' <i>(2007,Six Pack 2, The,115</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> Then I pushed [the Maori Councils Act] away, amazed that ... the Minister who fathered it, should have forgotten that the Maori acts from compulsion ... what Ngati-one proposes, Ngati-two will promptly oppose; while Ngati-three looks on and chuckles; and Ngati-pakeha pulls out his hair, and desperately wonders what next he shall do; ... <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,241</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Ngati Drongo not our tribe! eg `Was that your lot involved in the Maori Loans Affair?' `Nah. That was Ngati Drongo.' [Tom Scott] <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,75</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> (Sad Joke on a Marae) My name is Tu the freezing worker./ Ngati D.B. is my tribe./ The pub ismy Marae./ My fist is my taiaha. <i>(1979,Eyes of the Ruru,15</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> `Don't you understand English, Ngati DB?' `You're Ngati DB yourself, come to that.' <i>(1986,Te Kaihau,130</i>)

>  The addition of real life porn maker Steve Crow (<i>Vixen Direct</i>) into the mix, and the borrowing of what was presumably his premises, had one wondering if <i>Anal Mana</i> was the real Mc(or Ngati)Coy... <i>(Nov 27, 2004,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

>  8PM, MAORI TV <b>NGATI NRL</b> This series follows young Maori and Pacific Island league players as they try to crack the NRL ... <i>(Mar 30, 2006,NZ Herald,EG / 8</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### Ngati Pakeha
 
 <i>n.</i> Pakeha viewed in terms of a Maori tribe

The person to whom he was speaking responded, "I'll have to ask you about your kaupapa some time." He: "I'm Ngati Pakeha." <i>(Jul 13, 2002,Personal Communication</i>)

>  Maori might legitimately question where the privilege is in this furore, but Ngati Pakeha, with its catalogue of grievances real and imagined, is speaking with the louder voice. <i>(Mar 1, 2004,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  He tried his best to be provocative - claiming that "Ngati Pakeha" were getting all the money and putting parliament "on notice". <i>(Feb 18, 2001,Sunday Star Times,A7</i>)

>  Some Ngati Pakeha intermarried with me and became members of my whanau and hapu. <i>(Oct 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

>  [Hdg] Ngati Pakeha frightened off. <i>(Feb 26, 2004,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  The Government's stance on the foreshore and seabed, John Tamihere's red-blooded heterosexual blokery, Trevor Mallard's assertion he is Ngati Pakeha o Wainuiomata, Paul Swain's business-friendly employment law changes... <i>(Sep 6, 2004,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  The Maori language speaker, who jokes about being "Ngati Pakeha and proud" would be responsible for getting the trust to "take off", particularly with regards to its main source of revenue, fishing, and area of future development, tourism. <i>(Sep 27, 2003,Dominion Post,A7</i>)

### Ngati Poneke
 
 <i>n.</i> the Wellington Maori Club

>  And so the Ngati Poneke Young Maori Club was born. The name was taken from that given by Sir Apirana Ngata to a concert party in 1936 (Poneke is a transliteration of Port Nicholson... Ngati Poneke assured they [sc. young Maori] didn't slip down the cracks into what was a pretty wretched situation. <i>(Nov 10, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),59</i>)

>  In the 50s my friend Dalia and I got jobs with <i>Ngati Poneke</i> at the soft drink counter. <i>(Dec 1996,Mana,No. 14 : 24</i>)

### Ngati Porou
 
 <i>n.</i> an East Coast iwi

>  "I came from Ngati Porou, the tribe that the sun shines on first," she says laughing broadly. <i>(Jun 23, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C1</i>)

>  It seemed that Ngati Porou faithful were everywhere. <i>(Nov 25, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

### Ngati Toa
 
 <i>n.</i> a Wellington iwi

>  So Ngati Toa blockaded the Wairau Plains track, pulled out surveyors' pegs and burnt down a raupo hut they were using. <i>(Jun 1998,Cuisine,No. 22: 21</i>)

### Ngati tourism
 
 <i>n.</i> Maori cultural tourism

>  Ngati tourism benefits all <i>(Nov 1, 2005,NZ Herald,E2</i>) [NOTES: headline]

### ngawa
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> Hochstetter divides the steam jets into three kinds: 1. Puias, which are geysers, continuously orintermittently active. 2. Ngawas, which are inactive Puias - emitting steam, but not throwing up columns of water. 3. Waiarikis, which mean any sort of cistern of hot water suitable for bathing. <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,11</i>)

### ngawaka
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [kawaka](k-kea.md#kawaka))

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Libocedrus [plumosa]</i>. ... Native name <i>Kawaka</i> or <i>Ngawaka</i>, often termed <i>New Zealand Arbor Vitae</i> by the settlers. [earlier L. Doniana] <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,66,1940 p. 66 ed.</i>)

### ngawha
 
 <i>n.</i> Wiiliams 232 <i>Nga[a]wha[a] ... 2. Typha angustifolia ... =raupo[o].:</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Ngawha, Lindsay. Typha angustifolia. <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  People opposed to the building of the jail at Ngawha, near Kaikohe, moved on to the site on Saturday and refused access to workers trying to finish earthworks. <i>(Jun 5, 2002,Evening Post,4</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Williams 232 Nga[a]wha[a] ... 1. Boiling spring, or other volcanic activity.: early spg ngawa used in the North Island as a borowing from Maori passing into semi-technical geological or naturalists' use, ? passing out of use in the early 20th century)

>  The chief ngawha from which the greatest supply of water is derived is called "The Sodawater Spring." <i>(Mar 19, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  At intervals of considerable length, sometimes extending to many months, all these "ngawhas" begin to play together and form a scene which must be wonderful and beautiful. <i>(Jul 8, 1859,Auckland Provincial Gazette,VIII(14) : 98</i>)

>  Dr Hochstetter.. predicted that this very spot, in the vicinity of which are numerous ngawhas, fumeroles and hotwater streams, would some time be the area of a great volcanic eruption. <i>(Jan 15, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  <b>SELWYN</b> Here [at Ohinemutu] are to be seen all the varieties of Ngawha (hot-springs). There are mud cauldrons, black, blue, grey, green, yellow, and red, the very emblem [31] of laziness; ... <i>(1845,New Zealand (various authors/titles),30</i>) [NOTES: 1843 Oct 30 Selwyn Journal in 1845 NZ Part II (Church in the Colonies VII) 30]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> This is a most singular place [Ohinemutu] it is literally filled with ngawas and many are very[182] dangerous and many lives have been lost the natives cook all their provisions in them ... some are paved over on which they dry their hinanga and shell fish, ... <i>(Nov 26, 1845,Journal,3/181</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> We ... found the pa enveloped in vapour, which was rising from the numerous ngawha. <i>Ibid.</i> 169 We were received by a venerable-looking old man, ... who informed us that we must proceed to the ngawha by land, as the river was under tapu so that no canoe could enter it, . <i>(1959,Early Travellers,155</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Johnson Notes from Jrnl in 1959 Taylor Early Travs 155]

>  The word Puia is especially used in the Taupo country, to designate the intermittent, geyser-like fountains of Tokanu ... and of Whakarewarewa on Lake Rotorua. Puia has ... the more general meaning of crater or volcano, and is applied to active as well as extinct volcanoes. ... Ngawhas [is] a term specially used for non-intermittent springs, for the solfataras and sulphurous hot-springs on the Rotomahana, Rotorua, and Rotoiti. Finally, the springs suited to bathing purposes, the water of which never reaches the boiling point, and all naturally warm baths are called `Waiariki,' corresponding to the Laugar of Iceland. -->= ? ngawha <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/171</i>)

>  <b>MEADE</b> [We] soon found ourselves at the foot of the great ngawha, or hot spring, Te Tarata - a natural wonder ... [ibid. 449] There are about twenty-five large ngawha, as the natives term the hot springs of the Te Tarata kind, scattered round the lake, and many hundred smaller ones. <i>(Dec 30, 1870,Ride Through Disturbed Districts</i>) [NOTES: 1870 Dec 30 Meade Ride Through Disturbed Districtsin 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 447]

>  These characters ... distinguish the thermal waters of White Island from the Puias and Ngawhas of the Rotorua and Taupo Districts ... <i>(1871,TrNZI,3/282</i>) [NOTES: Davis]

>  ... innumerable steam jets, ngawhas, fumeroles, and geysers .... <i>(1873,TrNZI,5/337</i>) [NOTES: Kirk]

>  <b>TINNE</b> The `Babies' Cauldron' ... never rose into the air with a surging column of water, like the more turbulent ngahaus around. <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,14</i>)

>  Immediately across the ferry is a nearly circular Ngawha, or pond of boiling water, some twenty feet in diameter ... ... The water of this Ngawha is of a rich mazarine blue ... <i>(1877,TrNZI,9/8</i>) [NOTES: Travers `on the Lake Districts ...']

>  Hochstetter's map of Rotomahana, published in 1889 ..... shows the location of most of the ngawhas, puias, fumeroles, and solfataras that surrounded Rotomahana. <i>(1905,TrNZI,37/452</i>) [NOTES: Hill]

>  ... all aspects of volcanic phenomena occur, from the active volcano, as Ngaurahoe and Tongariro, to the slowly dying ngawha and puiaa as seen at places like Tokaanu ... [281] ... abounding with terraces, puias, ngawhas, and geysers ... <i>(1911,TrNZI,43/278, 281</i>) [NOTES: Hill]

>  <b>FULLER</b> In the thermal areas of the North Island it is not long ago that one could watch people diving for carp, ... and cooking them in the ngawha (hot springs). <i>(1978,Maori Food and Cookery,13</i>)

>  [Caption] In the Ngawhas, Whakarewarewa, in the Thermal Springs District, Auckland. <i>(Dec 28, 1920,Otago Witness,27</i>)

 <i>n.</i> Williams 232 <i>Ngehe</i> ... 4. n. A fish. =<i>kehe</i>.: cf KEHE

### ngere-ngere
 
 <i>n.</i> leprosy

>  The disease known to the Taupo and East Coast tribes as "ngere-ngere," to the Ngapuhi and Northern tribes as "puhipuhi" and to the Wanganui and Western tribes as "tuwhenua," is oneand the same disease, and is true leprosy. <i>(Jul 31, 1890,Otago Witness,19</i>)

### ngeri
 
 <i>n.</i> Williams 233 Ngeri (i) ... 2. A rough kind of cloak.: early spg neri

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... their dresses consisted of the kaitaka and karowai, made of silky flax, and covered with dog-skin mats, or neris [sic: e-acute] made of rushes, as protection against rain. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/177-178 A valuable garment for the stormy [178] climate of the southward is called neri[e-acute], and is made of undressed flax or of the kierakiki, a species of rush, impervious to rain. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/158</i>)

>  From [flax] the Chief's elegant and ornamented silky Paipairoa, and the shaggy bee-butt looking Pake and Ngeri, - with their many intermediate kinds of clothing mats, - were alone manufactured. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/33</i>) [NOTES: Colenso Botany North Isd]

>  <b>BULLER</b> Covered with the Ngeri (a coarse flax mat), they looked like so many thatched bee-hives. <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,27</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> ngeri: rough kind of cloak. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,193</i>)

### ngirungiru
 
 <i>n.</i> the South Island tomtit. Compare MIROMIRO. [Maori] Williams 233 <i>Ngirungiru ... Petroica toitoi, white-breasted tit</i> (North Island), and <i>P. macrocephala macrocephala, yellow-breasted tit</i> (South Island). = <i>miromiro</i> ...: spg also <i>ngidu ngidu, ngiru-ngiru </i>erron. <i>ngoru-ngoru</i> cf also MIROMIRO, PIRANGIRANGI : ? white breasted ? or yellow-breasted SI tit

>  <b>LEE& KENDALL</b> Ngi|du ng|idu; A bird so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,230</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>Ngirungiru</i> - This bird lays its eggs in the holes of trees; ... It is a very small bird, not larger than the tom-tit; ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),56</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The <i>nirungiru</i> [check] (Parus macrocephalus), or great-headed titmouse, is very common in the country. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/298</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 28. <i>Miro toitoi</i>, ... Nirungiru [sic] of the natives. <i>Polack</i>. Ngirungiru of the natives. <i>Yate, Dieffenbach</i>. To-i-toe of the natives. <i>Less. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/191</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/191]

>  PETROICA MACROCEPHALA, Gml. Ngirungiru. Piro piro. Tomtit. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/59</i>) [NOTES: Potts Birds]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 29. PETROICA DIEFFENBACHII. Gray. Yellow-breasted Tit. Ngirungiru. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,12</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> MYIOMOIRA TOITOI. (PIED TIT {NORTH ISLAND TOMTIT}.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Miromiro, Komiromiro, {Pimiromiro,} Ngirungiru{, Pingirungiru, and Pipitori}. ... [40] Its note in the early morning is like the Maori syllables <i>ngi-i-ru, ngiru-ngiru</i>, from which it derives its name, the first syllable being rather prolonged. <i>Ibid.</i> MYIOMOIRA MACROCEPHALA. (YELLOW-BREASTED TIT {SOUTH-ISLAND TOMTIT}.) <i>Native names</i>. Miromiro, Komiromiro, {Pimiromiro,} Ngirungiru{, Pingirungiru, and Pipitori}... <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,124,1888 2nd edn 1/39 ed.</i>)

>  28. <i>Petroeca macrocephala</i>, Gml. Yellow-breasted Tit or Robin, Miro-miro, Ngoru-ngoru [sic]. <i>(Nov 1884,NZ Journal of Science,286</i>) [NOTES: Potts]

>  <i>miromiro</i>, a bird (Petroeca toitoi), North Island tomtit; white-breasted tit it differs from the South Island form (P. macrocephala) yellow-breasted tit in the colouration of the breast. Whilst miromiro was the name of the North Island bird, both of them bore the name ngirungiru, so that has been adopted for the South Island form, though the South Island Maori would probably give the `ng' sound scurvy treatment and call the bird kirukiru, as he calls ngaio kaio. .. <i>ngirungru</i>, a bird (Petroeca macrocephala), South Island tomtit, <i>see</i> miromiro. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/154</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> TOMTIT <i>Petroica macrocephala Other names</i>: Miromiro (North Island), Ngirungiru (South Island), Wheedler, Butcher-bird (Taranaki). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,200</i>)

>  257.<i> b. Petroica macrocephala macrocephala<i> (Gmelin, 1789) <i>Yellow-breasted Tit (Ngiru-ngiru)</i> ... South Island. <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,68,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Tit Petroica macrocephala</i> (GMELIN, 1789) ... <i>Pied Tit P.m.</i> toitoi (LESSON, 1828). <i>Yellow-breasted Tit P.m. macrocephala</i> (GMELIN, 1789). <i>Chatham Island Tit P.m. chathamensis </i> FLEMING, 1950. <i>Black Tit P.m.dannefaerdi </i> ROTHSCHILD, 1894). <i>Auckland Island Tit P.m. marrineri </i>(MATHEWS & IREDALE, 1913). ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Ngiru-ngiru, piropiro, South Island tit, Maui-potiki, North Island tit or tomtit, white-breasted tit or tomtit, Snares tit, Snares Island robin. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,284</i>)

### ngoiro
 
 <i>n.</i> Williams 234 <i>Ngo[o]iro</i> ... 3. Young gulls, karoro.: (See [karoro](k-kea.md#karoro); [southern black-backed gull](sho-spa.md#southern-black-backed-gull))

>  The dissimilarity in appearance of immature gulls and their elders prompted Maori to give fully grown birds one name - karoro - and juveniles another - ngoiro. <i>(May 2005,NZ Geographic,73 / 47</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> LARUS DOMINICANUS. (SOUTHERN BLACK-BACKED GULL.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Karoro; the young bird distinguished as Ngoiro, {Koiro, and Punua}. [271] This fine Gull, which ranges over the whole Southern Hemisphere, is extremely plentiful. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,270,1888 2nd edn 2/47 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SHAND</b> The young of many sea birds ... were used as food [on the Chathams], such as <i>Kuaka</i>(plover), young gulls (<i>Ngoiro</i>), shags (<i>Kuau</i>) ... <i>Ho[macron]po</i> (the albatross), H <i>Hakoakoa</i> (mutton bird), <i>Taiko</i>[macron] (a smaller-sized mutton bird of slatey blue colour), <i>Titi</i>[i's macron] (a still smaller size) ... <i>(1911,Moriori People of the Chatham Islands, The,6</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al</b> SOUTHERN BLACK-BACKED GULL <i>Larus dominicanus Maori name</i>: Karoro, Ngoiro (imm). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,154</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the conger eel. [Maori ngoiro[o1-macron]] Williams 234 <i>ngo[oiro ... 1. Leptocephalus conger, conger eel</i>. see also<i> ngoringori</i> (Williams 235 1. a small black eel.) =CONGER EEL (See [also ngoringori](../A#also-ngoringori))

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Ng|oiro, s. A fish so called; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,230</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> CONGER EEL (<i>Conger vulgaris</i>). ... [18] Known to the Natives as ngoi[r]o, and attains a very large size, with a length of 6ft. ... Congers are captured by hooks and lines, either used by the hand, or long lines with hooks at regular intervals... <i>ibid.</i> 70 NGOIRO (see CONGER EEL) <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,17</i>)

>  <b>BEST</b> The following fish were caught with hook and line: araara, maomao, moki, mango, ngoiro, pakirikiri, kahawai, tamure, tarakihi, and warehou. The kanae, kehe, kopipiro, moho, nanu and other species were taken by net. <i>(1929,Fishing Methods,43</i>)

>  8. <i>Leptocephalus conger</i> Linnaeus. Conger Eel. Maori: Ko-iro, Ngoiro. <i>(1929,TrNZI,61/140</i>) [NOTES: Young]

>  <b>POWELL</b> CONGER EEL (<i>Leptocephalus verreauxii</i>), Ngoiro of the Maoris, is the common one of a number of marine eels. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,64</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> <i>Other names: Conger verreauxi</i>; ngoiro, koiro (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,198</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> CONGER EEL [<i>Conger verreauxi</i>] ... The Maori names koiro and ngoiro are sometimes used. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,30</i>)

### Nibble Nook
 
 <i>n.</i> theatre sweet and ice-cream franchise. 1960s esp. in Kerridge-Odeon theatres [obs]
