### nibbler
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a high country kea that rips wool from sheep's backs but does not kill them.

>  <b>MCKENZIE</b> If they are within easy approach and not killers but nibblers, simply mimic the call-kea and they will come right up close to be shot. Nibblers do not actually do harm, just rip the wool on the back of a sheep, and then fly off to another. Apart from that aspect one never knows when a nibbler wil become a killer. <i>(1951,Wakatipu Pioneers,99</i>)

>  <b>MCKENZIE</b> They [keas] automatically took up their positions, some holders, some nibblers, as though they were highly trained. <i>(1951,Wakatipu Pioneers,106</i>)

### nibby
 
 <i>n.</i> a mustering stick [station]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> By dint of much labour, not to mention the mining of several nibbies (mustering sticks) they finally chipped a hole in ice. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,86</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a straight mustering stick with no hook. (See [hillpole](hea-hom.md#hillpole),[hillstick](hea-hom.md#hillstick); [mustering stick](mob-mys.md#mustering-stick); [mustering pole](mob-mys.md#mustering-pole); [rakau](ra-red.md#rakau))

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The high-country musterer covers a lot of dangerous country, day after day, and with nothing to aid him but his nibby or walking stick. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,94</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> .. with the aid of my trusty nibbie I was able to get about slowly. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,162</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> By dint of much labour, not to mention the ruining of several nibbies (mustering sticks) they finally chipped a hole in the ice. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,86</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> The stick he's carrying is a 'nibby' or hill stick, made from a seasoned piece of straight manuka stripped of its bark. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,22</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> The mustering stick, known as a 'hillstick' or 'nibbie', was usually made from manuka and had a metal ferrule. <i>(1990,Forgotten Worker, The,63</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> nibbie: a musterer's staff, usually made of straight manuka. When killing a mutton, the nibbie is put through the hind legs so two men can support it while the third man finishes the dressing. [nibbie] <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,164</i>)

### nibling / niebling
 
 <i>n.</i> collective noun for nieces and nephews coined by Trina Stevens

>  Trina Stevens says she has invented the word "niblings", on the analogy of "siblings", to define nieces and nephews. She says it arose from frustration in describing family groupings with multiple nieces and nephews. It also owes something to "anklebiter". <i>(Jan 28, 2004,Dominion Post,B 5</i>)

>  <i>nieblings:</i> A collective term for your nieces and nephews, after siblings. try it it's handy. <i>(Dec 18, 2005,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),10</i>)

### niching
 
 <i>n.</i> [ODE has niche v.]

>  [He] says that niching - which might take the form of grouping specialised factory outlets, for example - is also proving popular with the consumer looking for most effectiveness. <i>(Jan 30, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

### nick
 
 <i>n.</i> IN THE NICK, in the nude (cf also NICKETTY BOO)



 
 <i>v.</i> with away, off, out, to depart (?quickly and often surreptitiously) -->to nip, pop, hop etc off (away) etc (Prob. a variant of nip off) [ca1951 Wellington X's nicked away to have a beer; ca1953 Wellington (H.S. Gajadhar) I'll just nick off and get some lunch.]

>  <b>McGILL</b> nick away to leave, usually surreptitiously or quickly; eg `Look, I'm going to nick away before the speeches start. Let me know if they say anything important, will you?' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,76</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Flynn, sensing my decision to nick out for a bit of hunting, kept dashing out. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,121</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> nick over to visit, usually without warning; eg `Marge, I'm just going to nick over and see Harry before din-dins, okay?' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,76</i>)

### nick in
 
 <i>v.</i> slot in, mesh (with)

>  <b>PERRY</b> It is a good idea to introduce a fresh strain from time to time to experiment with, and though it may not nick in well enough for the half-[62]strain rams to be used in the flock, the ewes will certainly be of use. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,61</i>)

### nicked
 
 <i>adj.</i> [farming]

>  After the deposit [of fat] had begun, every other part simultaneously receives its share, the back and kidneys receiving the most, the former becoming `nicked', i.e. the fat is felt through the skin to be divided into two portions, from the tail head along the backbone to the top of the shoulder... 39 when you find the rump `nicked' on handling you expect to find fat on the back. <i>(1902,NZ Loan and Merc. Annual Review,38</i>)

### nicker
 
 <i>n.</i> thief

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Hunka who always carried a B.S.A. spanner, the nicker's tool is the best said Hunka, ready to strip from an unguarded bike a dynamo and headlight. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,10</i>)

### nicketty-boo
 
 <i>adj.</i> naked; also express: IN THE NICKETTY-BOO,in the NICK (n1) naked. -->ca1935 Havelock boy's [possibly (cf NICK n1), a rhythmical reformation of naked + -boo suffix (cf ARSY-BOO), forms naked-dy-boo]

### nicky
 
 <i>adj.</i> nice (a reformation of nice) [ca1950. W. Mabbett `Women's slang on higher social levels' rare polite coll]

### NID
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Northern Industrial District

>  The Northern Industrial District Tailors, Cutters, Trimmers, Pressers and Related Trades Union is tackling the NID Combined Apparel and Related Trades Employees Union over what seems to right of clothing workers of join either union. <i>(Jul 28, 1987,Dominion Sunday Times,5</i>)

### nig
 
 <i>n.</i> a small drink, = NIGGLE n [(WW2, rare)]

>  (glossary) Nig: niggle, small drink (very rare) <i>(1946,Johnny Enzed in Italy</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> shortening of NIGGER, usu. as a nickname for a dark person [AND 1880] [ca1940 Havelock ca1951 Wellington bar (Gresham) in address]

### nigau
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [nikau](new-no.md#nikau))

### nigger
 
 <i>n.</i> an abusive and contemptuous term for MAORI relatively rare (at least in written record) since the Land Wars of the 1860's, when it was especially frequent among Taranaki settlers, regimental soldiers (?fresh from India and other colonial venues) In theRichmond-Atkinson Papers (1960), up to 1860, except for one use by Jane Maria (1860 July 171/618) and one by H. Ronalds in his Diary of the Taranaki War (1860 Mar. 9, 1/535), it seems the personal use of A.S. Atkinson all in his private journal and not in his `public' letters. His first use is Aug 18, 1855 (see quote), thence 1856 June 10, with two more `playful' uses of negroes, the first curious (1858 Sep 19, I-429 `Consulted with Maria whether it would be well to go among the Negroes for six months to learn Maori'), the second 1859 Mar 8 I-452 `Attended conference between Negroes and Governor'. [(obsolesc offensive)] (See [also NEGRO](n-new.md#negro); [HORI](hom-hyp.md#hori); [BLACK JOB](b-bla.md#black-job),[BROWN JOB](../B#brown-job))

>  <b>BLACKLEY</b> It was only when a number of new settlersin the district, mostly low class Irish,began to exhibit their inhospitabilitytowards the 'niggers', as they calledthem, that those 'niggers' began to do soalso to the whiteman. <i>(1992,Art of Alfred Sharpe, The,29</i>) [NOTES: From 1888 SHARPE in Newcastle Morning Herald]

>  We heard that Bishop Selwyn (who has been here two or three days) thinksthere is a fair chance of collision with the `niggers'. <i>(Aug 18, 1858,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/175,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: A.S. Atkinson Jrnl]

>  The 40th Band was sent to Waitara ... they meant to charm the niggers <i>Ibid.</i> 7/2 Suppose we were sure that the niggers wouldn't come in day light <i>(1860,Taranaki Punch,V/6</i>)

>  Will speaks of settling at Otago and us all going, to which Arthur elegantly remarks `he'll be blowed if he does' and James agrees with him that migration to an entirely `nigger' settlement would be preferable to living amongst those Scotchmen. <i>(Aug 31, 1860,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/631</i>)

>  The bump of antiniggerism is most consolingly prominent) <i>(1861,Taranaki Punch,2/3</i>)

>  <b>FITZGERALD</b> But you will find in these papers complaints by chiefs that some of the settlers are in the habit of speaking of them as `black niggers' and `bloody Maoris'. But this is only as much to say, that amongst the English settlers there are some ruffians and blackguards. ... <i>(1862,Native Policy of NZ, The,12</i>)

>  <b>CARBERRY</b> It is said that the niggers intend fighting. <i>(Nov 18, 1863,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1863 18 Nov CARBERRY Journal (MS ATB) (MS ATB)]

>  <b>NICHOLL</b> ...the natives were firing at them but they did not return it as they were about 1000 yards from each other. With my glasses I could see one of the niggers walking up and down on the parapet waving his hands about. <i>(1864,Journal,199</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> There can be little doubt that the same cause which rendered our countrymen the objects of native dislike in India also operated here; the Maori is constantly being called a nigger and black fellow to his face, and viewed as an inferior being. 113 ibid. Before [the present Taranaki War] began some of the Europeans felt so confident of their own power to maltreat, that they were neither ashamed nor afraid to express their wish to `polish off the black niggers'. <i>(1868,Past and Present of NZ,111</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... at least not to be such easy pots for the concealed niggers. From the place where we cavalrymen] halted, ... we saw the niggers come outside, and some mounted men riding to and fro. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,130</i>)

>  <b>HOOD</b> The Vulgar Europeans call their tawny skinned neighbours [about the time of the Taranaki Wars] `Niggers', `Savages' and other opprobrious epithets more applicable to themselves. <i>(1875,Chowbokiana,51</i>)

>  <b>WISNIOWSKI</b> Their boys thought they were too good for her [a Maori]. They called her a nigger girl. <i>(1877,Tikera,178,(trans. Podstolski 1972 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SENIOR</b> I met colonists who could not use objurgations too strong against the Maoris - `niggers' as, John Bull fashion, they term them. <i>(1880,Travel & Trout,198</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> ... those of later days, who arrive in the colony and have on their tongues the talk of the `nigger element', and who have no knowledge and no ideas of Maori character, save as gathered fromthese later days, ... <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk3/9/216</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> The boss of the new men call me `nigger' [fn] Maoris dislike the term `nigger' <i>(1895,Ngamiki,140</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Nigger, n. an Australian black or aboriginal. [Of course an incorrect use. He is not a negro, any more than the Hindoo is.] 1874 <i>(1898,Austral English,320</i>)

>  <b>118</b> [Potatau's, the Maori King's] last words to his people were: `Hold fast to love, to law, and to faith;' and from his deathbed he sent a message to his friend Sir William Martin, the former Chief Justice of New Zealand: `Be kind to the niggers.' [? date of death] <i>(1908,New Zealand Revisited</i>)

>  Heavens above, we filch the nigger's land and then we make an alien of him. Why the day will come when the Dago won't be at home here. <i>(Jan 7, 1911,NZ Truth,289/1/5</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> It was a very common thing [c1865] to refer to them as `niggers,' and it is difficult to discover any more opprobrious epithet to apply, for the Maori is not a `nigger' in any sense of the term ... <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,142</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> But the only girl Roddy liked was that Eriti Fleete. A nigger, thought Brian, who had already absorbed Auckland's notion of Maoris. <i>(1938,Promenade,166</i>)

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> Skin colour and the mixture of blood which is responsible for it [sc. high visibility in a white world] are often commented on by one Maori to another. The terms `mongrel', `black mongrel', `dago', or `black nigger', for instance, are often used by Maoris either in referring to themselves or to other Maoris in the neighbourhood. Thus one informant, in introducing herself, remarked: `I'm only a mongrel' - meaning only a mixed-blood Maori. <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,299</i>)

>  Later a girl by the campfire said `why don't you niggers do a haka and ....[sic] off.' <i>(Mar 28, 1984,Dominion,7</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> (Marama, Kaikoura) Or when we were playing around having a fight down the back, someone would say, `Go away, nigger.' ... Then there'd be a fight. Same thing when the towneyites - the kids from up Kaikoura - came down here. <i>(1985,I'm Ninety-five,98</i>)

>  <b>EDWARDS</b> Pakeha people will start calling me"black nigger" again. <i>(1992,Mihipeka,115</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [(prison slang, Paremoremo) ca1984 Paremoremo Prison (C.B.Orsman p.c.)]

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> In those days racism was more blatant than it is now. ... I had become accustomed to being addressed as `snow', `darky', and even `Hori', often by adults who would have been surprised had they known they were giving offence. And at high school Stuart was known as Sam (short for Sambo). But `nigger' was the ultimate, the unforgivable epithet. <i>(1984,Island to Island,93</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [equipment]

>  The "nigger", as it is called, disappears again into the floor. <i>(1944,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),2(9) : 26</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> an offensive term of abuse for a Polynesian (See [cf coconut](chi-con.md#coconut))



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> (adolescents') as a term of address for an equal [1953 17-18 M A35 Thames DHS 11 NIGGER [M3] -->1953 17 M A38 Thames DHS 11 NIGGER -->1953 18 M A40 Thames DHS 11 NIGGER]

### nigger wig
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [niggerhead](new-no.md#niggerhead))

>  <b>MEREDEITH</b> ... the [Otago] country I had to travel was intersected in all directions with `nigger wig' swamps ... quite impassable for a horse. <i>(1898,Reminiscences and Experiences,20</i>)

### nigger-brown
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  She sometimes wears a high-crowned nigger-brown hat swathed with turquoise ribbon. <i>(Sep 24, 1947,NZ Observer,23</i>)

### niggerhead
 
 <i>n.</i> sticks (? or figs) of coarse, dark `plug' or `twist'. spg also nigger head [? from brand name`negrohead', and/or having a negro's head as the trademark] [(obs)]

>  6 lb N Hd 6lb Cavendish 1 doz Pipes <i>(Apr 1839,Piraki Log,80</i>) [NOTES: Station Stocklist]

>  <b>E. J. WAKEFIELD</b> niggerhead (<i>passim</i>) <i>(Mar 30, 1840,Diary</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> Luckily I had two or three pieces of `nigger-head' tobacco (Barrett's twist) in my pocket, and we chewed this as we sat crouching back to back ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,76</i>)

>  <i>Niggerhead.</i>- (1) ... (2)The name of the tobacco usually kept in station stores for the men in the eighties. (C.) <i>(Nov 11, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>PASCOE</b> In the middle nineties [Douglas] smoked `niggerhead', a tobacco of two dozen thin sticks to the pound. <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,70</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (?rare) <i>Mariscus ustulatus </i> [ca1957 Nov. (R. Mason) `heard for the first time']



 
 <i>n.</i> a hard black-stone

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Nigger-head, n.</i> (1) Name given in New Zealand to hard black-stones found at the Blue Spur and other mining districts. They are prized for their effectiveness in aiding cement-washing. The name is applied in America to a round piece of basic igneous rock. -->(2) Name used in Queensland for blocks of coral above water. -->OED 1876 (New Guinea) black points of coral rock. 1877, 1886 a) black concretionary nodules formed in granite b) any hard dark-coloured rock weathering into ... [rounded] boulders; 1898 Morris <i>(1898,Austral English,321</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a swamp tuft-grass whose raised clumps turn black when dry. spg also nigger(-)head 1. the blackened tussocks of swamp-sedges of Carex spp., esp. Carex secta and C. virgata [are these synonyms?], (cf MAORIHEAD, NEGROHEAD) 1882 Poo 1900 DCO (C. virgata) 10 entries (C. secta) 1906 LB 1924 CCN. (Andersen) (See [Maorihead](m-mar.md#maorihead))

>  On the way they had to cross a gully five or six feet deep and about fifteen or twenty feet wide which was filled with nigger-heads. <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,150</i>) [NOTES: Writing of 1940]

>  We had a bacon house, where mum used to cure her bacon and dad used to smoke it, by burning niggerheads. <i>(1959,Women of Westland,32</i>) [NOTES: 2nd edition]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> The edges of streams on the tussock-clad plains are often fringed with flax and bulrush, whilst, in the water itself, stand numbers of blackened stumps about two feet high, bearing on their summits drooping brushes of long, coarse, green, or tawny threads. These are termed by the colonists, nigger-heads. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,5</i>) [NOTES: [check 1907]]

>  <b>BAKER</b> Maorihead for <i>Carex virgata, Niggerhead</i> for <i>Carex secta</i>. [check] <i>(1941,NZ Slang,45</i>)

>  <i>nigger-head:</i> sedge grass. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <b>SHAW & FARRANT</b> When the first settlers made their way over from Dunedin to take up the rural sections marked out by Mr Kettle's survey they were confronted with a land of swamps and winding canals, with giant `nigger-heads' showing above the water. <i>(1949,Taieri Plain,2</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> Nigger head <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,88 [Table 1],1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> The numerous wet places [at Waikaka] were crowded with interesting plants ... sedge, or cuttygrass, niggerheads, wiwi or rush, and epilobium or willow herb with its beautiful white flowers. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,239</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> These massive sedges best known as NIGGER-HEADS (<i>Carex secta, C. virgata</i>, and <i>C.appressa</i>) are very similar in general appearance. <i>(1929,Flora NZ,133,1929 ed.</i>)

>  <i>nigger-head</i>: The name used in New Zealand for a large kind of swamp grass growing in wet places; in Queensland for blocks of coral above water. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),275,8th ed.</i>)

>  <i>nigger-head</i> n. New Zealand name for certain hard black stones; above water blocks of Barrier Reef coral. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),621</i>)

>  <i>nigger-head</i> n. in Australia, stick of strong popular bush tobacco; in N.Z., clumps of coarse native grass or sedge in swamps, often on burnt tree stumps. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),14</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The land of the magpie and the myna, the pukeko scuttling into raupo and niggerhead, the plantations regimented across the landscape. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,20</i>)

>  <i>niggerhead [=] cyperus ustulatus [s] </i>... <i>niggerhead [s] Carex secta</i> Boott <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,53</i>)

>  <b>McNEISH</b> He crossed swamps, jumping from nigger-head to nigger-head. <i>(1970,Mackenzie,67</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Mata [a12-macrons] ... 2. <i>Carex diandra</i> and <i>C. secta</i>, niggerhead; swamp plants. <i>Ibid.</i> 308 <i>Pu[u]kio</i>, n. Carex diandra, niggerhead; a swamp plant which grows in raised tufts. <i>(1971,Dictionary of the Maori Language,185</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Pukio</i>, n.<i> Carex diandra, niggerhead</i>; a swamp plant that grows in raised tufts. <i>(1975,Dictionary of the Maori Language,308,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Carex secta</i> NIGGERHEAD <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,366</i>)

>  <i>niggerhead n. NZ </i>tree-fern; matted clump of swamp tussock-grass <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,506</i>) [NOTES: nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]

>  <b>WARR</b> Flax, niggerheads, toitoi and rushes grew in abundance in all swampy areas ... <i>(1988,From Bush-Burn to Butter,49</i>)

>  He has chanced to rest.. by a shining currentless creek, rising in a swamp and draining a clump of giant "niggerheads". <i>(Nov 11, 1897,Otago Witness,49</i>)

>  <b>BIDWILL & WOODHOUSE</b> The survey of the town [Dunedin] had not been completed [sc. in 1849], but the area had been roughly defined, and was covered with a rank growth of grass, flax, scrub, toi toi, nigger heads and spear grass. <i>(1927,Bidwill of Pihautea,61</i>)

>  <b>EASTWOOD</b> We had a bacon house, where mum used to cure her bacon and dad used to smoke it, by burning niggerheads (tussock) and he used to oil his oilskins and hang them up in the smoke house to drain and dry after the bacon had been transferred to the kitchen. <i>(1982,Brief History of Gold Mining, A,108</i>)

>  Untidy pincushions of niggerheads dot the lagoons. <i>(1987,Country Calendar (TVNZ),29</i>)

>  Trenching in the swamp and cutting nigger heads to fill up a bog on the track in the morning. <i>(Aug 27, 1864,Chudleigh Diary,143,1950 ed.</i>)

>  Swamps are frequent ... fringed by Raupo ... forming a bottom for many others, such as ... carex virgata (Nigger-head of the settlers) ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/26</i>) [NOTES: Buchanan Botany Otago]

>  Carex virgata (Niggerhead of the settlers) <i>(1868,TrNZI,28</i>) [NOTES: Buchanan Botany Otago]

>  <b>JOURNAL</b> Some [of the land] was very wet, and we had to step from one `nigger head' to another. <i>(1868,Saved from Shipwreck,27</i>)

>  <b>HAWKSWOOD</b> `Nigger Head' .... is a species of cabbage plant, which, as it withers, turns black, leaving a round,stumpy head and long dropping leaves. <i>(1880,Reminiscences of NZ,12</i>)

>  <i>Carex virgata</i> nigger-head <i>(1882,Out in the Open</i>) [NOTES: And]

>  ... the vegetation may be observed in thickets of tea-tree [fn <i>Leptospermum</i>], gigantic clumps of the tall flax-lily [fn <i>Phormium tenax</i>] ... and that curious sedge known popularly as nigger-head [fn <i>Carex vergata</i>] ... <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,179</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>Carex secta</i> nigger-head <i>(1906,NZ Flora</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Carex secta</i> niggerhead <i>(1907,Plants of NZ</i>) [NOTES: And.]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Carex secta</i> niggerhead <i>(1907,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: And.Report on a Botanical Survey of Kapiti Island]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Carex secta</i> niggerhead <i>(1909,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: Report on a Botanical Survey of Stewart Island And.]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Carex secta</i> niggerhead <i>(1910,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>) [NOTES: And.]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Carex secta</i> niggerhead <i>(1911,AJHR,C-13</i>) [NOTES: Report on the Dune-areas of NZAnd.]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> Very characteristic [of swamp vegetation] is the niggerhead (<i>Carex secta</i>), a species of sedge which builds for itself tall and stout `trunks' out of its dead roots and root-stocks, from the summit of which, like shock-heads of hair, the long leaves droop. <i>(1910,NZ Plants,109</i>)

>  <i>Carex secta</i> niggerhead <i>(1916,TrNZI,48</i>)

>  <i>Carex secta</i> nigger head <i>(1916,Dunedin Field Club Plant Catalogues</i>) [NOTES: And.]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Carex secta</i> niggerhead <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story,2nd ed.</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Carex secta</i> niggerhead <i>(1924,Cultivation of NZ Plants, The</i>) [NOTES: And.]

>  <i>`Pukio</i> was the Maori name of `niggerheads'.... <i>(1921,TrNZI,52/72</i>) [NOTES: (Beattie)]

>  Various grass-like sedges occupy the moist ground ... and these include the niggerhead (Carex secta), cutting-grass (Mariscus ustulatus) ... <i>(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,8/203</i>)

>  <i>Niggerhead.</i?- (1) Carex secta. A plant which grows up like a tussock out of the water in swamps and at the edges of lagoons. When it is burnt and goes black it is more or less the shape of a head; hence the name. (C.) (2)... (C.) <i>(Nov 11, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

 <i>n.</i> prunes and rice [(boarding-Schoolboys') 1925 Kings College, Auckland (R. Mason) cf P. - snowstorm Curry and rice; stewed prunes and rice: naval: late 19-20c. Bowen. P. sup: - snow is the usual post - 1920 form of the `prunes and rice' sense of the prec. Granville.]

### niggle
 
 <i>n.</i> also pl. a small drink (cf NIG)(also NIGGLER (nf in this sense P8), NIGGLY (P8 bad tempered from 1910), variant of OED niggling [check]) [WW2]

### night
 
 <i>n.</i> winter in the Antarctic

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> "Who are the chaps who stay over through the winter - your winter population here?" "Well, there are usually about fourteen staying put during the night." <i>(1962,One Foot at the Pole,149</i>)

### night barker
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep dog that persistently requires attention or incites other dogs at night by barking.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> night barker: Pretty obvious term for a dog with that bad habit. It invariably disturbs other dogs which also start up, and in next to no time a whole district is reverberating to a distressing chorus. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,ii 2</i>)

### night camp
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a place to where sheep rise during the evening.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> camp: sheep will settle down for the night in groups and always in the same spot. This night camp is easily noticed by day as the ground is covered only by plants such as the horehound which will tolerate very rich soil. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,15</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> This avoids the extreme heat and catches sheep just up from their night camp, when they travel better because their bellies are empty. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,44</i>)

### night demon
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Majaqueus parkinsoni</i> Bray, BLACK PETREL (See [hakuwai](h-hea.md#hakuwai))

>  <i>Majaqueus parkinsoni</i> Gray. (Black petrel.) ... I am quite satisfied that this bird is the `Night Demon' of our diggers. I had a live one ... from Collingwood ... On windy nights the bird would become very excited, and then it would give vent to the hysterical laugh or scream from which it takes its name. <i>(1895,TrNZI,27/122</i>) [NOTES: Buller]

### Night Limited
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The introduction of the Limited train between Auckland and Wellington made an immediate appeal to the popular imagination... The introduction of a summer running train, which took advantage of the daylight, was also 'limited in its accommodation'... It brought about a desire for more specific titles, so "Daylight Limited" and "Night Limited" became the terms commonly used in referring to them. <i>(Jul 18, 1928,NZ Free Lance,35</i>)

### night paddock
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [stud farming] a paddock where stud mares, ewes or cows are kept before and immediately after parturition.

>  <b>TAIT</b> From 1930, the practice of using night paddocks for the herd was discontinued. <i>(1958,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol II,495</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) an accommodation paddock for stock in droves, [usually overnight or for short periods.]

>  <b>PULLEN</b> We were given this great night paddock full of grass. <i>(1996,On the Road Again,31</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> It was bright enough to have picked up a bridle and gone looking for a horse in the night paddock on a station that still relied on them. <i>(1993,Station Country I,54</i>)

>  <b>PULLEN</b> They [the cattle] must have been getting enough [feed] to keep them contented though, as they would camp as soon as they got to their night paddocks each evening. <i>(1996,On the Road Again,17</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [dairying] a paddock where cows are kept prior to morning milking.

>  <b>DARE</b> The night paddock was land surrounding the house and easily accessible ... <i>(1956,Rouseabout Jane,122</i>)

>  The paddock is now used as a night paddock for 18 cows.. <i>(Oct 30, 1926,NZ Dairy Exporter,56</i>)

>  <b>STAPLEDON</b> On an appreciable number of farms the system of the 'night paddock' is largely adapted. This consists of always bringing the dairy cows to a a particular paddock at night. The 'night paddock' will be rotated round the farm just as the dairy beasts themselves. <i>(1928,Tour in Australia and NZ,55</i>)

>  To avoid trouble of this kind cows should be prevented from wading in stagnant water and kept out of badly fouled night paddocks. <i>(May 20, 1933,NZ Journal of Agriculture,330</i>)

>  The cows have three or four days in each paddock, with a separate night paddock. The night paddocks are also grazed in rotation. <i>(Aug 1, 1936,NZ Dairy Exporter,44</i>)

>  In the case of night paddocks, it is useful to remember that cows do not eat much after midnight. <i>(Jun 1, 1946,NZ Dairy Exporter,13</i>)

>  To save time and work getting in the cows in the morning, a farmer will frequently use his paddocks handy to the cowshed as night paddocks .. . <i>(Oct 1, 1949,NZ Dairy Exporter,75</i>)

>  It may be difficult to avoid day and night paddocks on a farm broken by deep gullies or awkward terrain. <i>(Jun 1982,NZ Dairy Exporter,2</i>)

>  <b>STEWART</b> We were genuinely able to be of some usein the cowshed - helping Dick to bring upthe herd.. from the night paddock,hustling them into the shed, leg-ropingthem.. attaching the machine's strangecups to their fleshy teats. <i>(1983,Springtime in Taranaki,116</i>)

### night parrot
 
 <i>n.</i> indigenous parrot, the kakapo [aka kakapo]

>  The kakapo, a night parrot (<i>Strigops habroptilus</i>), is a kind of yellowish green owl, also making its nest in a hole in the ground, and coming out at night. <i>(Apr 23, 1870,Otago Witness,9</i>)

>  Another writer added: "in Otago during the construction of a certain road, the Night Parrot was slaughtered in great numbers, one bird making 'a plenteous meal for two men'." <i>(Mar 2002,NZ Geographic,56/24</i>)

>  ka[a]ka[a]po[o], a bird (Strigops habroptilus), ground-parrot; green parrot; night-parrot; once common in both islands, now confined to rugged south-west mountains of South Island (po, night, whence night-parrot). <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/143</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English In O.E.D., but no macrons.]

>  The destruction of the bush, the introduction of stoats, weasels and ferrets, the increase of wild cats, the importation of European birds and the consequent greater demand made on the supply of bird-food, are all blamed, and no doubt those remarkable birds, the kakapo (or night parrot), the kiwi and the wood hen (weka) will fall an easy prey to stoats and weasels, more especially the two former, by reason of their sleeping in holes and hollow logs in the day time. <i>(Mar 27, 1897,Star, The,6</i>)

>  There is a small lake named kakapo (the night parrot, stringops) on the river, which runs into Lake Poteretere from the north. <i>(Oct 13, 1909,Otago Witness,63</i>)

>  [The Maori] ... said that formerly he had often been there to catch kakapos (green night parrots) in a black birch wood above the Awatere pass. <i>(Feb 21, 1851,NZ Government Gazette,31</i>) [NOTES: 1851 Jan Weld Letter in 1851 Feb 21 NZGG 31]

>  <i>ka[a]ka[a]po[o]</i>, a bird (Strigops habroptilus), ground-parrot; green parrot; night-parrot; once common in both islands, now confined to rugged south-west mountains of South Island (po, night, whence night-parrot). <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/143</i>) [NOTES: In O.E.D., but no macrons.]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Night parrots are almost extinct. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/28</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> We were separated from the whole world, our only neighbour the abnormal night parrot, whose shrill cry enlivened the dismal nights ... <i>(1948,Julius Von Haast,282</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> During the night we heard for the first time the cry of the kakapo, and of the large kiwi, ... I was much interested here on observing the tracks of numerous kakapos, or night parrots, ... <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,57</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> ... the range ... had in old times had been a well-known hunting-ground for the kakapo (night-parrot or ground-parrot) ... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/459</i>)

>  <i>night parrot n 1</i> : KAKAPO <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1527</i>)

>  46. <i>Stringops habroptilus,</i> Gray. Ground parrot, Night par8rot, Kakapo. - It is still abundant in many parts of Westland; notwithstanding the persecution that constantly pursues it for the sake of supplying the requirements of museums and collectors. <i>(Jul 1885,NZ Journal of Science,479</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <i>Kakapo Strigops habroptilus</i> GRAY, <i>1845 ... OTHER NAMES</i>: Tarapo, tarepo, owl <i>parrot, ground parrot, night parrot.</i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,242</i>)

### night pen
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [joc.] a hotel room or bar facilities for use when wet weather postpones shearing or mustering.

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> Country hotels often keep a night pen for wet day shearers, so do they keep some wild sorts of sheep for wide running dogs ... [night-pen; nightpen] <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,61</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a sheltered pen (often in shearing shed) where sheep can be kept the night before shearing [(station)]

>  From each side of a central building.. are the night pens and shearing floors. <i>(Oct 12, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> [The fleeces] was all here on top of the nightpen rail. <i>(1907,Return of Joe,305</i>)

>  <b>REES</b> [A nightpen] is inside the [shearing] shed. The sheep are kept in there ready for the morning's shearing. [check this quotation] <i>(1928,Wild, Wild Heart,72</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> ... they [the tally pens] can be used as a night pen. No actual night pen is lost by this, ... . Except in dire circumstances it is not good practice to fill the catching pens for use as night pens, ... <i>(1955,Wool Away!,112</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> The woolshed was ... enormous with its four-stand shearing plant, large wool room and [73] storage space for about 200 bales, and a huge night pen to cover over 1,500 sheep. <i>Ibid.</i> 130 The drafting yards and dagging pens were concreted and all under cover, so they could also be used as night pens. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,72</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a covered yard for retaining of dry sheep prior to shearing or a small area in woolshed or under cover to house stud stock during lambing or calving.

>  <b>TAIT</b> The old slab-built shed was therefore dismantled, the iron being used to enlarge the night pen of the new shed. <i>(1958,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol II,347</i>)

>  There are two shearing boards, one running down each side of the night pens; ten men shear on each side. <i>(Oct 1891,NZ Farmer,418</i>)

>  The night pen should be yarded warily in the afternoon. <i>(Oct 1918,NZ Farmer,1243</i>)

>  <b>MCKENNY</b> Don't put daggy sheep into the night pen before shearing; let them stand in the yard before shedding and save discomfort for the shearer. <i>(1927,Sheep-Farming, Fencing etc.,104</i>)

>  The building is conventional in floor layout with the woolroom and catching pens elevated to give headroom to tally-pens and night-pens underneath. <i>(May 8, 1969,NZ Farmer,2</i>)

>  The shed has also increased the farm's night pen space from 700 to 1600 ewes ... <i>(2002,Dominion Post,C10</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  He sleeps out when his two-week night shelter pass runs out. <i>(Feb 14, 1988,Dominion Sunday Times,19</i>)

>  The [Collins English] dictionary does not, however, contain entries for DPB, EFTPOS, GST, CNG, first fifteen, street kids, May holidays, and it is still too early for orange roughy, night shelter, and Rogernomics, twink and lotto (New Zealand-style) ... <i>(Feb 27, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),65</i>) [NOTES: G. Kennedy]

 <i>n.</i> a fish

[ed. fn The Goat Fish, <i>Upeneichthys kumu.</i>] <i>(1777,Cook's Journals,3/808,1967 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb Anderson's Journal in Cook Journals (1967) 3/808]

 <i>n.</i>

### night-cart
 
 <i>n.</i> a sanitary cart -->get cits from rejectsTruck that called night to empty long drop toilet receptacles. [(obs)]

>  We had moved on into a flat with the inevitable Ngaruawahia "night cart" plumbing, except that the flat was a few yards beyond Borough limits, so the cart didn't call. <i>(Mar 1986,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,75/41</i>)

>  <b>LANGDALE-HUNT</b> There were no flush toilets, only the `Old Dunny' in the backyard, both in town and country with the only difference that a vehicle called a night cart called in town in latter years, ... <i>(1985,Last Entail Male,65</i>)

>  Kainga [in North Canterbury] is the last place in New Zealand to have sewerage removed by night cart. <i>(Sep 4, 1986,Evening Post,9</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [(busdrivers' humorous) -->1953 (Lower Hutt busdrivers', but not used by Wgton) `The last bus which collects drivers & takes them home.']

### night-hawk
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Ninox novaeseelandiae</i>, =MORE-PORK

>  <b>MONEY</b> I struck up [a song] .... electrifying the moreporks or `night-hawks' in the neighbourhood. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,44</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Two loud harsh notes assail her ear -/ The <i>night-hawk's!</i> harsh but yet so <i>near! </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 1/305 [Get quote] <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/247</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a morepork or ruru.

>  It is not generally known that the night hawk (mopoke or ruru) will attack birds as large as a starling. <i>(Dec 13, 1918,Hawera Star,2</i>)

 <i>n.</i> the job of filling supermarket shelves at night [Internet search Feb 2006: also used in Austral; one citation found for Canada.] (See [dayfill](d-dif.md#dayfill))

 <i>n.</i>

### nightshade
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>nightshade, white-edged [s] Solanum marginatum</i> L.f. <i>Ibid.</i> 85 <i>white-edged nightshade [s] Solanum marginatum</i> L.f. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,53</i>)

>  <b>HILL</b> Boxthorn and barberry hedges grow only in rural sectors of Moaville. In the town ... they're classified as noxious plants, along with ... ragwort and woolly nightshade. <i>(1988,More from Moaville,55</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> WOOLLY NIGHTSHADE (<i>Solanum mauritianum</i>) ... introduced from South America ... a serious weed mainly in North Auckland. ... <i>(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,175</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>nightshade, yellow-berried [s] Solanum nigrum</i> L. var. <i>angustifolium</i> Fil. <i>Ibid.</i> 89 <i>yellow-berried nightshade [s] Solanum nigrum</i> L. var. <i>angustifolium</i> Fil. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,53</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>nightshade, red-berried [s] Solanum nigrum</i> L. var. <i>puniceum</i> Gmel. <i>Ibid.</i> 63 <i>red-berried nightshade [s] Solanum nigrum</i> L. var. <i>puniceum</i> Gmel. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,53</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>nightshade, small-flowered [s] Solanum nodiflorum</i> Jacq. <i>Ibid.</i> 70 ;<i>small-flowered nightshade [s] Solanum nodiflorum</i> Jacq. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,53</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> ... the tree night-shade (<i>Solanum nigrum</i>) grows in wild luxuriance, its pale blue bells having a pretty effect against the sombre foliage. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/235</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>nightshade, velvety [s] Solanum gracilius</i> Herter <i>Ibid.</i> 82 <i>velvety nightshade [s] Solanum gracilius</i> Herter <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,53</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> VELVETY NIGHTSHADE (<i>Solanum sublobatum</i> syn. <i>gracilius</i>) Perennial with some likeness to black nightshade. ... This South American plant grows abundantly in many places along the West Coast of the South Island. ... <i>(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,157</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> = black nightshade

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> BLACK NIGHTSHADE (<i>Solanum nigrum</i>), is called also white nightshade, mother-of-thousands, black tomato, black potato, wonder berry, and by a serious confusion deadly nightshade and bittersweet. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,152,1967 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> any of a large group of plants and shrubs related to the potato and poroporo, the berries of some of which, especially the potato, belladonna or deadly nightshade, are poisonous. See also BLACK NIGHTSHADE.

>  <b>BANKS</b> <i>(1770,Banks Journal</i>) [NOTES: see sowthistle]

>  (Queen Charlotte Sound) Amongst the known kinds of plants are common and rough Bindweed, [ed. fn 4 Probably <i>Calystegia sepium</i> and the smaller <i>C. turguriorum</i>.] Nightshade [author's fnd Solanum Linnaei. [ed. note [Probably <i>Solanum aviculare</i>, described by Forster from Queen Charlotte Sound: ...]]] and Nettles, [fne Urtica [ed. [<i>Urtica ferox</i>]]] both which grow to the size of small trees; a shruby [sic] Speedwell found near all the Beeches, Sow thistles, [805] Virgins Bower, ... <i>(Feb 1777,Cook's Journals,3/804,1967 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb Anderson's Journal in Cook Journals (1967]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Nightshade. <i>Solanum nigrum</i>. <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  Nightshade. <i>Solanum nigrum</i>. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  (Queen Charlotte Sound) Amongst the known kinds of plants are common and roughBindweed, Nightshade [author's fnd Solanum Linnaei. [ed. note [Probably <i>Solanum aviculare</i>, described by Forster from Queen Charlotte Sound: ...]]] and Nettles, ... American night shade, [fnf Phytolacca. [ed. [Phytolacca in New Zealand is an introduced weed. If Anderson's Nightshade, note a above, is <i>Solanum aviculare</i>, this `American night shade'is perhaps <i>S. nodiflorum</i>.]]] Knotgrass, [fng Polygonum. [ed. [<i>Polygonum aviculare</i>.]]] ... <i>(Feb 1777,Cook's Journals,3/804,1967 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb Anderson's Journal in Cook Journals]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> bittersweet, black potato, black tomato, nightshade, mother-of-thousands, white nightshade, wonder berry; (mistakenly) deadly nightshade

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Solanum nigrum</i> black nightshade <i>(1907,Plants of NZ</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> BLACK NIGHTSHADE (<i>Solanum nigrum</i>), is called also white nightshade, mother-of-thousands, black tomato, black potato, wonder berry, and by a serious confusion deadly nightshade and bittersweet. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,152,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Solanum nigrum (The Black Nightshade)</i>. A common weed. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,389</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> English names of animals and plants are sometimes mistakenly applied. The great buttercup of the alps, <i>Ranunculus lyalli</i>, is called the mountain lily; the indigenous black nightshade,<i>Solanum nigrum</i> is often called deadly nightshade which is a very different species. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/680</i>) [NOTES: Wall NZ Speech]

>  <i>black nightshade [s] Solanum nigrum</i> L. <i>Ibid.</i> 53 <i>nightshade, black [s] Solanum nigrum</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,5</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> BLACK NIGHTSHADE (<i>Solanum nigrum</i>) ... has many different forms which are regarded as variants of one species ... [It] is common throughout New Zealand but less abundant in the southern half of the South Island. <i>(1980,Weeds of Crops,16</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>climbing nightshade [=] bittersweet [s]</i> <i>Ibid.</i> 53 <i>nightshade, climbing [=] bittersweet [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,14</i>)



 
<b>5</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BENNETT</b> <i>Solanum aviculare</i> cut-leaved nightshade <i>(1860,Gatherings of a Naturalist</i>)



 
<b>6</b> <i>n.</i> a. mistakenly =black nightshade usually applied to <i>Solanum</i> sp(p), BLACK NIGHTSHADE, but in Rotorua-Taupo district it is sometimes applied to <i>Leycesteria formosa </i> [1957 R. Mason: used by the then Wildlife Branch, Internal Affairs Dept]

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> BLACK NIGHTSHADE (<i>Solanum nigrum</i>), is called also white nightshade, mother-of-thousands, black tomato, black potato, wonder berry, and by a serious confusion deadly nightshade and bittersweet. Increasingly common from Auckland southward to Otago. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,152,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> English names of animals and plants are sometimes mistakenly applied. the great buttercup of the alps, <i>Ranunculus lyalli</i>, is called the mountain lily; the indigenous black nightshade, <i>Solanum nigrum</i> is often called deadly nightshade which is a very different species. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/680</i>) [NOTES: Wall NZ Speech]

### nikahere
 
 <i>n.</i> tobacco

>  Their names for the brands of tobacco ... are purupuru, pongi, and parehe, not to mention nikahere (negrohead). <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/87</i>)

### nikau
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> One half of the long native house had been prepared for the reception of the females ... it was neatly filled with the <i>nikau</i> and made very comfortable. <i>(Nov 8, 1827,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,83,1961 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a native palm tree with a tall unbranched trunk and a crown of fan-like leaves with very broad leaf-bases. [Maori nikau[i-macron]] 1.<i> Rhopalostylis</i> (formerly <i>Areca) Sapida</i>, the NEW ZEALAND PALM, NIKAU-PALM, the edible flesh of its shoots, or its leaf or leaves [>1843. `An Ordinance for imposing a tax on Raupo Houses, Session II. No. xvii. of the former legislative Council of New Zealand': -->[From A. Domett's collection of Ordinances 1850.] `Section 2,... there shall be levied in respect of every building constructed wholly or in part of raupo-->, nikau-->, toitoi-->, wiwi-->, kakaho-->, straw or thach of any description 20 [pounds].']

>  <b>HUNT</b> You could also make great pickles out of nikau. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,130</i>)

>  In <i>The Natural World of the Maori</i>, ethnologist Margaret Orbell states that the word "nikau" is derived from the Tahitian word for the leaf of the coconut palm which the nikau closely resembles - sans nuts. <i>(Sep 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 65/79</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> N|Ikau, s. A shrub, so called from its numerous leaves proceeding from the same stalk. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,185</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> One half of a long native house ... was neatly filled up with <i>nikau</i> and made very comfortable [sc nikau leaf] <i>(Nov 8, 1827,Journal,83,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> we rested at ten in a little shed constructed of palm leaves (the nigau) where the Waimate boys had passed the preceding night <i>Ibid.</i> 2/179 ... nigau ... <i>(Nov 13, 1839,Journal,2/167</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Another tree called the Nikau is said to be very good; I have had a little bit and found it very nice, but was told that what I had was of a very inferior quality. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,20</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Chapman in 1982 Burton Two Hundred Years of NZ Food 20]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> [The house was] covered with a thick coating of the nikau (a kind of palm) <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/270</i>)

>  <b>MACKIE</b> I cut a beautiful leaf of `nikau', areca sapida, from 9 to 12 ft. long ... <i>(Jul 23, 1853,Traveller under Concern,124,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> <i>Areca sapida</i>, Soland. ... Nat. name, `Nikau', <i>Colenso. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 262</i>)

>  <b>LUSH</b> [He] said he would soon get me some luncheon ... We speedily spied a young <i>Nikau</i>, the native palm tree, and cutting off the upper end he cut the inside ... It tasted like sweet turnip. <i>(Feb 4, 1854,Auckland Journals,150,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GOLDER</b> note The <i>necko</i> or <i>neko</i> is a large tree-like plant known elsewhere as the mountain cabbage. <i>(1854,Pigeon's Parliament,75</i>)

>  You have to make yourself a small hut of poles and thatch it with nikau (the N.Z. palm) ... <i>(Nov 10, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/157</i>) [NOTES: H.A. Atkinson]

>  There she lived for six weeks on Nicau and now and then a few potatoes, got at the risk of her life. <i>(1864,Von Tempsky Memoranda,25</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Nikau, <i>Col[enso]. Areca sapida. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  The young inner blanched leaves and heart of the Ti, or `Cabbage-tree' (<i>Cordyline australis</i>), and of the Nikau, or New Zealand Palm, (<i>Areca sapida</i>), were eaten both raw and cooked. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/32</i>) [NOTES: Colenso Botany North Isd]

>  <b>MONEY</b> We found a good quantity of bush plant called `nikou,' the pith of which is much like hazel nut, and is very good eating when roasted or boiled. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,61</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> The ground was covered with ... young seedling palms, `Nikou'. [sic] [149] ... Perhaps Nikau palms are the most beautiful specimens ... in the forests of New Zealand. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,148</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Savoury Palm-tree (Nikau) ..... O. Palmeae; G.Areca; S. A. sapida. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,2/336</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Nikau</i>... Maori name for a New Zealand palm-tree, <i>Areca sapida</i>, N.O. Palmeae. <i>Spelt also</i> Necho <i>and</i> Neko. <i>(1898,Austral English,321</i>)

>  Even the semi-tropical true pepper [fn <i>Piper excelsum</i>.] disputes with the false native pepper [fn <i>Drimys colorata</i>.] the right to its name, and yet another semi-tropical plant, the nikau [fn <i>Areca sapida</i>.] - New Zealand's only palm - extends as far south as Banks Peninsula, though not found on the Port Hills. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,184</i>)

>  The nikau, or southern palm, is plentiful all through the district, and is everywhere accompanied by noble tree-ferns and palm-lilies. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,116</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> For in their season he could supplement his dry fare with berries and nuts, with the tender bulbous shoots of the tii (cabbage tree), at heart white and delicate, baked in a haangi; the tender leaves of the nikau - that part between the last ring and where the leaf stems separate, below which there is a huge bulge, clasped firmly, clasp on clasp, with the grip of steel bands. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,16</i>)

>  <i>nikau</i>, a palm (Rhopalostylis sapida), the one true palm of New Zealand. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/154</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  nikau The Maori name of a handsome New Zealand palm, the `nikau palm'. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),275,8th ed. ed.</i>)

>  ~ n. New Zealand palm-tree [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),621</i>)

>  <i>nikau</i> ... n. N.Z. palm-tree [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),14</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> nikau: a palm, whose leaves can be used to thatch roofs or decorate dance-halls. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,141</i>) [NOTES: Pearson's Glossary]

>  <i>nikau n.</i> native NZ palm. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,507</i>) [NOTES: nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 328]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He.. took up his residence in a nikau whare with a clay floor. <i>(Sep 21, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,12</i>)

>  It was a wing of the nikau "homestead" of Alf. Woodham <i>(Sep 21, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,12</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> as an attrib or in combin. NIKAU LEAF, HUT, TREE

>  <b>POLACK</b> Some remains of sheds, made of <i>nikau</i>, or palm leaves, indicated that some travellers had put up at this place previously to our arrival. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/69</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> In the evening it came on very raining, we have encamped by a river in a nikau hut. <i>(Jan 8, 1840,Journal,2/178</i>)

>  <b>BODELL</b> We made a Miamia (a small house) composed of sticks tied together and the leaves of the NikauTree. <i>(1890,Soldier's View of Empire, A,171,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CARBERRY</b> Curious customs in Maori Warfare ... she fled into the Bush when she heard our soldiers ransacking the whares and lived there for six weeks her only food being the pith of the Nikau Tree ... <i>(1865,Journal</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  ...we set to work and built a nikau shelter and got a fire going in the bed of the creek... <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/24</i>) [NOTES: 1932 Journal of W M Hamilton in NZ Geographic]

>  Nikau fronds make an ideal impromptu shelter.. but the palm was important to Maori not just for thatching but also for weaving and as a food source: the young flower stalks and heart ("millionaire's salad") were the parts most commonly eaten. Even so, the first Polynesian arrivals to Aotearoa were no doubt nonplussed that the local palm did not bear edible fruit like the cocnut. Indeed, one explanation for the origin of the name "nikau" is that it means "no nuts." <i>(Sep 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 65/85</i>)

>  <b>HAWES</b> He'd learnt from his mother who'd learnt from her mother, back in the days when nikau jam was legal. <i>(2000,Dream of Nikau Jam, The,18</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Rhopalostylis Cheesemanii </i> <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>)

### nikau heart
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BURTON</b> Succulent and sweet to the taste, nikau heart later became known as `millionaire's salad' because the tree dies when the heart is removed. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,7</i>)

>  Hungry children went to the bush and got the heart of nikau, a good food. <i>(Sep 21, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,12</i>)

### nikau hut
 
 <i>n.</i> a rough or makeshift hut made of (or thatched with) nikau leaves.

>  <b>TINNE</b> Our nearest neighbours are a colony of gum-diggers, at the Kaiwaka swamp ...; they have built quite a little village of these `nekau' huts ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,53</i>)

>  He lived with his maternal grandparents, Pera Te Kaongahau and Hitia Te Rangihaeata in a nikau hut in the bush until he was seven. <i>(Jan 2, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35</i>)

### nikau palm
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A more compelling argument for a moratorium to monitor the effects of nikau palm genes on genus apteryx would be hard to find. <i>(Oct 23, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  In the fifth second we married / Your dress was made of nikau palm. <i>(Jul 6, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),55</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> <i>Rhopalostylis SAPIDA</i> nikau-palm <i>(1906,Introductory Classbook of Botany</i>) [NOTES: And.]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>Rhopalostylis sapida</i> nikau-palm <i>(1906,NZ Flora</i>) [NOTES: And.]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWLL</b> <i>Rhopalostylis sapida (The Nikau Palm). </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,84,1940 p. 84 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Rhopalostylis sapida</i> nikau-palm <i>(1907,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: And. Report on a Botanical Survey of Kapiti Island]

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> ... nekau palm. <i>(1907,Return of Joe,188</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Rhopalostylis SAPIDA nikau-palm </i> <i>(1908,AJHR,C-14</i>) [NOTES: And.Report on a Botanical Survey of Waipoua Kauri Forest]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> <i>Rhopalostylis SAPIDA nikau-palm </i> <i>(1909,NZ Naturalist's Calendar</i>)

>  <i>Rhopalostylis SAPIDA</i> nikau-palm <i>(1912,TrNZI,44</i>) [NOTES: And.]

>  <i>Rhopalostylis sapida</i> nikau-palm <i>(1916,Official Trade Register of Nurserymen</i>) [NOTES: And.]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Rhopalostylis sapida</i> nikau-palm 2nd ed. <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story,2nd ed. ed.</i>) [NOTES: And.]

>  nikau : tree palm. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> ... Jake leaves the creek and wanders among the groves of nikau-palms that line the lower slopes. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,80,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> A land of glaciers bulking towards the bush, nikau palms bursting beside the sea, a land of embushed lakes mirroring the eternal snows and ferns garlanding the road to Lake Kaniere. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,76</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> The drooping leaves of the nikau palms sprinkled a confetti of sunlight on the fern ... above us. <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,30,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: The Woman's Story]

>  <i>nikau</i> or <i>nikau palm</i> n -s [Maori <i>nikau</i>] : a graceful pinnate-leaved New Zealand palm (<i>Kentia sapida</i>) <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1528</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> nikau .. .. nikau .. .. <i>Rhopalostylis sapida </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  A favourite food of the settlers was the pith of the nikau palm which was edible raw as a green vegetable. It was nicknamed `millionaire's salad' because if the pith was taken the palm died. <i>(Jan 4, 1985,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> The the camp had to be near running water ... It wasusually made of the trunks of sapling trees ... and the walls were mde from leaves of the nikau palm. ... I have slept many times in those bush camps, and I was always conscious of being very comfortable indeed. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,151</i>) [NOTES: Joe Gasparich, Northland kauri country]

>  [Advt] The striking metal Nikau palms in Wellington's new Civic Centre represent the finishing touches to four years of intensive work by Athfield Architects Limited. <i>(Dec 7, 1991,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  The Nikau palm (<i>Rhopolostylis sapida</i>) is a well known and well loved native palm, but we don't often think of it as a food source. <i>(Oct 2000,Growing Today,40</i>)

>  And nikau palms, with their ragged pseudo-branches permanently drooping and drying up on their ill-favoured trunks. <i>(Aug 15, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>)

>  To the raw, windswept West Coast, set among the nikau palms and rugged valleys... <i>(Dec 4, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),31</i>)

>  The nikau palms which line the river's banks may have been part of a cargo of plants and animals from the Malayo-Pacific region that began arriving in New Zealand when dinosaurs were still chasing each other up and down what is today Queen Street. There were coconuts here then, and probably crocodiles, but that was before the Big Chill. The crocs and coconuts didn't survive the ice ages but nikau palms came through. <i>(Sep 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 65/76</i>)

>  <b>HAWES</b> It's extremely dangerous making nikau jam from the nikau palm.<i>Ibid.</i> 18 There are long hours at a time when you can't leave the stove, because nikau palm jam is incredibly shifting. <i>Ibid.</i> 39 "...you may not know that the nikau palm is scientifically known as <i>Rhopalostylis sapida</i> and that <i>sapida</i> means saavoury. The spadix of the nikau is edible." <i>(2000,Dream of Nikau Jam, The,18</i>)

>  It [sc. the camp] was usually made of trunks of sapling trees.. and the walls were made from leaves of the nikau palm. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,151</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> Second in choice to eat were the berriesof the lawyer vine; next the heart, the"rolling pin" of the nikau palm. <i>(1988,Point of Departure,7</i>) [NOTES: Quoted from c1900]

>  <b>McCAULEY</b> They'd drive fifty to a hundred miles north or south to play in ageing wooden halls with streamers tacked to the rafters and nikau palm fronds spread across the walls. <i>(1990,Bad Music,32</i>)

>  <i>Rhopalostylis sapida </i> <i>(1869,TrNZI,191</i>) [NOTES: CNZ (Andersen 16 entries)]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> ... there is an undergrowth ... composed of the nikau palm (areca sapida), and the beautifultree-fern, which ... has been aptly styled the king of ferns. <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/245</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The luxuriant beauty of the New Zealand forests almost equals that of the tropics, indeed the presence of the graceful fern-trees, and elegant nikau palms, gives them quite a tropical aspect. <i>(1959,Early Travellers,121</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Johnson Notes from a Journal in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 121]

>  <b>HAAST</b> After half an hour's walk through bush, consisting of kie-kie, fern-trees, and nikau palms, we again took to the beach. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,45</i>)

>  Our huts or sheds kept out the rain well; the nicau-palm affords the readiest and best material, inNew Zealand bush, for roofs; it grows everywhere in bushland ... <i>(1864,Von Tempsky Memoranda,17</i>)

>  <i>Rhopalostylis SAPIDA nikau-palm </i> <i>(1869,TrNZI,1</i>)

>  <b>TINNE</b> ... the roof ... make of the spreading leaves of the `nekau' palm ... <i>Ibid.</i> 61 I could never resist slicing off the head of a `nekau-palm' ..., if it looked particularly tempting; for the white succulent flesh of the young leaves is very like celery in consistency and taste. <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,53</i>)

>  <i>Rhopalostylis SAPIDA nikau-palm </i> <i>(1878,TrNZI,10</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> There is the cabbage-tree palm, with bare shank and top-knot; the nikau palm, with weird and wondrous frondage; the lancewood, ...; the fern-tree, .... There is the twisting, squirming rata; the gaunt ... kahikatea; the golden kowhai; the dark velvet-covered rimu; the feathery red tawai; the perfumy mangiao; ... <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/187</i>)

>  <b>VON MUELLER</b> [?NIKA-PALM] <i>Rhopalostylis SAPIDA nikau-palm </i> <i>(1888,Select Extra-tropical Plants</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>HETLEY</b> <i>Rhopalostylis SAPIDA nikau-palm </i> <i>(1888,Native Flowers of NZ</i>) [NOTES: And.]

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> <i>Rhopalostylis SAPIDA nikau-palm </i> <i>(1890,Descriptive & Priced List</i>) [NOTES: aND.]

>  <b>WALLACE</b> The common NIKAU PALM <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,242</i>)

>  <b>BOYD</b> <i>Rhopalostylis SAPIDA nikau-palm </i> <i>(1900,Our Stolen Summer</i>) [NOTES: And.]

>  <b>WHITE</b> ... but those who cut down large Nikau Palms, which are the slowest growing of all kinds, and use them for the decoration of ballrooms and for other festive occasions, are greatly to blame. <i>(1905,My NZ Garden,51</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Rhopalostylis SAPIDA</i> nikau-palm <i>(1906,Plants of NZ</i>)

### nikau whare
 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> Nikau whares are extremely pretty and picturesque, but are now rarely seen, owing to the cheapness of corrugated iron. Bushmen, however, still make them occasionally for temporaryresidences. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,84,1940 p. 87 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MANDER</b> [The gum-digger] made a hut of sacks and mud and ti-tree, or he built a nikau whare, an art learned from the Maoris, out of the broad-leaved native palm. <i>(1920,Story of a NZ River, The,230,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> And where the nikau whare stood,/ ... Now sheltering stands in calm repose,/ The Saxon cot, encircled with the rose. <i>(1879,Waikato War,28</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> It was not long before the Rongo family instructed the brothers in building a ponga and nikau whare (bush hut) alongside their tent, in which they could cook and eat their meals. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,14</i>)

### nikau-thatched
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>COWAN</b> Most of the people appeared to be gathered in the large nikau-thatched meeting-house. <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,159</i>)

### nikou
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [nikau](new-no.md#nikau))

### nimble policy
 
 <i>n.</i> a flexible and dynamic policy

>  Business New Zealand chief executive Phil O'Reilly said the country needed a "nimble policy" to ensure it kept up with the required skills. <i>(Sep 9, 2005,NZ Herald,A7</i>)

### nimbyist
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Everyone is a nimbyist above a certain level, and Meridian has clearly breached that level... <i>(Jan 21, 2006,Listener (New Zealand Listener),6</i>)

>  In the interim I very much resent the fact that these two nimbyists claim to represent all of Wellington... <i>(Jun 18, 2005,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

### Nina
 
 <i>abbr.</i> No Irish need apply [ORIGIN: acronym]

>  The acronym "Nina" in job advertisements in New Zealand and Australia meant "No Irish need apply." <i>(May 11, 2002,Dominion Weekend Magazine,7</i>)

### nine o'clock flu
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LELAND</b> nine o'clock flu: A disease that strikes workers and schoolchildren only during weekdays. Weekends are miraculously free of this disorder. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,70</i>)

### nine-ouncer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Just in case anybody gets the wrong idea ... [he] does not souvenir aforesaid shortweight glasses without substituting proper nine-ouncers in their place. <i>(1957,NZ Truth</i>) [NOTES: J.R. Sheehan]

### Ninepins
 
 <i>n.</i> the Moeraki Boulders

>  The early Pakeha whalers who worked the Otago coast from the 1830s had their own folklore to explain the boulders. They called them The Ninepins, evoking an image of giants at play with skittles. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/16</i>)

### ninety mile sniper
 
 <i>n.</i> an artillery man [WW2 See quot whack-oh 1945]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> I, at 26, a 90 Mile sniper as they say, was an ex-soldier then... Our 25-pounder artillery field gun in the 2nd N.Z.E.F. got cleaned up by Jerry (German) tanks in Libya, 1941. <i>(1998,From Top to Bottom : Reinga to Bluff,45</i>) [NOTES: Reprinted from <i>Contact</i> June 1945]

### ninety nine
 
 <i>exclam.</i>

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Ninety-nine. A more recent addition to shearers' slang. When a woman enters the usually all-male shed, the words `ninety-nine' are passed down the board, presumably to give the shearers a chance to smarten up their language. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,97</i>)

>  ninety nine: This means there is a woman on the board or at least within hearing distance, so watch your language. It originated from the Maxwell Smart television programme in the 1960s where the female agent was called `ninety nine'. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/26</i>) [NOTES: Wallace Shearing Glossary]

### Ninety-Mile Beach
 
 <i>n.</i> [also Ninety Miles Beach (South Island)]

>  As New Zealanders prepare to hit the road these Christmas holidays, Teresa Maxwell takes a drive down Ninety-Mile Beach which is so long and isolated, there are road signs so you don't get lost... This day, we decide to make use of the Ninety-Mile beach "road" and its the only place I've ever seen an official road sign on a beach. <i>(Dec 18, 2000,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  <b>SELWYN</b> At sunset, from the top of the last hill ... of the [Banks] Peninsula, we obtained a magnificent view ... Below us stretched out the apparently interminable line of the `ninety miles beach', a continuous range of uniform shingle without a headland or bay. <i>(Jan 9, 1844,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Jan 9 Selwyn Journal in 1851 NZ Part III (Church in the Colonies VIII) 9]

>  68. <i>Strepsilas interpres</i>, L. Turnstone. - This cosmopolitan wader was, I believe, first observed between Ellesmere and Ninety-mile beach, Nov. 1870. <i>(Sep 1885,NZ Journal of Science,511</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

### ning
 
 <i>n.</i> [1960 16C F B4 Wanganui Girls C. 1 NING]

### ning-nong
 
 <i>n.</i> a fool, an idiot, a dimwit, =FUCKWIT (cf NONG) [ca1960 Wellington Words must be understandable by sub-editors and other advance species of Ning-Nong while conveying that writer has had college education. ->1984 14 F E120 Wgton Girls C. 1 NING NONG [silly person] -->1984 14 F E134 Wgton Girls C. 29 NING-NONG [term of friendhip]]

>  <b>VIRTUE</b> Sister was always a ning-nong about planning for the future. <i>(1991,Always the Islands of Memory,7</i>)

>  I'm waiting for Dad to laugh and call Mrs Vesty a ning-nong, but he's got a serious face and is concentrating on cutting his steak. <i>(Oct 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

>  I'm waiting for Dad to laugh and call Mrs Vesty a ning-nong, but he's got a serious face and is concentrating on cutting his steak. <i>(Oct 11, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

>  <b>McLEAN</b> and if ning-nong sub-editors don't cut it out of copy, ... <i>(1964,Willie Away,163</i>)

>  <b>WATSON</b> ... the boss was a bit of a ningnong and the customers were beginning to rely on Jerry instead. <i>(1965,Stand in the Rain,54</i>)

>  Featherston County Council ... forces are out to preserve privies and `long drops' for the relief of Ning Nong locals. <i>(Sep 26, 1972,NZ Truth,25/3</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> ningnong idiot, in variation of Northern English dialect `ning-nang';also a worthless person or horse; eg `What a bloody ningnong Percy turned out to be. Just shows some qualifications aren't worth the paper they're printed on.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,76</i>)

>  nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,507</i>)

### ninzby
 
 <i>n.</i> a NZ nimby (from not in New Zealand's back yard) [ORIGIN: acronym]

>  NIMBY? Yes, proudly so, but we also subscribe to NINZBY, not in NZ's backyard either. <i>(Jul 6, 2010,Gisborne Herald,?</i>)

### nip
 
 <i>n.</i> a standard measure of spirits; a small glass of spirits (occas the approx? 3 fluid ounce glass a `nip' is served in) (cf NOBBLER)

>  Ordinary nips were being charged up to eighteenpence in the lounge and bar. <i>(Nov 29, 1943,NZEF Times,III/127 : 8</i>)

>  There would be from 21 to 22 quarter-gill "nips" in the bottle. <i>(May 18, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,16</i>)

>  Poor Haupokia.. will never again receive a "nip" out of the royal bottle. <i>(May 3, 1873,Auckland Weekly News,5</i>)

>  I had started ... with ... a bottle of grog fo [sic] the men on the other side of the river. ... I gave them a nip and then started back. <i>(Jan 29, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,75,1950 ed.</i>)

>  Going into pubs is worse. ... If I did get a skinful many a `nip' I drank and stood. <i>(1869,Punch or the Auckland Charivari,83</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> A man who was taking a `nip' five minutes before in a shanty that I had entered for the rum, ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,122</i>)

>  <b>LADY BARKER</b> The Landlord ... was amazed at the orders for tea and coffee instead of the usual `nips' of spirits. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,103</i>)

>  <b>CAIRD</b> He probably gets a `nip' as a glass of grog is called. <i>(1874,NZ Sheepfarming,25</i>)

>  <b>ELWELL</b> About every two hours he went round with a bottle of rum, giving each hand a small `nip', as it was called. <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,31</i>)

>  <b>BRADSHAW</b> [At the hotel] a halt was made, during which the ladies filled their pockets with `collies', and the gentlemen made an opportunity for a quiet `nip' <i>(1883,New Zealand As It Is,85</i>)

>  <b>D'AVIGDOR</b> Either he takes to `nips' and degenerates into a mere loafer, scrambling ... for odd shillings which he spends in `shouting' (standing drinks to his friends) - a life which ... never lasts long. <i>(1888,Antipodean Notes,222</i>)

>  <b>DAVIDSON</b> His friend Kelly could always do a stiff nip when off duty ... <i>(1889,Stories of NZ Life,3</i>)

>  [Hdg] Price Fixed for Standard Nips <i>(Jul 16, 1945,NZEF Times,IV/212 : 2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> pinch esp. Hard nip, hard pinch a steep part of a (coach or waggon) road

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> Look at mother and dad; pulled the old waggon uphill together, now they can spell-ho. Stands to reason; they took a good grip; hard nips here and there; wheels wanting grease and such-like! <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,305</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to take a (companionable) nip

>  <b>PHILLIPS</b> Giddy and mate called and nipped <i>(Sep 22, 1867,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,116</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to snatch, steal [in ODE, US informal]

>  `Oh an old fellow came in while you were out and nipped your `paper'.' I said: `oh, did he?' `Yes,' said the other fellow, `he came in, looked over all the seats, and then put his peeper on your sheet and poled it.' <i>(Jan 21, 1911,NZ Truth,291/6/5</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

[they had] only their own implements of destruction - spears, nipes, and an instrument made out of beautiful blue or green marble stone, which they call a Mary, none but a chief is allowed to take them to battle, they are very scarce and very dear. <i>(1913,Old Whaling Days,48</i>) [NOTES: 1833 Sheridan Letter from Sydney Monitor in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 48]

 <i>adj.</i> a fast? or slippery turn to extract money from; PUT THE NIPS IN ?

>  <i>nipplewort [s] Lapsana communis</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,53</i>)

### Nippon clipon
 
 <i>n.</i> either of the Auckland Harbour Bridge extensions [often plural]

>  Auckland's Harbour Bridge had two lanes added to each side in the early seventies - "the Nippon Clip-ons" - and the contractors towed huge barges in from.. Japan to use as construction platforms. <i>(Mar 1998,Cuisine,88</i>)

>  <b>armstrong</b> Trouble is.. New Zealand First voters are corried about the big Nippon Clip-on you've attached to the bridge to let large numbers of Asian migrants in. <i>(1997,True Colours,53</i>)

>  The Auckland Harbour Bridge, built too small from the beginning, and forced to live its life forever with the indignity of the "Nippon clip-ons" hanging off its flanks is another [reminder of letting opportunity slip]. <i>(Jan 31, 2001,Capital Times,19</i>)

>  In Japan he will be involved in negotiations with ...... I.H.I., known in this country for its "Nippon clip-on" extension to the Auckland harbour bridge. <i>(Aug 5, 1970,Otago Daily Times,2</i>)

>  When we reach the western "Nippon Clip-on", one of the two massive pre-fab structures literally clipped on to the original structure in 1969 to add four lanes, you can see what he means. The clip-ons move visibly up up and down, up to half a metre at a time. <i>(Oct 29, 2002,Dominion Post,D2</i>)

>  As far as the public was concerned, what became known as the Nippon Clip-ons couldn't be attached soon enough. <i>(Oct 5, 2000,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  Built in 1959 as a four-lane span, its [sc. Auckland Harbour Bridge's capacity was doubled 10 years later when Japanese engineers built two more lanes ('The Nippon Clip-ons') on each side. <i>(1987,Insight Guides (NZ),112</i>)

>  <b>TEMPLE & APSE</b> The centre four lanes [of the Auckland Harbour Bridge] were opened in 1959 but traffic flows trebled in less than a decade forcing the addition of four more outer lanes. These are affectionately known as 'Nippon clip-ons' <i>(1986,New Zealand From the Air,45</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> Nippon-clipon: The AUCKLAND HARBOR BRIDGE was once a two-lane bridge ... Thanks to... Japanese engineering, it is now four lanes. The extra two were built in japan, towed to Auckland and attached to the sides of the existing bridge. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,70</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Nippon clipon the extra lanes on the outside of the Auckland harbour bridge, Japanese-made and attached without piles to existing structure. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,76</i>)

>  The motion left more than a 10cm gap between the "Nippon clip-on" and the main bridge yesterday. <i>(Apr 28, 2004,Dominion Post,p1</i>)

>  <b>POPE & POPE</b> Inevitably its [sc. Auckland Harbour Bridge's] design earns the nickname, 'the coat hanger', while the Japanese-built extensions adding additional lanes on either side are known as the 'Nippon clip-ons'. <i>(1982,Mobil Illust. Guide NZ,45</i>)
