### niu
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) a pole erected for the religious ceremonial purposes of the 19th century Pai Marire cult. [Maori niu, divination] -->esp. in collocation with POLE, MAST [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>IHIMAERA & LONG (ed.)</b> The shadow of the niu which fell upon the land has been taken from the land. <i>(1982,Into the World of Light,32</i>)

>  It was Hauhau who had raised the niu pole Rongo Nui at Maraekowhai. <i>(Oct 1998,Mana,No. 24: 56</i>)

>  Perhaps we could do well to remember another monument, away up the other end of the river, the niu pole, Riri kore ("No more war"), erected by the King movement at the cessation of hostilities. <i>(Dec 3, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  A pole of a certain height, to be called a Niu, should be erected, around which all true believers were to worship, standing in a circle; those found worthy would receive the gift of tongues. <i>(May 16, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)

>  In the flat open space behind the pa there was a pole (or niu) standing used in their new Pai Marire religion. It was about 10 feet high, square for 2 or 3 feet at top & bottom & rounded in the middle. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/155 (A.S.Atkinson) I was curious to know where the first niu, or worshipping post, was set up & when he said at Kaitake during the present war I said I did not see it there. `To you,' he said, `it was a color (flagstaff) to us it was a niu. . <i>Ibid.</i>2/228 (A.S. Atkinson) There is a flagstaff here [Waimate Plains], fenced in and surmounted by the figure of a bird, probably a kawekawea as it was a great niu or Pai Marire pole at one time. <i>(Oct 11, 1864,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/125,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: A.S. Atkinson]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Their [Hauhaus'] worship consisted in uttering some singular compositions containing a strange mixture of heathenism with popery and gibberish, which they chanted whilst dancing round a pole, called their Niu; <i>(1868,Past and Present of NZ,147</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> [The priest] cast the niu rods, or interpreted the `jerkings' (takiri) of the limbs or body of asleeping man ... <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,336</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> This niu was the central symbol of worship under Te Ua's dispensation. The term was the olden Maori word for the short sticks used by the tohunga in his mystic arts of divination, particularly before a battle. Te Ua's niu was a tall pole or flag-mast, round which the faithful were to march in procession chanting their hymns. <i>Ibid..</i> 157 A tall niu, the pole of worship, stood in the principal part of the settlement; ... There were similar niu masts at other villages ... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/6</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> At the principal villages in that rugged region ... the Hauhau poles, the sacred niu, were set up and round them marched the half-crazed people ... chanting their newly learned hymns ... the prayers of the `Kororia,' Maorified English phrases and scraps of English and Roman Catholic church services. <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,85</i>)

>  <b>BABBAGE</b> The central object of worship [of the new religion of Tella Haumene] was the `Niu.' The origin of this innovation was described in the following terms:- .in 2;`The Angel Gabriel said: `Go back to your house and erect a niu.' Horopapera enquired what a niu was. The Angel replied: `A post.' Horopapera enquired for what pur- pose? The Angel replied: `To work for you for the acquirement of the languages of all the races upon the earth.'' The term `niu' was the old Maori term for the short sticks used by the tohunga priest in his mystic arts of divination, particularly before a battle. <i>(1937,Hauhauism,29</i>)

>  niu, divination by means of small rods. Niu is a Polynesian name of the coconut, and in the Pacific the nut was spun in a divination rite: the Maori lost the name for the tree when he came to Newzealand, but retained it for the divinatory rite. The Hauhau fanatics gave the name niu to the pole they set up for their ceremonial dancing procession i[n] what was a horrid kind of maypole dance. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/154</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> The services of the new Maori evangelism were held at a niu, a long pole, perhaps fifty feet high, with yard-arms from which hung ropes. The congregation revolved roundthis mast as round a maypole while the priests conducted a service of prayer. . <i>Ibid.</i> 302 [Maori glossary] niu Small sticks used in divining;a pole <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,137</i>)

>  <b>BRATHWAITE</b> NIU Mast erected for purposes of religous rites and observances in Pai-marire faith. <i>(1967,Evil Day,380</i>) [NOTES: Gloss.]

>  But that is Pakeha history. More significant are the Nui poles, erected in the early 1860s and well-known to canoeists who traverse the river today. The remaining two poles, at Marae-kowhai, are distinguished by their cross-arms reaching to the compass points, oneof which (Rongo Niu) has carved hands on it. From this flew the war pennants of the Pai Marire or Hauhau, around which the warriors danced prior to battle. <i>(Mar 31, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> A few [colonial] expressions were not familiar to me. When we should say in England, `Certainly not', it is here `No fear', or `Don't you believe it'. When they want to answer in the affirmative, they say, `It is so', `It does so'. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,ch.iii 31</i>) [NOTES: 1860 Jan 27 in 1863 Butler First Year]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> A few [colonial] expressions were not familiar to me. When we should say in England, `Certainly not', it is here `No fear', or `Don't you believe it'. When they want to answer in the affirmative, they say, `It is so', `It does so'. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,ch.iii 31</i>) [NOTES: 1860 Jan 27 in 1863 Butler First Year]

 <i>n.</i> New Zealand (?play title) [ORIGIN: Samoan]

### Niu Tireni
 
 <i>n.</i> New Zealand [ORIGIN: Maori, also below Tirini, Tirani; transliteration]

>  A further (obsolescent) Maori name is <i>Niu Tireni</i>, the Maori transliteration of <i>New Zealand</i> (with many variants: <i>Niu Tirani, Niu Tirini, Nuitereni, Nui Tireni, Nu Tirani</i>, etc...Both <i>Aotearoa</i> and <i>Niu Tireni</i>.. are listed among the Maori place names approved for addressing purposes by New Zealand Post. <i>(Oct 2000,NZWords,4 : 2</i>)

>  <b>TUWHARE</b> Niu Tireni (Long White Shroud) / (a bare-arsed land, manipulated by the wrong white crowd of minority speculators - with big foreign investors - busily weaving a long white shroud to fit us all). <i>(1997,Shape-Shifter,88</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> Of New Zealand, for example, they make Niutireni. <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),216</i>)

>  Niu Tirini is in a mist for the loss of his medicine. <i>(Dec 19, 1860,Taranaki Punch,8</i>) [NOTES: Check spelling of Tirini]

>  I am proud of that ship [sc. Maheno]. Wegirls felt we could jump over the moon.Well done Nui [sic] Tireni! <i>(Dec 2, 1915,Hawke's Bay Herald,6</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... Potatau the First ... in June 1858 ... entered [Rangiawhia] preceded by his flag, bearing the device of a cross and three stars, with the name of the country, `Niu Tirini,' in the centre, ... <i>(1868,Past and Present of NZ,123</i>)

>  New-Zealanders have long spoken of `Peowhairangi,' Bay of Islands; `Akarana,' Auckland; `Niu Tirani,' New Zealnd, &c. <i> Ibid.</i> 25/411 In some cases our English names of places have been adopted by the Maori, with such alteration as is necessary to make them easily pronounced by Maori organs of speech. New Zealanders have long spoken of `Peowhairangi,' Bay of Islands; `Akarara,' Auckland; `Niu Tirani', New Zealand ... <i>(1893,TrNZI,25/402</i>) [NOTES: (Smith)]

>  I observed that the term `New Zealander' is already employed in this district; only, in lieu of `Noui-Tireni", as the natives of the Bay of Islands pronounce it, they say `Noui-Tirangui' [Nui Tirangi] [sic], which gives the word more of an indigenous sound. The word pakeha serves them to signify all whites, whom they also call `Iouropi' (Europeans). I did not observe that they had a special name for the English. <i>(1909,TrNZI,41/139</i>) [NOTES: Smith transl. D'Urville's Visit to Tolaga Bay in 1827]

>  <b>CURRIE</b> Whether in Niu Tireni or Te Reinga/ Now you live, or in countries over the ocean - <i>(1949,Centennial Treasury of Otago Verse,110</i>) [NOTES: (`Epilogue')]

>  I very much object to Sir James Henare's suggestion that this country be known as Nu Tirini. That is merely the corruption of the English name to which so many Maoris object. <i>(Aug 13, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>) [NOTES: M.A.C. Blanshard]

### No 8/Number 8 (wire/fencing wire)
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) standard Number eight gauge fencing wire

>  <b>MARRIOTT</b> The sag on 65 metres of no 8 carrying the weight of a man is considerable, and for three-quarters of the way it was downhill. <i>(1981,Life in the Gorge,30</i>)

>  <b>Owen Marshall</b> A strand of number eight under tension had whipped back from the strainer and cut the bridge of his nose and the soft skin beneath his left eye. <i>(2007,Drybread,199</i>)

>  Just arrived, ex 'Coronilla', 20 tons BB annealed fencing wire, No 8. 1884 North NZ Settler Jan 17 I look upon a wire fence as worse than useless, particularly one of No 8 black wire, let there be as many wires as you like. <i>(Sep 4, 1872,Hawke's Bay Herald,1</i>)

>  Ordinary No. 8 fencing-wire is stretched along the top of each row of poles each way ... <i>(Feb 20, 1937,NZ Journal of Agriculture,108</i>)

>  A North Taranaki farmer, A A Jonas, Motunui, uses two strands of No 8 wire twisted together. <i>(Oct 11, 1951,NZ Farmer,19</i>)

>  So now all gates and fastenings are festooned with snaggles of bent No 8. <i>(Aug 10, 1972,NZ Farmer,39</i>)

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> Along came also the hunter chains concept of fencing and the introduction of high tensile 125 gauge wire which was slowly to replace man's best friend, the number eight wire. <i>(1994,I Was So Lucky,186</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [further cits.]

>  We have, if you like, extended the myth of what the New Zealand character is. The man alone battling against nature, facing banks, and the expertise with a piece of No 8 wire. <i>(Apr 4, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  Four wires at the bottom of No.8, and two wires at the top of No. 6, will require 1 ton 7 cwt. per mile. <i>(1883,Brett's Colonists Guide,77</i>) [NOTES: Pagination from 1980 edition.]

>  The thing is, New Zealand has plenty of culture. And not just Culture with a capital 'C' like opera and ballet - we've got the good old, down home, No 8 fencing wire culture that big-spending visitors would ford the Fox River in flood on a farm bike to experience. <i>(Aug 31, 2003,Sunday Star Times,A3</i>)

>  The first military telegraph signalling in New Zealand used No 8 wire, strung into place behind British troops as they advanced into the Waikato during the land wars of the 1860s. <i>(Oct 19, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>)

>  Nothing is worse than the military myth, the belief that - give us a piece of number eight wire and, if the need is there, we can go overseas and be good. <i>(Apr 10, 2004,Dominion Post,E4</i>)

>  Earlier this year, we had sampled the more No 8-wire alpine experience of past memory up Mt Taranaki. <i>(Aug 14, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)

>  [Hdg] What's the Spanish for No 8 wire? <i>(Apr 18, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C8</i>)

>  Bell's got a distinctive DYI number eight fencing wire approach to theatre publicity. <i>(Apr 23, 1997,Capital Times,7</i>)

>  If one could make records out of No 8 fencing wire, this is what they would sound like. <i>(May 24, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

>  Undaunted, New Zealand addicts adopted the No 8 wire approach and invented their own - homebake - converting pharmaceuticals, mainly headache pills like codeine and Panadene, for their opiate fix. <i>(May 5, 2001,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  I believe we are losing our rural roots and with it our Kiwi No 8 wire mentality, can-do attitude and spirit of free enterprise. ... List MP Kate Wilkinson also lamented the loss of the no 8 wire attitude. <i>(Nov 18, 2005,Dominion Post,A11</i>)

>  Don't know about you, but those No. 8 wire Kiwi stories just make me shiver with pride. <i>(Nov 14, 2005,NZ Herald,B4</i>)

>  "We are losing our rural roots and our No. 8 wire Kiwi mentality, can-do attitude and spirit of free enterprise." <i>(Nov 28, 2005,Dominion Post,B7</i>) [NOTES: Nathan Guy, National MP maiden speech]

>  But we're not talking No 8 fencing wire here. <i>(May 29, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>)

With little money and makeshift resources he broke open the secret of the atomic nucleus. <i>(Mar 11, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)

>  Along the way he gets beaten to a pulp by 10 guitars and generally takes the Kiwi, No 8 wire, "Let's effing do it!" spirit to absurd extremes in the home of trash television. <i>(Jan 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),78</i>)

>  Do we sleep better at nights because a piece of New Zealand number eight fencing wire might one day hurtle through space and miss its target by a million miles? <i>(Jan 18, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)

>  As well as being the acme of the No 8 wire tradition, Pearse was also the acme of the self-deprecating achiever - and you can't help thinking that the last thing on his mind was public recognition. <i>(Mar 29, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)

>  I think we are of an international standard, but we do it on No 8. wire. <i>(Jul 18, 2001,Dominion,29</i>)

>  In its turn, the LTSA last week released a brochure to try to educate young drivers about vehicle modification... "It's a bit of a battle, especially here in New Zealand... It's the No 8 wire mentality." <i>(Apr 28, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),31</i>)

>  Now he is teaming up with Wellington venture capitalists Jenny Morel and Peter Allport at No 8 Ventures Management... No 8 Ventures turned down vastly more investment proposals than it invested in. <i>(May 29, 2002,Dominion,23</i>)

>  Peter Jackson's incredible achievement is in a category all of its own in many ways: self-effacing Wellington boy sticks two peeves of number eight fencing wire together and makes movie monument., That the series keeps reaching new heights is nothing short of gobsmacking. <i>(Dec 22, 2002,Sunday Star Times,D2</i>)

>  [Cartoon caption] We regret Creative New Zealand will no longer be giving out money... We do, however, have a strictly limited allocation of Number Eight wire. <i>(May 23, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

>  Then the reactor heats up and rather than abandon ship or accept help from the Americans Harrison chooses the proud Soviet option of fixing the problem with number eight wire. <i>(Nov 10, 2002,Sunday Star Times,F4</i>)

>  So baches are examples of what it is to be Kiwis - bugger the council, we can do anything with No 8 wire. <i>(Jan 19, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

>  Number eight (4mm thick) wire formed kitchen implements, latched a million dunny doors, and dried the nation's laundry. <i>(Aug 28, 1992,Dominion,23</i>)

>  While they could, no doubt, benefit from having a quick peruse of the Madden working drawings, I feel sure that in the twinkling of a Southland eye, a more robust design would emerge as the number eight wire mountain men of Central Otago jam their thinking caps on. <i>(Dec 3, 2001,Dominion,13</i>)

>  <b>MCGILL</b> Number Eight. The best, strongest, most likely to succeed; from the thick gauge No 8 fencing wire used on farms ... <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,77</i>)

>  We can't see that archival footage of a farmer playing his No 8 fencing wire too often. <i>(Dec 25, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),70</i>)

>  In the tradition of number eight wire down on the farm, New Zealanders are booking marquees, generators and barbies for hire in unprecedented numbers to avoid the possibility of singing "Auld Lang Syne" in the dark. <i>(Dec 25, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

>  A key phrase that's been going backwards and forwards is: "Don't know. Can do." It captures one of our biggest assets, our "number eight wire" mentality. <i>(Oct 30, 2000,Dominion Infotech Weekly,No. 465 : 4</i>)

>  We have made our one lapse into folly work, in a number-eight fencing-wire sort of way. <i>(Aug 5, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  The Kiwi No 8 fencing wire attitude is everywhere, even down to raising preschool children. <i>(Mar 28, 2002,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  Thieves raided his highly publicised bra fence three times in 10 days during February, leaving him virtually bra-less. "I even had a huge one ... I reckon it was a No 8 wire ... but it got stolen," Mr Lee said. <i>(Apr 16, 2002,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> There was just one telephone line that ran away down to the end of habitation - a good stout 8-gauge fencing wire, often stapled from tree-trunk to tree-trunk beside the pack-track. <i>(1967,Return to Open Country,20</i>)

>  New Zealanders live off their reputation as super-competent all-rounders who can.. play Mozart on tea-chest with a length of No. 8 fencing wire. <i>(1991,Cuisine,101</i>)

>  I can relate the occasion when I watched my mother teaching a group of Maori women how to knit, using Number 8 wire fashioned into knitting needles. <i>(Oct 2003,NZ Memories,No. 44/32</i>)

>  We really do believe that the world is a simple place and that there's no problem than can't be solved with a bit of of Kiwi ingenuity. Or the Weta special effects team - our post-modern equivalent of No 8 wire. <i>(Dec 7, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a Number 8 gauge (4mm) fencing wire, esp. when used inventively and practically for other than fencing purposes; a smooth wire, 8 gauge; a standard wire, of many and almost legendary uses around the farm

>  No! - let me tell you about No 8 fencing wire... You might notice its ingenious usage, from makeshift umbrella spokes to a water diving rod (one man I knew used it in his car to strap the engine block onto the mount) or again as a staple of the eternal temporary-fix we are so fond of - tying a flying fox round a tree, containing old newspapers on a shelf, even in the backblocks holding tight a wired-up broken jaw. <i>(Jan 14, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  <b>SHERWOOD</b> `They were buying a roll of number eight ...' `Fencing wire,' Johnson interjected. <i>(1985,Botanist at Bay,131</i>)

>  number eight NZ wire of 4mm. gauge used esp. for fencing. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,514</i>) [NOTES: nf 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 332]

>  [Ironmongery advt includes] Fencing Wire, Nos. 6, 7, 8, 9, and 10; Fencing Staples and WireStretchers. <i>(Jul 8, 1876,Saturday Advertiser,10</i>)

>  ... a good paling fence on ordinary bushlands with double No. 8 wires at top and bottom ... can be erected at about 12s. per chain. <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,514</i>)

>  (2.) Batten and wire fence, at least 3 ft. 9 in. high, of substantial material, firmly erected, with one or more wires, not lighter than No. 8 in steel, or black or galvanised iron; battens either driven securely into the ground and securely stapled, or intertwined in wires in an upright position, and not more than 3 in. apart. <i>Ibid.</i> 19 ... and a good paling fence on ordinary bush land with double No. 8 wires top and bottom, with 1/2 in. palings, and 7 in. posts sunk 2 ft. in the ground, can be erected at about 12s. per chain. <i>(1895,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,161</i>) [NOTES: 1895 Fencing Act in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 161]

>  The palings are interwoven by a double No. 8 wire at the top and a double No. 8 wire at the bottom. <i>(Jul 15, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,10</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> The variety so well known as No. 8 has become the unwritten standard for New Zealand fences. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,131</i>)

>  Turner (p. 135), gives a quotation from Crump on wire. I think `A piece of No 8' is well-enough established in farming jargon to deserve a place as No 8, signifying 8-gauge fencing wire. <i>(Jul 29, 1966,Letter</i>) [NOTES: Letter I. Davey (Whangarei)]

>  <b>WILSON</b> Then eventually we made barbs for our harpoons, by cutting a length of Number Eight wire from some farm fence at the strainer post, ... <i>(1973,NZ Jack,27</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> A very long toasting fork made of twisted No. 8 fencing wire, last night's unwashed coffee cups, . <i>(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,102</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Each bale had half a kerosine tin with handle made of number eight wire. <i>(1978,Elver, The,37</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> number 8 fencing wire: The traditional 5 wire fence on New Zealand farms is made with this stuff. In consequence it is one of the things that is always available round a farm when you need to build or repair something. It is said that some cockies can put together a landrover, a tractorand half a milk shed with nothing but a roll of number 8. Wire comes in sizes ranging from 1 to approx. 64, with 1 being the thickest. Number 8 is 5/32 of an inch or about half the diameter of a pencil. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A</i>)

>  <b>GLEN</b> This time I took a piece of No.8 wire and carried the trap to the creek. <i>(1980,Bush in Our Yard,68</i>)

>  Wire ... No. 8 wire: Smooth wire, 8 gauge (4mm). <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,30</i>)

>  <b>CHRISTIE</b> Christoffer looked at what he supposed was a gate. It was made of barbed wire and battens strung between two strainer posts to which it was tied with No 8 fencing wire. Being the first Taranaki gate he had encountered, he studied the primitive contraption with a puzzled frown, then realised it was simplicity itself. `Just lift off this wire loop.' The gate sprang from his fingers, coiled into the air and collapsed in a tangled heap. Christoffer cursed the man who had invented it. However he had to admit that it was an efficient, cheap and easily-made thing - a sort of `instant gate'. <i>Ibid.</i> 150 The swing bridge, made of No 8 fencing wire, made a good support for the pipelines. <i>(1987,Candles and Canvas,86</i>)

>  <b>MILLER97</b> No. 8 wire was to him as string is to the average man. He could tie it ..., and with a strainer he could tighten a wire until it literally sang when it was flicked. <i>(1946,There Was Gold</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> The only tools we used to build the camp were an axe, spade, slasher, a short length of No. 8 wire, ... <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,56</i>)

>  No 8 wire has always been the great standby of rural handypersons. <i>(Jul 23, 1988,Dominion,8</i>)

>  New Zealanders are proud of their `do it yourself' flair, whipping up anything to order with a few lengths of four-by-two and some No. 8 fencing wire. <i>(Feb 14, 1989,Press, The,6</i>)

>  One by one [the vehicles] ground to a halt, despite some amazing feats of No-8-wire-mechanical engineering and cannibalism. <i>(Sep 12, 2001,Capital Times,2</i>)

>  ...nimble-fingered Hawke's Bay ladies.. started tossing scone dough round tins with 5 cent piece holes, and dousing the dough with local boy-made-good James Wattie's canned spaghetti in tomato sauce that it hit me... Now we're an enlightened species, we all know it was a No 8 fencing wire attempt that spawned parlours and pizzerias more resembling the real thing. <i>(Feb 22, 2003,Dominion Post,F8</i>)

>  He is an eloquent chap, wild-haired and more than a little wild-haired and more than a little wild-eyed as he reels off the traumatic list of disasters and desperate remedies that made up the "No-8-wire treasure hunt". <i>(Jan 31, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)

>  Innovation exponents have criticised New Zealand's "No 8 wire" philosophy as outdated and question its relevance to the country's constantly evolving national identity... Historian Jock Phillips said No 8 wire was an ingrained part of the national identity, but did not exclusively belong to New Zealand. "I think there's been a very powerful mythology about it, which you will find also in other countries ... Australia, the United Kingdom, and the United States," he said. <i>(Jun 28, 2004,Dominion Post,C3</i>)

### No 8/number 8 (wire/fencing wire)
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [fig.] a symbol for Kiwi ingenuity.

>  No 8 wire can't fix it: New Zealand needs to tap into the international corporate culture. The No 8 mentality is about making do, about having no ambition to be or have the best. <i>(Mar 3, 2002,Sunday Star Times,E2</i>)

>  Number Eight the best, strongest, most likely to succeed; from the thick gauge No. 8 fencing wire used on farms; eg Joy Shepard remembers a doctor saying to a patient on whom he had done surgery that had come a bit unstuck: `I'll use No.8 this time and make a real job of it.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,77</i>)

>  With a No 8 fencing wire approach, he has turned the logistical labyrinth into a workable reality. <i>(Jan 30, 2006,Dominion Post,A11</i>)

>  Whack 'em on the backs of the trucks with No 8 wire and drive as fast as we can? <i>(Mar 3, 2001,Dominion,22</i>)

>  This, together with the sheer competence of the New Zealand farmer, is what has made the varied uses of number eight wire - the metaphor for many New Zealanders' basic approach to life," said Dame Silvia. <i>(Jun 14, 2001,Waikato Times,4</i>)

>  'Beyond No 8 wire' is the theme of Massey University Richmond Southern North Island Sheep and Beef cattle farmers conference ... <i>(May 2, 2002,Internet - see Notes,News</i>) [NOTES: www.agriculture.massey.ac.nz]

### No 8/Number eight (wire)
 
 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of people or ventures] ingenious.

>  <b>DENSEM</b> From these layers outwards "Men," in the number-eight-wire sense, dominated. <i>(1995,Letter,90</i>) [NOTES: in Dominy (2001) Calling the Station Home]

>  Despite this failure Richard Pearse was a genuine Kiwi original - a No 8-wire man of the first rank. <i>(Nov 1994,Quote Unquote,30</i>)

>  <b>DOMINY</b> As the back country that "number eight Men" populated gets retired and covenanted for conservation purposes ... <i>(2001,Calling the Station Home,91</i>)

>  If you have a production story, a community event or comments for the programme, the No 8 team would love to know. <i>(May 22, 2002,RD1.com,On the Farm 39</i>)

>  Burt Munro was the ultimate eccentric - a real 'number eight' wire inventor. <i>(May 2007,Tearaway,22</i>)

### no beg-pardons
 
 <i>phr.</i> (See [beg-pardon](b-bla.md#beg-pardon))

### no biggie
 
 <i>phr.</i> no big deal

>  "I'm not really too fussed about it," he shrugged. "If it happens it happens. But it's no biggie." <i>(Jul 23, 2000,Sunday Star Times,B1</i>)

>  I don't think I've got to churn out a play every year too. If it happens for me too it's great but if it doesn't that's no biggie. <i>(Mar 3, 2002,Sunday Star Times,F7</i>)

### no fear
 
 <i>exclam.</i> I don't believe you; not under any circumstances; no way, an exclamation intensifying a negative (`certainly not') answer to a question or request is very common in NZ, and often thought to be local idiom.

>  <b>BUTLER</b> A few [colonial] expressions were not familiar to me. When we should say in England, `Certainly not', it is here `No fear', or `Don't you believe it'. When they want to answer in the affirmative, they say, `It is so', `It does so'. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement</i>) [NOTES: 1860 Jan 27 in 1863 Butler First Year]

>  <b>MUTER</b> A `mob of cattle', to use the colonial term, after a few expressions of `My word' and `No fear' would be sold at some 10 pounds per head. <i>(1864,Travels in NZ,2/273</i>)

>  <b>BURGESS</b> He replied: `Did you think I was going to let a bloody wretch like that best me? No fear, the - shall sweat there till Monday ...' <i>(1866,Confessions,93,1983 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COOPER</b> One energetically declared that the new ground to the south was the best, while another ... vociferated, `No fear! no fear!'. <i>(Dec 14, 1867,Digger's Diary,22,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> `Why, Janson, I thought she was going to turn turtle.' `No fear', he replied ... <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,75</i>)

>  As expletives `My word' and `No fear' are favourites, both drawn out as long as possible. <i>(1885,TrNZI,18/54</i>) [NOTES: J.C.Crawford]

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> `Are you not going to make a pile at the diggings ... ' `No fear,' laughingly replied Leslie; ... <i>(1881,Waitaruna,145</i>)

>  `Can you produce them?' `No fear'. <i>(1895,Diary of Jonathan Roberts,5</i>)

>  It is the result of conviction or the dictates of conscience? No fear. Nothing of the kind. <i>(Jun 24, 1905,NZ Truth,1-2</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> The settlers, he learnt, said `no fear' instead of `certainly not'. [He = Samuel Butler]. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,101</i>)

>  <b>MUIR</b> `She didn't commit no bloody suicide, mate. No bloody fear. The flatties thought of that one. Mind you, she was in pod, you know.' <i>(1960,Word for Word,254</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> ... I'd like to see the bloody Hun who's going to make me start now. No fear, me old cobber General Freyberg, VC,'d never forgive me. <i>(1975,Breathing Spaces,97</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> And there was no hot water laid on, no fear. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,20</i>)

>  <b>LADY CAMPBELL</b> [Tobin warns the speaker that it is T's father he is to tell bad news of] `No fear; I am not likely to forget.' <i>(1864,Martin Tobin,2/68</i>)

### no good to gundy
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  This [interference] was no good to Gundy, so cobber the wood-chopper got busy swinging his axe ... <i>(Sep 2, 1918,Quick March,1</i>)

>  (phrase) elaboration of the simple phrase `no good to me' [orig. doubtful, possibly American. [cf NZ nickname]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),616</i>)

>  gundy, no good to (Phr.) elaboration of phrase not good enough for me [Scot. coll. gundie greedy]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)

### No Maoris, No Tour
 
 <i>phr.</i> slogan of the campaign to prevent the All Blacks touring South Africa without Maori representation

>  The subsequent success of the No Maoris - No Tour movement affirmed the belief that New Zealand rugby would be played the New Zealand way and not according to the repugnant rules applying in South Africa. <i>(Jun 28, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5</i>)

>  The radical <i>Notes from the Second Year</i>.. focused me very firmly in the muddled turmoil of No Maori No Tour, hippiedom and the Vietnam war. <i>(Sep 17, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),108</i>)

### no milk in one's coconut
 
 <i>phr.</i> no brains in one's head

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> no milk in her coconut no brains in her head <i>(1984,Blackball 08,244</i>)

### no sense sticking your nose in butter...
 
 <i>phr.</i> in full: no sense sticking your nose in butter if you're going to have to eat dripping the rest of your life

>  <b>McGILL</b> no sense sticking your nose in butter if you're going to have to eat dripping the rest of your life a discouragement to put on airs if you cannot afford them, usually parental negativity towards offspring leaving the nest in Depression era, as quoted in Stevan Eldred-Grigg's Oracles and Miracles. (Longest c/p known to compiler.) <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,76</i>)

### no show/not a show
 
 <i>phr.</i> no chance (also as exclamation - see notes) [As exclamation meaning something like Not Likely, or No Chance, or Over my Dead Body, as in: "Are you going to the Smith's Cocktail Party?" "No show!" This is quite different from no-show as used in restaurants - "They're a no-show" meaning they didn't turn up to claim their table reservation.]

>  <b>BARLOW</b> Engineers, to use a colonialism, have not a `show' at all [of making money] - particularly now that the borrowing policy has been partially given up. <i>(1889,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,205</i>)

>  <b>ASHTON-WARNER</b> `She's dying ... [sic] or should be; there's not a show.' `What does that mean ... [sic] `there's not a show'?' `Oh ... [sic]', he laughs. `It means `She hasn't got a chance'. New Zealandese.' <i>(1960,Incense to Idols,162</i>)

### no worries
 
 <i>phr.</i> not a problem (US), she'll be right; cf. no sweat [ca1961 Wellington No worries. I'll get a dozen from home.]

>  <b>McGill</b> 'Forget the damned dog,' Jack growled. 'Ring that priest, will you? See if he's coming.' 'No worries,' Ron said, wondering what was eating Jack. <i>(1995,Gold in the Creek,29</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> no worries expression of confidence; eg `Even without Martin Crowe we're a match for the Aussies, no worries.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,76</i>)

### no wukkers
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  I offer him a few of my own ideas [of Kiwi words], and he reaches for a pen and paper. House of Pain? "It's very regional." No wukkers? "Spell it." Sweet as? "Well ... I don't know...it doesn't strike me as particularly New Zealand." <i>(Jun 29, 2002,Dominion,11</i>)

### no-boner
 
 <i>n.</i> [? mistake for no-brainer]

>  "It's a no boner," he said. <i>(Jun 16, 2003,Dominion Post,D1</i>)

### no-build
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Developers Buckle Barracks plan to protect the airspace above the McDonald's building with a no-build condition. <i>(Jul 21, 2005,Wellingtonian,3</i>)

### no-capacity man
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MAY</b> ... councillors advocated that the names of the `no-capacity' men (that is, those unwilling to assist the war effort in any capacity whatever) be made public. <i>(1975,Miners and Militants,134</i>) [NOTES: Griffiths Collection]

### no-exit
 
 <i>adj.</i> [Jim Cameron writes: This is used attributively as well as being a road sign]

>  Conservation Minister Sandra Lee has asked.. park managers to spend $260,000 upgrading a no-exit road. <i>(Aug 22, 2000,Dominion,1</i>)

>  The californian bungalow is nestled among mature trees at the end of a quiet no-exit street. <i>(Aug 22, 2000,Wairarapa Times-Age,Suppl. : 00</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> The mansion sprawled across the end of this no-exit street. <i>(1998,Mates,32</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> There are a few no-exit offshoots near the town but once the ten miles of tarseal give on to the rough, metalled surface, one is bereft even of the comforting sighting of a friendly crossroads and a few direction signs. <i>(1971,Plekhov Place, The,9</i>)

### no-eyes
 
 <i>n.</i> a person wearing spectacles (cf FOUR EYES) [early ? nonce]

>  <b>POWER</b> [Maoris' nicknames for the pakehas in Power's party] A stalwart officer ... is generally known as Puku-nui, or Big-belly; an artillery officer is Biggy-bung; one wearing spectacles is Four-eyes, or No-eyes. <i>(Jan 30, 1848,Sketches in NZ,159,1849 ed.</i>)
